arXiv:1805.08760v5 [math-ph] 11 Jan 2019 
 
 
 Moments of random matrices and
 hypergeometric orthogonal polynomials 
 
 
 Fabio Deelan Cunden
 
 F. D. Cunden 
 School of Mathematics and Statistics, University College Dublin, Belfield, Dublin 4, Ireland
 
 ,  
 Francesco Mezzadri
 
 F. Mezzadri 
 School of Mathematics, University of Bristol, University Walk, Bristol BS8 1TW, United Kingdom
 
 ,  
 Neil O’Connell
 
 N. O’Connell 
 School of Mathematics and Statistics, University College Dublin, Belfield, Dublin 4, Ireland
 
  and  
 Nick Simm
 
 N. Simm 
 Mathematics Department, University of Sussex, Brighton, BN1 9RH, United Kingdom
 
 Date: August 24, 2026 

 Abstract. 
    
 We establish a new connection between moments of  n × n n\times n  random matrices  X n X_{n}  and hypergeometric orthogonal polynomials. Specifically, we consider moments  𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n − s \mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-s}  as a function of the complex variable  s ∈ ℂ s\in\mathbb{C} , whose analytic structure we describe completely. We discover several remarkable features, including a reflection symmetry (or functional equation), zeros on a critical line in the complex plane, and orthogonality relations. An application of the theory resolves part of an integrality conjecture of Cunden  et al.  [F. D. Cunden, F. Mezzadri, N. J. Simm and P. Vivo, J. Math. Phys. 57 (2016)] on the time-delay matrix of chaotic cavities. In each of the classical ensembles of random matrix theory (Gaussian, Laguerre, Jacobi) we characterise the moments in terms of the Askey scheme of hypergeometric orthogonal polynomials. We also calculate the leading order  n → ∞ n\to\infty  asymptotics of the moments and discuss their symmetries and zeroes. We discuss aspects of these phenomena beyond the random matrix setting, including the Mellin transform of products and Wronskians of pairs of classical orthogonal polynomials. When the random matrix model has orthogonal or symplectic symmetry, we obtain a new duality formula relating their moments to hypergeometric orthogonal polynomials.

 1.  Introduction 

 
 In this paper we present a novel approach to the moments of the classical ensembles of random matrices. Much of random matrix theory is devoted to moments  𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k \mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k}  ( k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} ) of random matrices of finite or asymptotically large size  n n . The Gaussian, Laguerre and Jacobi unitary ensembles have been extensively studied and virtually everything is known about the moments as functions of the matrix size  n n . In particular, for the GUE,  𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k \mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k}  is a polynomial in  n n . This fact is a consequence of Wick’s theorem, it is usually called ‘genus expansion’, and it is at the heart of several successful theories such as the topological recursion  [ 6 ,  33 ] . For example, the  4 4 -th moment of GUE matrices of size  n n  is 
 

 
 
 1 n ​ 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n 8 = 14 ​ n 4 + 70 ​ n 2 + 21 . \frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{8}=14n^{4}+70n^{2}+21. 
 
 
 
 
 In contrast to the wealth of results on moments as functions of the size  n n , less attention has been devoted to them as functions of the order  k k .
One of the consequences is that some remarkable properties have been somehow missed.
The theory described in this paper is intended to fill this gap.
By looking at the moments as functions of  k k , we gain access to additional structure.
Several results contained in this paper are in fact facets of the same phenomenon, which appears to be a new observation:  moments  𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k \mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k}  of classical matrix ensembles, if properly normalized, are hypergeometric orthogonal polynomials as functions of  k k .  For example, for a GUE random matrix of size  n = 4 n=4 
 

 
 
 1 ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X 4 2 ​ k = 4 3 ​ k 3 + 4 ​ k 2 + 20 3 ​ k + 4 , \frac{1}{(2k-1)!!}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{4}^{2k}=\frac{4}{3}k^{3}+4k^{2}+\frac{20}{3}k+4, 
 
 
 and this polynomial is actually a Meixner polynomial. In fact, the moments are essentially Meixner polynomials as functions of  ( n − 1 ) (n-1) , too. 
 
 
 During our investigation it became natural to consider  complex  moments  𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k \mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k}  ( k ∈ ℂ k\in\mathbb{C} ) or, equivalently, averages of spectral zeta functions of random matrices. 
 
 
 1.1.  Spectral zeta functions of random matrices 

 
 There exist various generalizations of the Riemann  ζ \zeta -function, associated with operator spectra and which are generically called  spectral zeta functions . 
 
 
 Consider a compact operator  A A  on a separable Hilbert space. Then  A ​ A ∗ AA^{*}  is a nonnegative operator, so that  | A | = A ​ A ∗ |A|=AA^{*}  makes sense. The singular values of  A A  are defined as the (nonzero) eigenvalues of  | A | |A| . If  A A  is self-adjoint with discrete spectrum  λ 1 , λ 2 , … \lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots , the singular values are  | λ 1 | , | λ 2 | , … |\lambda_{1}|,|\lambda_{2}|,\dots . The Dirichlet series representation of the Riemann zeta function  ζ ⁡ ( s ) \zeta(s)  suggests to define the spectral zeta function  ζ A ​ ( s ) \zeta_{A}(s)  of the operator  A A  as the maximal analytic continuation of the series 
 

 
 
 ∑ j ≥ 1 | λ j | − s \sum_{j\geq 1}|\lambda_{j}|^{-s} 
 
 
 (this is also called Minakshisundaram–Pleijel  [ 63 ]  zeta function of  A A .)
In this sense, the Riemann  ζ ⁡ ( s ) \zeta(s)  is the spectral zeta of the integer spectrum  λ j = j \lambda_{j}=j . Several authors have posed the question of how the ‘spectral’ properties of Riemann’s zeta function carry over (or not) to various spectral zeta functions  [ 74 ] ; classical properties of the Riemann zeta function are: 
 
 (1)  
 
 Functional equation: the function  ξ ( s ) = π − s / 2 Γ ( s / 2 ) ζ ( s ) \xi(s)=\pi^{-s/2}\Gamma(s/2)\zeta(s)  satisfies  ξ ⁡ ( 1 − s ) = ξ ⁡ ( s ) \xi(1-s)=\xi(s) ; 
 
 (2)  
 
 Meromorphic structure:  ζ ⁡ ( s ) \zeta(s)  is analytic in  ℂ ∖ { 1 } \mathbb{C}\setminus\{1\} ; 
 
 (3)  
 
 Special values: trivial zeros  ζ ⁡ ( − 2 ​ j ) = 0 \zeta(-2j)=0  for  j = 1 , 2 , … j=1,2,\dots ; 
 
 (4)  
 
 Complex zeros of  ζ ⁡ ( s ) \zeta(s)  and the Riemann hypothesis (RH): the complex Riemann zeros are in the critical strip  0 < Re ⁡ ( s ) < 1 0<\operatorname{Re}(s)<1  and enjoy the reflection symmetries along the real axis and the line  Re ⁡ ( s ) = 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(s)=1/2 . It is conjectured (RH) that the nontrivial zeros all lie on the critical line  Re ⁡ ( s ) = 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(s)=1/2 . 
 
 
 
 
 Let  X n X_{n}  be a  n × n n\small\times n  random Hermitian matrix, and denote by  λ 1 , … , λ n \lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}  its eigenvalues. Assume that, with full probability,  0 0  is not in the spectrum of  X n X_{n}  (this is certainly true for the classical ensembles of random matrices). We proceed to define the averaged spectral zeta function  ζ X n ​ ( s ) \zeta_{X_{n}}(s)  as the maximal analytic continuation of 
 

 
 
 𝔼 ​ ζ X n ​ ( s ) = 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ | X n | − s = 𝔼 ​ ∑ j = 1 n | λ j | − s . \mathbb{E}\,\zeta_{X_{n}}(s)=\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}|X_{n}|^{-s}=\mathbb{E}\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\lambda_{j}|^{-s}. 
 
 
 Note that  𝔼 ​ ζ X n ​ ( s ) \mathbb{E}\,\zeta_{X_{n}}(s)  is not a random function. Much of the paper is devoted to pointing out the analytic structure of  𝔼 ​ ζ X n ​ ( s ) \mathbb{E}\,\zeta_{X_{n}}(s)  when  X n X_{n}  comes from the Gaussian, Laguerre or Jacobi ensembles. 
 
 
 
 1.2.  Time-delay matrix of chaotic cavities 

 
 Random matrix theory provides a mathematical framework to develop a statistical theory of quantum transport. This theory is believed to apply in particular to mesoscopic conductors confined in space, often referred as  quantum dots , connected to the environment through ideal leads. For these systems, Brouwer, Frahm and Beenakker  [ 18 ] , showed that the proper delay times are distributed as the inverse of the eigenvalues of matrices  X n X_{n}  in the Laguerre ensemble (the size  n n  being the number of scattering channels) with Dyson index  β ∈ { 1 , 2 , 4 } \beta\in\{1,2,4\}  labelling the classical symmetry classes, and parameter  α = n \alpha=n . 
 
 
 The moments of the proper delay times have been studied using both random matrix theory  [ 25 ,  27 ,  28 ,  43 ,  56 ,  62 ,  70 ,  71 ]  and semiclassical scattering orbit theory  [ 68 ,  14 ,  50 ] . Of course, the subject of moments on rather general matrix ensembles has been extensively studied. There is, however, one important complication here: the moments of the time-delay matrix are  singular  spectral linear statistic on the Laguerre ensemble. (Invariant random matrix ensembles with singular potentials received considerable interest in recent years in mathematical physics, see, e.g. Refs.  [ 4 ,  10 ,  15 ,  22 ,  60 ,  17 ] .) 
 
 
 In  [ 25 ,  27 ,  28 ] , it was conjectured that the  1 / n 1/n -expansion of the cumulants of power traces for the time-delay matrix of quantum dots has positive integer coefficients. In this paper we prove that the conjecture is true for the first order cumulants, i.e. the moments, when  β = 2 \beta=2  (systems without broken time reversal symmetry). 
 
 
 
 1.3.  Mellin transform of orthogonal polynomials 

 
 The averaged zeta function is related to the Mellin transform of the one-point correlation function 1 1 
               1 
              
              
              
            See Section  3  for the definition of the one-point function and the important identity ( 3.4 ). . In the classical unitary invariant ensembles, by using the well-known determinantal formulae and Christoffel-Darboux formula, the one-point correlation function can be written as a Wronskian 
 

 
 
 ρ n ( 2 ) ​ ( x ) = ∑ j = 0 n − 1 ψ j 2 ​ ( x ) = k n − 1 k n ​ Wr ⁡ ( ψ n − 1 ​ ( x ) , ψ n ​ ( x ) ) , \rho^{(2)}_{n}(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\psi_{j}^{2}(x)=\frac{k_{n-1}}{k_{n}}\operatorname{Wr}(\psi_{n-1}(x),\psi_{n}(x)), 
 
 
 where  ψ j ​ ( x ) \psi_{j}(x)  are the  normalized  Hermite, Laguerre or Jacobi wavefunctions with leading coefficient  k j k_{j} . (The superscript stands for  β = 2 \beta=2 .)
The Mellin transform of a function  f ⁡ ( x ) f(x)  is defined by the integral 
 

 
 
 ℳ ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( x ) ; s ] = ∫ 0 ∞ f ⁡ ( x ) ​ x s − 1 ​ 𝑑 x , \mathcal{M}\left[f(x);s\right]=\int_{0}^{\infty}f(x)x^{s-1}dx, 
 
 
 when it exists. We set  f ∗ ​ ( s ) = ℳ ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( x ) ; s ] f^{*}(s)=\mathcal{M}\left[f(x);s\right] . Of course,  𝔼 ​ ζ X n ​ ( s ) = ρ n ( 2 ) , ∗ ​ ( 1 − s ) \mathbb{E}\,\zeta_{X_{n}}(s)=\rho^{(2),*}_{n}(1-s) . In all instances in this paper, the Mellin transforms have meromorphic extensions to all of  ℂ \mathbb{C}  with simple poles (see Appendix  B.1 ). 
 
 
 Bump and Ng  [ 20 ]  and Bump, Choi, Kurlberg and Vaaler  [ 21 ]  made the remarkable observation that the Mellin transforms of Hermite and Laguerre functions  ψ j ​ ( x ) \psi_{j}(x)  form families of orthogonal polynomials (OP’s) and have zeros on the critical line  Re ⁡ ( s ) = 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(s)=1/2 . (Jacobi functions were not considered by them.)
A few years later, Coffey  [ 23 ]  and Coffey and Lettington  [ 24 ]  pointed out that the polynomials described by Bump  et al.  were hypergeometric OP’s and investigated other families. 
 
 
 Indeed, we show that for the classical matrix ensembles, the Mellin transform  ρ n ( 2 ) , ∗ ​ ( s ) \rho^{(2),*}_{n}(s)  of a Wronskian of two adjacent wavefunctions is a hypergeometric OP (up to a factor containing ratios of Gamma functions). We stress that the proof does not go along the lines of the method of Bump  et al. . They started from the orthogonality of the classical wavefunctions which is preserved by the Mellin transform (a unitary operator in  L 2 L^{2} ). In our case, by explicit computations, we identify a discrete Sturm-Liouville (S-L) problem satisfied by  ρ n ( 2 ) , ∗ ​ ( s ) \rho^{(2),*}_{n}(s)  (as a function of  s s ) and this turns out to be the same S-L of the classical hypergeometric OP’s.
We remark that the Mellin transforms  ρ n ( 2 ) , ∗ ​ ( s ) \rho^{(2),*}_{n}(s)  of  ρ n ( 2 ) ​ ( x ) \rho^{(2)}_{n}(x)  do have a probabilistic meaning (moments of random matrices). The Mellin transforms  ψ j ∗ ​ ( s ) \psi_{j}^{*}(s)  studied in  [ 20 ,  21 ] , while not unmotivated, do not have an obvious probabilistic interpretation. 
 
 
 Once the analytic structure of the Mellin transform of  Wr ⁡ ( ψ n ​ ( x ) , ψ n + 1 ​ ( x ) ) \operatorname{Wr}(\psi_{n}(x),\psi_{n+1}(x))  was established, it became natural for us to look for similar polynomial properties for Wronskians of nonadjacent wavefunctions  Wr ⁡ ( ψ n ​ ( x ) , ψ n + k ​ ( x ) ) \operatorname{Wr}(\psi_{n}(x),\psi_{n+k}(x)) ,  k > 1 k>1 . Such Wronskians do not have a random matrix interpretation. Nevertheless, they have a certain interest in mathematical physics as they appear when applying Darboux-Crum  [ 29 ]  transformations on a Schrödinger operator to generate families of  exceptional orthogonal polynomials   [ 39 ,  49 ,  69 ,  41 ] . 
 
 
 
 1.4.  Orthogonal and symplectic ensembles 

 
 The theory developed for the classical ensembles of complex random matrices suggested to look for similar polynomial properties in the real and quaternionic cases (orthogonal and symplectic ensembles). 
 
 
 Now a fundamental insight came from recursion formulae satisfied by orthogonal / symplectic moments coupled with moments of the corresponding unitary ensembles (see  [ 53 ]  in the Gaussian case and  [ 28 ]  in the Laguerre ensemble).
It turns out that for the classical ensembles of random matrices with orthogonal and symplectic symmetries, certain combinations of moments satisfy three term recursion formulae which, again, correspond to the S-L equations defining families of hypergeometric OP’s. Therefore, this combination of moments plays the role of the single moments in the unitary case: they satisfy three term recursions, have hypergeometric OP factors, reflection symmetries, zeros on a vertical line, etc. 
 
 
 The (single) moments of the symplectic ensembles do have polynomial factors, but these do not belong to the Askey scheme. In the orthogonal cases, we use a novel duality formula (based on the results by Adler  et al.   [ 3 ] ) to write the moments of real random matrices of odd dimension as quaternionic moments plus a remainder containing an orthogonal polynomial factor. 
 
 
 Coupling this result with a classical duality between orthogonal and symplectic ensembles, we discover a functional equation for moments of real random matrices. 
 
 
 
 1.5.  Outline 

 
 The paper has the following structure: 
 
 •  
 
 In Section  2  the physics motivations and application to quantum transport in chaotic cavities are presented; 
 
 •  
 
 In Section  3  we set some notation and we recall the definition of the classical ensembles of random matrices and hypergeometric OP’s; 
 
 •  
 
 In Section  4  we present the main results along with their proofs for the Gaussian, Laguerre and Jacobi unitary ensembles; 
 
 •  
 
 In Section  5  we discuss the large- n n  asymptotics of the spectral zeta functions; 
 
 •  
 
 In Section  6  we discuss the relation of our findings with earlier works on the Mellin transform of classical orthogonal polynomials; then, we extend our results beyond random matrix theory by considering Mellin transforms of products and Wronskians of generic pairs of orthogonal polynomials; 
 
 •  
 
 In Section  7  we discuss the extension of duality formulae between moments of random matrices to higher order cumulants; 
 
 •  
 
 In Section  8  we consider the classical orthogonal and symplectic ensembles and, in particular, present
a new duality formula relating their moments to hypergeometric orthogonal polynomials. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 2.  Motivation and applications 

 
 One of the original motivations for this work was to make some progress on an integrality conjecture for the  1 / n 1/n -expansion of the Laguerre ensemble put forward in  [ 25 ,  27 ,  28 ] . The problem originated from a random matrix approach to quantum transport in chaotic cavities. 
 
 
 In this section, we will first present some of our findings on the Laguerre ensemble. Then we will briefly review the connection between the Laguerre ensemble and the time-delay matrix in chaotic cavities, and explain the applicability of our results to the integrality conjecture. 
 
 
 2.1.  The Laguerre ensemble: reciprocity law and spectral zeta function 

 
 Let  X n X_{n}  be a random matrix from the Laguerre Unitary Ensemble (LUE) with parameter  m ≥ n m\geq n . That is,  X n X_{n}  is distributed according to 
 

 
 (2.1) 
 
 d ​ ℙ ​ ( X ) = 1 Z ​ ( det X α ) ​ exp ⁡ ( − Tr ⁡ X ) ​ d ​ X d\mathbb{P}(X)=\frac{1}{Z}(\det X^{\alpha})\exp(-\operatorname{Tr}X)dX 
 
 
 on the space  𝒫 n \mathcal{P}_{n}  of nonnegative Hermitian matrices, where  d ​ X dX  is Lebesgue measure on  𝒫 n ≃ ℝ n 2 \mathcal{P}_{n}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}} ,  Z Z  the
normalizing constant, and  α = m − n \alpha=m-n . 
 
 
 Consider for integer  k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} , the (inverse) moments 
 

 
 (2.2) 
 
 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n − k = 𝔼 ​ ∑ j = 1 n λ j − k , \mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-k}=\mathbb{E}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}^{-k}, 
 
 
 where  λ 1 , … , λ n \lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}  are the eigenvalues of  X n X_{n} . The above moments are finite if and only if  k ≤ α k\leq\alpha   [ 51 ] . We will prove the following remarkable property of these moments. 
 
 
 Proposition 2.1   (Reciprocity law for LUE) . 
 
 

 
 (2.3) 
 
 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n − ( k + 1 ) = ( ∏ j = − k k 1 α + j ) ​ 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k . \mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-(k+1)}=\left(\prod_{j=-k}^{k}\frac{1}{\alpha+j}\right)\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 The above identity can be verified using the recurrence relation for moments proved by Haagerup and Thorbjørnsen  [ 44 ]  and extended to inverse moments in  [ 28 ] . This gives an explicit formula for the inverse moments given known identities for positive moments.
For instance, 
 

 
 
 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n 0 \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{0} 
 = n \displaystyle=n 
 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n − 1 \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-1} 
 = n α \displaystyle=\frac{n}{\alpha} 
 
 
 
 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n 1 \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{1} 
 = n 2 + α ​ n \displaystyle=n^{2}+\alpha n 
 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n − 2 \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-2} 
 = n 2 + α ​ n ( α − 1 ) ​ α ​ ( α + 1 ) \displaystyle=\frac{n^{2}+\alpha n}{(\alpha-1)\alpha(\alpha+1)} 
 
 
 
 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n 2 \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{2} 
 = 2 ​ n 3 + 3 ​ α ​ n 2 + α 2 ​ n \displaystyle=2n^{3}+3\alpha n^{2}+\alpha^{2}n 
 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n − 3 \displaystyle\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-3} 
 = 2 ​ n 3 + 3 ​ α ​ n 2 + α 2 ​ n ( α − 2 ) ​ ( α − 1 ) ​ α ​ ( α + 1 ) ​ ( α + 2 ) . \displaystyle=\frac{2n^{3}+3\alpha n^{2}+\alpha^{2}n}{(\alpha-2)(\alpha-1)\alpha(\alpha+1)(\alpha+2)}. 
 
 
 
 
 It is natural to consider complex moments or, equivalently, the averaged LUE spectral zeta function defined as 
 

 
 (2.4) 
 
 𝔼 ​ ζ X n ​ ( s ) = 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n − s = 𝔼 ​ ∑ j λ j − s , for  Re ⁡ ( s ) ≤ α , \mathbb{E}\zeta_{X_{n}}(s)=\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-s}=\mathbb{E}\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}^{-s},\quad\text{for $\operatorname{Re}(s)\leq\alpha$}, 
 
 
 and by analytic continuation for other values of  s s . We list below a few remarkable properties of the averaged LUE spectral zeta function. 
 
 (1)  
 
 Functional equation: the reciprocity law ( 2.3 ) suggests to consider the function 
 

 
 (2.5) 
 
 ξ n ​ ( s ) = 1 Γ ⁡ ( 1 + α − s ) ​ 𝔼 ​ ζ X n ​ ( s ) , \xi_{n}(s)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1+\alpha-s)}\mathbb{E}\,\zeta_{X_{n}}(s), 
 
 
 so that ( 2.3 ) becomes the functional equation 
 

 
 (2.6) 
 
 ξ n ​ ( 1 − s ) = ξ n ​ ( s ) . \xi_{n}(1-s)=\xi_{n}(s). 
 
 
 
 (2)  
 
 Analytic structure: it turns out that  𝔼 ​ ζ X n ​ ( s ) \mathbb{E}\,\zeta_{X_{n}}(s)  can be analytically extended to the whole complex plane; In particular,  ξ n ​ ( s ) \xi_{n}(s)  is a polynomial of degree  2 ​ ( n − 1 ) 2(n-1) . 
 
 (3)  
 
 Special values: trivial zeros  𝔼 ​ ζ X n ​ ( 1 + α + j ) = 0 \mathbb{E}\,\zeta_{X_{n}}(1+\alpha+j)=0  for  j = 1 , 2 , … j=1,2,\dots ; 
 
 (4)  
 
 Complex zeros and Riemann hypothesis: as for the Riemann zeta function, the set of complex zeros is symmetric with respect to reflections along the real axis and the critical line  Re ⁡ ( s ) = 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(s)=1/2 . It is tempting to ask whether a RH holds true for the averaged LUE zeta function. Amusingly, the answer is ‘Yes’:  the nontrivial zeros of  𝔼 ​ ζ X n ​ ( s ) \mathbb{E}\,\zeta_{X_{n}}(s)  all lie on the critical line  Re ⁡ ( s ) = 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(s)=1/2 . 
 
 
 These facts are an immediate consequences of the main results presented in Section  4 . See Fig.  1  for a plot of the averaged LUE zeta function and Fig.  2  for an illustration of the zeros. 
 
 
 Remark . 
 
 For any fixed  n n , the function  ζ X n ​ ( s ) \zeta_{X_{n}}(s)  is a finite sum of exponentials. Therefore, without taking the average,  ζ X n ​ ( s ) \zeta_{X_{n}}(s)  is a random analytic function in  ℂ \mathbb{C}  and never vanishes. 
 
 
 
 Figure 1 .  Expected LUE spectral zeta function (modulus) on the critical line  s = 1 / 2 + i ​ t s=1/2+it .  
 
 
 
 2.2.  Application to quantum transport in chaotic cavities 

 
 In  [ 25 ,  27 ] , it was proposed that, for  β ∈ { 1 , 2 } \beta\in\{1,2\} , the cumulants of the time-delay matrix of a ballistic chaotic cavity have a  1 / n 1/n -expansion  with positive integer coefficients  (similar to the genus expansion of Gaussian matrices). The precise conjectural statement is as follows. 
 
 
 Consider the measure ( 2.1 ) with  α = n \alpha=n , and the rescaled inverse power traces 
 

 
 
 τ k ​ ( n ) = n k − 1 ​ Tr ⁡ X n − k ( k ≥ 0 ) . \tau_{k}(n)=n^{k-1}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-k}\quad(k\geq 0). 
 
 
 It is known that the expectation of  τ k ​ ( n ) \tau_{k}(n)  has a  1 / n 1/n -expansion 
 

 
 
 𝔼 ​ τ k ​ ( n ) = ∑ g = 0 ∞ κ g ​ ( k ) ​ n − g . \mathbb{E}\tau_{k}(n)=\sum_{g=0}^{\infty}\kappa_{g}(k)n^{-g}. 
 
 
 
 
 Conjecture   ( [ 28 ] ) . 
 
 κ g ​ ( k ) ∈ ℕ \kappa_{g}(k)\in\mathbb{N} . 
 
 
 
 The conjecture was supported by a systematic computation of certain generating functions, and it is in agreement with the diagrammatic expansions of scattering orbit theory. The integrality of the coefficients in the large- n n  expansion has been also conjectured in the real case (LOE)  [ 28 ] , and for higher order cumulants  [ 25 ,  27 ] . 
 
 
 The results reported in this paper resolve the conjecture in the complex case. 
 
 
 Theorem 2.2 . 
 
 The above conjecture is true. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 To prove the Theorem we take advantage of the reciprocity law to use known results for positive moments of the Laguerre ensemble. 
 
 
 Let  X n X_{n}  be in the LUE with parameter  α = m − n \alpha=m-n . For  k ≥ 0 k\geq 0 , from  [ 45 , Corollary 2.4]  (see also  [ 64 , Exercise 12] ) we read the formula 
 

 
 (2.7) 
 
 1 n k + 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k = ∑ σ ∈ S k n # ⁡ ( σ ) + # ⁡ ( γ k ​ σ − 1 ) − ( k + 1 ) ​ ( m n ) # ⁡ ( σ ) , \frac{1}{n^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k}=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}n^{\#(\sigma)+\#(\gamma_{k}\sigma^{-1})-(k+1)}\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)^{\#(\sigma)}, 
 
 
 where  S k S_{k}  is the symmetric group, and for a permutation  σ ∈ S k \sigma\in S_{k} ,  # ⁡ ( σ ) \#(\sigma)  denotes the number of cycles in  σ \sigma . By  γ k \gamma_{k}  we denote the  k k -cycle  ( 1 2 3 ​ … ​ k ) (1\;2\;3\;\ldots\;k) .
If  m = c ​ n m=cn  with  c > 0 c>0 , the above formula shows that  1 n k + 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k \frac{1}{n^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k}  is a polynomial in  n − 2 n^{-2}  with positive coefficients (see Lemma  4.5  below). 
 
 
 By the reciprocity law ( 2.3 ) with  α = n \alpha=n  (or, equivalently,  c = 2 c=2 ) 
 

 
 (2.8) 
 
 𝔼 ​ τ k + 1 ​ ( n ) = ( ∏ j = 1 k 1 1 − j 2 n 2 ) ​ 1 n k + 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k . \mathbb{E}\tau_{k+1}(n)=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{1-\frac{j^{2}}{n^{2}}}\right)\frac{1}{n^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k}. 
 
 
 The factor  ∏ j = 1 k ( 1 − j 2 n 2 ) − 1 \prod_{j=1}^{k}\left(1-\frac{j^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)^{-1}  in ( 2.8 ) is a product of geometric series. Therefore, we have 
 

 
 (2.9) 
 
 𝔼 ​ τ k + 1 ​ ( n ) = ∑ σ ∈ S k ∑ i 1 , … , i k = 0 ∞ ( 2 # ⁡ ( σ ) ​ ∏ j = 1 k j 2 ​ i j ) ​ n # ⁡ ( σ ) + # ⁡ ( γ k ​ σ − 1 ) − ( k + 1 ) − 2 ​ ( i 1 + ⋯ + i k ) , \mathbb{E}\tau_{k+1}(n)=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}=0}^{\infty}\left(2^{\#(\sigma)}\prod_{j=1}^{k}j^{2i_{j}}\right)n^{\#(\sigma)+\#(\gamma_{k}\sigma^{-1})-(k+1)-2(i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k})}, 
 
 
 and this readily prove that  𝔼 ​ τ k \mathbb{E}\tau_{k}  has an expansion in  n − 2 n^{-2}  with positive integer coefficients.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark . 
 
 From ( 2.7 ) we see that, if  c ∈ ℕ c\in\mathbb{N} , then  1 n k + 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k \frac{1}{n^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k}  ( k ≥ 0 k\geq 0  integer) has a  1 / n 1/n -expansion with positive integer coefficients. The computation above shows that the integrality of the coefficients for the LUE negative moments also holds whenever  c / ( c − 1 ) ∈ ℕ c/(c-1)\in\mathbb{N} . Therefore,  c = 2 c=2  is the only case when all moments  1 n k + 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k \frac{1}{n^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k}  ( k ∈ ℤ k\in\mathbb{Z} ) have integer coefficients in their large- n n  expansion. 
 
 
 
 
 
 3.  Notation and definitions 

 
 3.1.  Classical ensembles of random matrices 

 
 We will consider expectations with respect to the measures 
 

 
 (3.1) 
 
 1 C n , β ​ ∏ j = 1 n w β ​ ( x j ) ​ χ I ​ ( x j ) ​ ∏ 1 ≤ j < k ≤ n | x k − x j | β ​ d ​ x 1 ​ ⋯ ​ d ​ x n \frac{1}{C_{n,\beta}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}w_{\beta}(x_{j})\chi_{I}(x_{j})\prod_{1\leq j<k\leq n}|x_{k}-x_{j}|^{\beta}dx_{1}\dotsm dx_{n} 
 
 
 for finite  n n  and for any value of  β ∈ { 1 , 2 , 4 } \beta\in\{1,2,4\} .
The value of  β \beta  corresponds to ensembles of real symmetric
( β = 1 \beta=1 ), complex hermitian ( β = 2 \beta=2 ) or quaternion self-dual
matrices ( β = 4 \beta=4 ). The function  w β ​ ( x ) w_{\beta}(x)  is the weight of the ensemble: 
 

 
 (3.2) 
 
 w β ​ ( x ) = { e − ( β / 2 ) x 2 / 2 , I = ℝ , x ( β / 2 ) ​ ( m − n + 1 ) − 1 ​ e − ( β / 2 ) ​ x , I = ℝ + , ( 1 − x ) ( β / 2 ) ​ ( m 1 − n + 1 ) − 1 ​ x ( β / 2 ) ​ ( m 2 − n + 1 ) − 1 , I = [ 0 , 1 ] , w_{\beta}(x)=\begin{cases}e^{-(\beta/2)x^{2}/2},&I=\mathbb{R},\\
x^{(\beta/2)(m-n+1)-1}\;e^{-(\beta/2)x},&I=\mathbb{R}_{+},\\
(1-x)^{(\beta/2)(m_{1}-n+1)-1}\;x^{(\beta/2)(m_{2}-n+1)-1},&I=[0,1],\end{cases} 
 
 
 for Gaussian, Laguerre, and Jacobi, respectively.  C n , β C_{n,\beta}  is a normalization constant which depends on the ensemble and is known explicitly  [ 36 ] .
For convenience we set  α = m − n > 0 \alpha=m-n>0  in the Laguerre ensemble and  α 1 = m 1 − n > 0 , α 2 = m 2 − n > 0 \alpha_{1}=m_{1}-n>0,\;\alpha_{2}=m_{2}-n>0  in the Jacobi
ensemble. 
 
 
 We define the  one-point eigenvalue density   ρ n ( β ) ​ ( x ) \rho^{(\beta)}_{n}(x)  corresponding to ( 3.1 ) by 
 

 
 (3.3) 
 
 ρ n ( β ) ​ ( x ) = 𝔼 ⁡ ( ∑ j = 1 n δ ⁡ ( x − x j ) ) \rho^{(\beta)}_{n}(x)=\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\delta(x-x_{j})\right) 
 
 
 We will call ( 3.3 ) the eigenvalue density corresponding to the weight  w β ​ ( x ) w_{\beta}(x)  defining the expectation over ( 3.1 ). The following identity easily follows from the definitions ( 3.3 ) and ( 3.1 ): 
 

 
 (3.4) 
 
 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k = ∫ I x k ​ ρ n ( β ) ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x . \mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k}=\int_{I}\,x^{k}\rho^{(\beta)}_{n}(x)\,dx. 
 
 
 
 
 
 3.2.  Hypergeometric orthogonal polynomials 

 
 We use the standard notation for hypergeometric functions 
 

 
 
 F q p ​ ( a 1 , … , a p b 1 , … , b q , z ) = ∑ j = 0 ∞ ( a 1 ) j ⋯ ( a p ) j ( b 1 ) j ⋯ ( b q ) j ​ z j j ! , {}_{p}F_{q}\left(\begin{matrix}a_{1},\dots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\dots,b_{q}\end{matrix};z\right)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a_{1})_{j}\cdots(a_{p})_{j}}{(b_{1})_{j}\cdots(b_{q})_{j}}\frac{z^{j}}{j!}, 
 
 
 where  ( q ) n = Γ ⁡ ( q + n ) / Γ ⁡ ( q ) (q)_{n}=\Gamma(q+n)/\Gamma(q) .
We need to introduce some families of hypergeometric OP’s  [ 48 ] . Recall that there are three types of hypergeometric OP’s: 
 
 (1)  
 
 Polynomials of the first type are solutions of usual S-L problems for second order differential operators: Jacobi  P n ( α 1 , α 2 ) ​ ( x ) P^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})}_{n}(x)  and its degenerations, Laguerre  L n ( α ) ​ ( x ) L^{(\alpha)}_{n}(x)  and Hermite  H n ​ ( x ) H_{n}(x) . They have a hypergeometric representation, but they are perhaps better known by the Rodrigues-type formulae 
 

 
 (3.5) 
 
 H n ​ ( x ) \displaystyle H_{n}(x) 
 = ( − 1 ) n ​ e x 2 ​ d n d ​ x n ​ e − x 2 , \displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\,e^{x^{2}}\frac{d^{n}}{dx^{n}}e^{-x^{2}}, 
 
 
 (3.6) 
 
 L n ( α ) ​ ( x ) \displaystyle L_{n}^{(\alpha)}(x) 
 = 1 n ! ​ x − α ​ e x ​ d n d ​ x n ​ x n + α ​ e − x , \displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!}\,x^{-\alpha}e^{x}\frac{d^{n}}{dx^{n}}x^{n+\alpha}e^{-x}, 
 
 
 (3.7) 
 
 P n ( α 1 , α 2 ) ​ ( x ) \displaystyle P_{n}^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})}(x) 
 = 1 n ! ​ ( − 1 ) n ( 1 − x ) α 1 ​ x α 2 ​ d n d ​ x n ​ ( 1 − x ) α 1 + n ​ x α 2 + n . \displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(1-x)^{\alpha_{1}}x^{\alpha_{2}}}\frac{d^{n}}{dx^{n}}(1-x)^{\alpha_{1}+n}x^{\alpha_{2}+n}. 
 
 
 
 
 Note that the Jacobi polynomials considered in this paper are orthogonal with respect to the measure  ( 1 − x ) α 1 ​ x α 2 ​ d ​ x (1-x)^{\alpha_{1}}x^{\alpha_{2}}dx  on the unit interval  [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] ; 
 
 (2)  
 
 Polynomials of the second type are solutions of discrete S-L problems (three-terms recurrence relations) with real coefficients: Racah  R n ​ ( λ ⁡ ( x ) , α , β , γ , δ ) R_{n}(\lambda(x);\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta) , including its degenerations Hahn  Q n ​ ( x , α , β , N ) Q_{n}(x;\alpha,\beta,N) , dual Hahn  R n ​ ( λ ⁡ ( x ) , γ , δ , N ) R_{n}(\lambda(x);\gamma,\delta,N) , Meixner  M n ​ ( x , β , c ) M_{n}(x;\beta,c) , etc. They can be represented as finite hypergeometric series 
 

 
 (3.8) 
 
 R n ​ ( λ ⁡ ( x ) , α , β , γ , δ ) \displaystyle R_{n}(\lambda(x);\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta) 
 = F 3 4 ​ ( − n , n + α + β + 1 , − x , x + γ + δ + 1 α + 1 , β + δ + 1 , γ + 1 , 1 ) \displaystyle=\;{}_{4}F_{3}\left(\begin{matrix}-n,n+\alpha+\beta+1,-x,x+\gamma+\delta+1\\
\alpha+1,\beta+\delta+1,\gamma+1\end{matrix};1\right) 
 
 
 (3.9) 
 
 Q n ​ ( x , α , β , N ) \displaystyle Q_{n}(x;\alpha,\beta,N) 
 = F 2 3 ​ ( − n , n + α + β + 1 , − x α + 1 , − N , 1 ) \displaystyle={}_{3}F_{2}\left(\begin{matrix}-n,n+\alpha+\beta+1,-x\\
\alpha+1,-N\end{matrix};1\right) 
 
 
 (3.10) 
 
 R n ​ ( λ ⁡ ( x ) , γ , δ , N ) \displaystyle R_{n}(\lambda(x);\gamma,\delta,N) 
 = F 2 3 ​ ( − n , − x , x + γ + δ + 1 γ + 1 , − N , 1 ) \displaystyle={}_{3}F_{2}\left(\begin{matrix}-n,-x,x+\gamma+\delta+1\\
\gamma+1,-N\end{matrix};1\right) 
 
 
 (3.11) 
 
 M n ​ ( x , β , c ) \displaystyle M_{n}(x;\beta,c) 
 = F 1 2 ​ ( − n , − x β , 1 − 1 c ) , \displaystyle={}_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}-n,-x\\
\beta\end{matrix};1-\frac{1}{c}\right), 
 
 
 where  λ ⁡ ( x ) = x ⁡ ( x + γ + δ + 1 ) \lambda(x)=x(x+\gamma+\delta+1) . Note that some authors define the Meixner polynomials as  m n ​ ( x , β , c ) = ( β ) n ​ M n ​ ( x , β , c ) m_{n}(x;\beta,c)=(\beta)_{n}M_{n}(x;\beta,c) ; 
 
 (3)  
 
 Polynomials of the third type are solutions of discrete S-L problems with complex coefficients: Wilson  W n ​ ( x 2 , a , b , c , d ) W_{n}(x^{2};a,b,c,d)  including its degenerations, continuous dual Hahn  S n ​ ( x 2 , a , b , c ) S_{n}(x^{2};\;a,b,c) , continuous Hahn  p n ​ ( x , a , b , c , d ) p_{n}(x;a,b,c,d) , Meixner-Pollaczek  P n ( λ ) ​ ( x , ϕ ) P_{n}^{(\lambda)}(x;\phi) , etc.
They have the following hypergeometric representations: 
 

 
 
 W n ​ ( x 2 , a , b , c , d ) \displaystyle W_{n}(x^{2};a,b,c,d) 
 = ( a + b ) n ​ ( a + c ) n ​ ( a + d ) n \displaystyle=(a+b)_{n}(a+c)_{n}(a+d)_{n} 
 
 
 (3.12) 
 
 
 × F 3 4 ​ ( − n , n + a + b + c + d − 1 , a + i ​ x , a − i ​ x a + b , a + c , a + d , 1 ) \displaystyle\small\times\;{}_{4}F_{3}\left(\begin{matrix}-n,n+a+b+c+d-1,a+ix,a-ix\\
a+b,a+c,a+d\end{matrix};1\right) 
 
 
 (3.13) 
 
 S n ​ ( x 2 , a , b , c ) \displaystyle S_{n}(x^{2};a,b,c) 
 = ( a + b ) n ​ ( a + c ) n ​ F 2 3 ​ ( − n , a + i ​ x , a − i ​ x a + b , a + c , 1 ) \displaystyle=(a+b)_{n}(a+c)_{n}\;{}_{3}F_{2}\left(\begin{matrix}-n,a+ix,a-ix\\
a+b,a+c\end{matrix};1\right) 
 
 
 
 p n ​ ( x , a , b , c , d ) \displaystyle p_{n}(x;a,b,c,d) 
 = i n n ! ​ ( a + c ) n ​ ( a + d ) n \displaystyle=\frac{i^{n}}{n!}(a+c)_{n}(a+d)_{n} 
 
 
 (3.14) 
 
 
 × F 2 3 ​ ( − n , n + a + b + c + d − 1 , a + i ​ x a + c , a + d , 1 ) \displaystyle\small\times{}_{3}F_{2}\left(\begin{matrix}-n,n+a+b+c+d-1,a+ix\\
a+c,a+d\end{matrix};1\right) 
 
 
 (3.15) 
 
 P n ( λ ) ​ ( x , ϕ ) \displaystyle P_{n}^{(\lambda)}(x;\phi) 
 = ( 2 ​ λ ) n ​ e i ​ n ​ ϕ n ! ​ F 1 2 ​ ( − n , λ + i ​ x 2 ​ λ , 1 − e 2 ​ i ​ ϕ ) . \displaystyle=(2\lambda)_{n}\frac{e^{in\phi}}{n!}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}-n,\lambda+ix\\
2\lambda\end{matrix};1-e^{2i\phi}\right). 
 
 
 
 
 At the top of the hierarchy of hypergeometric orthogonal polynomials are the Wilson and Racah polynomials.
In this paper, the parameter ranges are such that it is most natural to consider the polynomials which appear
as Wilson polynomials and their degenerations, specifically continuous dual Hahn and Meixner-Pollaczek polynomials.
The reader can find the common notation and the most important properties of hypergeometric OP’s in  [ 48 , Section 9] . 
 
 
 
 
 4.  Unitary ensembles 

 
 It is known that the  k k -th moments of the classical unitary invariant ensembles of random matrices  X n X_{n}  of dimension  n n  are polynomials in  n n  (or in  1 / n 1/n  after rescaling). Here we show that the (completed) moments can also be seen as polynomials in the parameter  k k . These polynomials are hypergeometric orthogonal polynomials OP’s belonging to the Askey scheme  [ 8 ] . The polynomial property suggests to consider complex moments or, equivalently, averages of spectral zeta functions.
For the three classical ensembles, define 
 

 
 
 ζ X n ​ ( s ) = Tr ⁡ | X n | − s , X n ∼ { GUE , LUE , JUE } , \zeta_{X_{n}}(s)=\operatorname{Tr}|X_{n}|^{-s},\quad X_{n}\sim\{\mathrm{GUE},\mathrm{LUE},\mathrm{JUE}\}, 
 
 
 and 
 

 
 
 ξ n ​ ( s ) = { 2 2 ​ s Γ ⁡ ( 1 / 2 − 2 ​ s ) ​ 𝔼 ​ ζ X n ​ ( 4 ​ s ) if  X n ∼ GUE , 1 Γ ⁡ ( 1 + α − s ) ​ 𝔼 ​ ζ X n ​ ( s ) if  X n ∼ LUE , Γ ⁡ ( 1 + α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n − s ) Γ ⁡ ( 1 + α 2 − s ) ​ 𝔼 ​ ( ζ X n ​ ( s ) − ζ X n ​ ( s − 1 ) ) if  X n ∼ JUE , \xi_{n}(s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad}cr}\displaystyle\frac{2^{2s}}{\Gamma\left(1/2-2s\right)}\;\mathbb{E}\,\zeta_{X_{n}}(4s)&&\text{if $X_{n}\sim\mathrm{GUE}$}\ ,\\
\\
\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Gamma(1+\alpha-s)}\;\mathbb{E}\,\zeta_{X_{n}}(s)&&\text{if $X_{n}\sim\mathrm{LUE}$}\ ,\\
\\
\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(1+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n-s)}{\Gamma(1+\alpha_{2}-s)}\;\mathbb{E}\left(\zeta_{X_{n}}(s)-\zeta_{X_{n}}(s-1)\right)&&\text{if $X_{n}\sim\mathrm{JUE}$}\ ,\end{array}\right. 
 
 
 when the expectations exist ( s < 1 / 4 s<1/4 ,  s < α + 1 s<\alpha+1 , and  s < α 2 + 1 s<\alpha_{2}+1  for  GUE \mathrm{GUE} ,  LUE \mathrm{LUE} , and  JUE \mathrm{JUE} , respectively) and by analytic continuation otherwise (see Appendix  B.1 ). 
 
 
 We can now state the first result. 
 
 
 Theorem 4.1 . 
 
 For all  n n ,  ξ n ​ ( s ) \xi_{n}(s)  is a hypergeometric orthogonal polynomial: 
 

 
 
 ξ n ​ ( s ) = { i 1 − n π ​ P n − 1 ( 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ x , π / 2 ) if  X n ∼ GUE 1 Γ ⁡ ( n ) ​ Γ ​ ( α + n ) ​ S n − 1 ​ ( x 2 , 3 2 , 1 2 , α + 1 2 ) if  X n ∼ LUE Γ ⁡ ( α 1 + α 2 + n + 1 ) Γ ⁡ ( n ) ​ Γ ​ ( α 2 + n ) ​ ( − 1 ) n − 1 ​ ( α 1 + n ) × W n − 1 ​ ( x 2 , 3 2 , 1 2 , α 2 + 1 2 , 1 2 − α 1 − α 2 − 2 ​ n ) if  X n ∼ JUE , \xi_{n}(s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad}cr}\displaystyle\frac{i^{1-n}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\;P^{(1)}_{n-1}\left(2x;\;\pi/2\right)&\text{if $X_{n}\sim\mathrm{GUE}$}\\
\\
\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Gamma(n)\Gamma(\alpha+n)}\;S_{n-1}\left(x^{2};\;\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2},\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)&\text{if $X_{n}\sim\mathrm{LUE}$}\\
\\
\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+n+1)}{\Gamma(n)\Gamma(\alpha_{2}+n)}(-1)^{n-1}(\alpha_{1}+n)\\
\small\times\;\displaystyle W_{n-1}\left(x^{2};\;\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2},\alpha_{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-2n\right)\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{if $X_{n}\sim\mathrm{JUE}$}\ ,\end{array}\right. 
 
 
 where  x = i ⁡ ( 1 / 2 − s ) x=i(1/2-s) . In particular,  ξ n ​ ( s ) \xi_{n}(s)  satisfies the functional equation  ξ n ​ ( s ) = ξ n ​ ( 1 − s ) \xi_{n}(s)=\xi_{n}(1-s)  in the LUE and JUE cases, and  ξ n ​ ( s ) = ( − 1 ) n − 1 ​ ξ n ​ ( 1 − s ) \xi_{n}(s)=(-1)^{n-1}\xi_{n}(1-s)  for the GUE. Moreover, all its zeros lie on the critical line  Re ⁡ ( s ) = 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(s)=1/2 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 See Theorems  4.2 ,  4.4  and  4.7  below.
∎ 
 
 
 
 The weights of the OP’s in Theorem  4.1  are 
 

 
 
 w ⁡ ( s ) = { | 2 ​ π ​ Γ ​ ( 2 ​ s ) | 2 if  X n ∼  GUE | Γ ⁡ ( s ) ​ Γ ​ ( s + 1 ) ​ Γ ​ ( s + α ) Γ ⁡ ( 2 ​ s − 1 ) | 2 if  X n ∼  LUE | Γ ⁡ ( s ) ​ Γ ​ ( s + 1 ) ​ Γ ​ ( s + α 2 ) Γ ⁡ ( s + α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n ) ​ Γ ​ ( 2 ​ s − 1 ) | 2 if  X n ∼  JUE , w(s)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad}cr}\displaystyle\left|2\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma(2s)\right|^{2}&\text{if $X_{n}\sim$ GUE}\\
\\
\displaystyle\left|\frac{\Gamma(s)\Gamma(s+1)\Gamma(s+\alpha)}{\Gamma(2s-1)}\right|^{2}&\text{if $X_{n}\sim$ LUE}\\
\\
\displaystyle\left|\frac{\Gamma(s)\Gamma(s+1)\Gamma(s+\alpha_{2})}{\Gamma(s+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n)\Gamma(2s-1)}\right|^{2}&\text{if $X_{n}\sim$ JUE}\ ,\end{array}\right. 
 
 
 For the GUE and LUE, the following orthogonality conditions hold 
 

 
 (4.1) 
 
 1 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ∫ 1 2 + i ​ ℝ + ξ m ​ ( s ) ​ ξ n ¯ ​ ( s ) ​ w ​ ( s ) ​ 𝑑 s = h m ​ δ m ​ n , \displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int\limits_{\frac{1}{2}+i\mathbb{R}_{+}}\xi_{m}(s)\overline{\xi_{n}}(s)w(s)ds=h_{m}\;\delta_{mn}, 
 
 
 where  h m h_{m}  is an explicit constant depending on the ensemble (see Appendix  C ). 
 
 
 For an illustration of the zeros on the critical line, see Fig  2  and  3 . For the reader’s convenience, the relation between moments of the unitary ensembles and hypergeometric OP’s is summarised in Table  1 . 
 
 
 Remark . 
 
 The orthogonality in the JUE is slightly different to the GUE and LUE cases. First of all, the fourth parameter of the Wilson polynomial is negative. An orthogonality relation in this case is far from obvious and was established by Neretin  [ 67 ] , see also Appendix  C . This fourth parameter also depends on  n n , therefore each  ξ n ​ ( s ) \xi_{n}(s)  belongs to a distinct family of orthogonal polynomials obtained by fixing the fourth parameter. As before, this orthogonality implies that the zeros lie on the line  Re ⁡ ( s ) = 1 / 2 \mathrm{Re}(s)=1/2 . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 2 .  Zeros of  ξ n ​ ( s ) \xi_{n}(s)  for the LUE. 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 3 .  Zeros of  ξ n ​ ( s ) \xi_{n}(s)  for the JUE. 
 
 
 4.1.  Gaussian Unitary Ensemble 

 
 The GUE is a classical orthogonal polynomial ensemble. In particular, the correlation functions can be compactly and conveniently written in terms of Hermite polynomials. It turns out that the complex moments are essentially a Meixner-Pollaczek polynomial. The moments for GOE and GSE can be expressed using known formulae relating the one-point correlation functions of the three Gaussian ensembles. 
 
 
 Let  X n X_{n}  be a GUE random matrix of dimension  n n . Define  Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) = 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n 2 ​ k Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)=\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{2k}  for all  k ∈ ℂ k\in\mathbb{C}  for which the expectation exists. It is known  [ 46 ]  that, for  k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} , 
 

 
 (4.2) 
 
 Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) = ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ ∑ i = 1 n 2 i − 1 ​ ( n i ) ​ ( k i − 1 ) , Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)=(2k-1)!!\sum_{i=1}^{n}2^{i-1}\binom{n}{i}\binom{k}{i-1}, 
 
 
 where
 ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! = 2 k ​ Γ ​ ( 1 / 2 + k ) / π (2k-1)!!=2^{k}\Gamma(1/2+k)/\sqrt{\pi}  (this is equal to  ( 2 k − 1 ) ( 2 k − 3 ) ⋯ 1 (2k-1)(2k-3)\cdots 1  for  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1  integer).
For each  k k , the moment  Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)  is a polynomial in  n n  with positive integer coefficients: 
 

 
 
 Q 0 ℂ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle Q_{0}^{\mathbb{C}}(n) 
 = n \displaystyle=n 
 
 
 
 Q 1 ℂ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle Q_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}(n) 
 = n 2 \displaystyle=n^{2} 
 
 
 
 Q 2 ℂ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle Q_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}(n) 
 = 2 ​ n 3 + n \displaystyle=2n^{3}+n 
 
 
 
 Q 3 ℂ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle Q_{3}^{\mathbb{C}}(n) 
 = 5 ​ n 4 + 10 ​ n 2 . \displaystyle=5n^{4}+10n^{2}. 
 
 
 
 Q 4 ℂ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle Q_{4}^{\mathbb{C}}(n) 
 = 14 ​ n 5 + 70 ​ n 3 + 21 ​ n . \displaystyle=14n^{5}+70n^{3}+21n. 
 
 
 This fact is the well-known genus expansion for Gaussian complex matrices. 
 
 
 As observed in  [ 75 , Theorem 8] ,  ( 4.2 ) can be written in terms of a (terminating) hypergeometric series: 
 

 
 (4.3) 
 
 Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) n ⁡ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! = F 1 2 ​ ( − k , 1 − n 2 , 2 ) . \frac{Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)}{n\;(2k-1)!!}=\;{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}-k,1-n\\
2\end{matrix};2\right). 
 
 
 From this hypergeometric representation, we see that the moment  Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n) , if properly normalised, is a Meixner-Pollaczek polynomial in  i ⁡ ( k + 1 ) i(k+1)  of degree  n − 1 n-1 . 
 
 
 Theorem 4.2 . 
 
 If we write  x = i ⁡ ( k + 1 ) x=i(k+1) , then for  Re ( k ) > − 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(k)>-1/2 
 

 
 (4.4) 
 
 Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) = i 1 − n ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ P n − 1 ( 1 ) ​ ( x , π / 2 ) Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)=i^{1-n}\;(2k-1)!!\;P^{(1)}_{n-1}(x;\;\pi/2) 
 
 
 In particular,  i n − 1 ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) / ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! i^{n-1}Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)/(2k-1)!!  can be extended to an analytic function in  ℂ \mathbb{C}  (a polynomial), invariant up to a change of sign under reflection  k → − 2 − k k\to-2-k , with complex zeros on the vertical line  Re ⁡ ( k ) = − 1 \operatorname{Re}(k)=-1 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Consider the polynomials 
 

 
 
 q r ​ ( s ) = i − r 1 + r ​ P r ( 1 ) ​ ( i ​ s , π / 2 ) . q_{r}(s)=\frac{i^{-r}}{1+r}P_{r}^{(1)}(is;\ \pi/2). 
 
 
 From the definition of Meixner-Pollaczek polynomials ( 3.15 ) of  P n − 1 ( 1 ) ​ ( x , π / 2 ) P^{(1)}_{n-1}(x;\;\pi/2)  and the hypergeometric representation ( 4.3 ) we see that  Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) = n ⁡ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ q k ​ ( n ) Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)=n\;(2k-1)!!\;q_{k}(n)  when  k k  is a nonnegative integer. In order to prove the general complex case, we use a procedure of analytic continuation from integer points to a complex domain via Carlson’s theorem  [ 7 , Theorem 2.8.1] .
A standard calculation in random matrix theory shows that, for  Re ( k ) > − 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(k)>-1/2 , 
 

 
 
 Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) = ∫ 0 ∞ y 2 ​ k ​ e − y 2 / 2 2 ​ π ​ ∑ j = 0 n − 1 ( H j ​ ( y / 2 ) 2 j ​ j ! ) 2 ​ 𝑑 y , Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)=\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{2k}\frac{e^{-y^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\left(\frac{H_{j}(y/\sqrt{2})}{2^{j}j!}\right)^{2}dy, 
 
 
 where  H j H_{j}  denotes the Hermite polynomial ( 3.5 ) of degree  j j . This shows that  Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)  is analytic in the half-plane  Re ( k ) > − 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(k)>-1/2 . If we write  ∑ j = 0 n − 1 ( 2 j ​ j ! ) − 2 ​ ( H j ​ ( y / 2 ) ) 2 = c 2 ​ n − 2 ​ y 2 ​ n − 2 + ⋯ + c 1 ​ y + c 0 \sum_{j=0}^{n-1}({2^{j}j!})^{-2}\left(H_{j}(y/\sqrt{2})\right)^{2}=c_{2n-2}y^{2n-2}+\cdots+c_{1}y+c_{0}  for some constants  c i c_{i} , then 
 

 
 
 | Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) | \displaystyle\left|Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)\right| 
 = | ∫ 0 ∞ y 2 ​ k ​ e − y 2 / 2 2 ​ π ​ ∑ j = 0 2 ​ n − 2 c j ​ y j ​ 𝑑 x | ≤ ∑ j = 0 2 ​ n − 2 | c j | ​ ∫ 0 ∞ y 2 ​ k + j ​ e − y 2 / 2 2 ​ π ​ 𝑑 y \displaystyle=\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{2k}\frac{e^{-y^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{j=0}^{2n-2}c_{j}y^{j}dx\right|\leq\sum_{j=0}^{2n-2}\left|c_{j}\right|\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{2k+j}\frac{e^{-y^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}dy 
 
 
 We use now the elementary inequality  a + b ​ y + c ​ y 2 ≤ ( a + b ) + ( b + c ) ​ y 2 a+by+cy^{2}\leq(a+b)+(b+c)y^{2}  for  a , b , c , y ≥ 0 a,b,c,y\geq 0 . Setting  d i = | c 2 ​ j − 1 | + 2 ​ | c 2 ​ j | + | c 2 ​ j + 1 | d_{i}=|c_{2j-1}|+2|c_{2j}|+|c_{2j+1}|  (with  c − 1 = c 2 ​ n − 1 = 0 c_{-1}=c_{2n-1}=0 ) we have then 
 

 
 
 | Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) | \displaystyle\left|Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)\right| 
 ≤ ∑ j = 0 n − 1 d j ​ ∫ 0 ∞ y 2 ​ k + 2 ​ j ​ e − y 2 / 2 2 ​ π ​ 𝑑 y \displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}d_{j}\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{2k+2j}\frac{e^{-y^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}dy 
 
 
 
 
 = ∑ j = 0 n − 1 d j ​ 2 k + j − 1 π ​ Γ ​ ( k + j + 1 / 2 ) \displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}d_{j}\frac{2^{k+j-1}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Gamma(k+j+1/2) 
 
 
 
 
 = 2 k π ​ Γ ​ ( k + 1 / 2 ) ​ ∑ j = 0 n − 1 d j ​ 2 j − 1 ​ ∏ i = 0 j − 1 ( k + 1 / 2 + i ) . \displaystyle=\frac{2^{k}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Gamma(k+1/2)\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}d_{j}2^{j-1}\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}(k+1/2+i). 
 
 
 Therefore  i n − 1 ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) / ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! = O ⁡ ( k n − 1 ) i^{n-1}Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)/(2k-1)!!=\operatorname{O}(k^{n-1})  as  | k | → ∞ |k|\to\infty  with  Re ( k ) > − 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(k)>-1/2 . In conclusion,  Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) / ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)/(2k-1)!!  and the polynomial  P n − 1 ( 1 ) ​ ( i ⁡ ( k + 1 ) , π / 2 ) P^{(1)}_{n-1}(i(k+1);\;\pi/2)  coincide on nonnegative integers and their difference is  O ⁡ ( e c ​ | k | ) \operatorname{O}(e^{c|k|})  for any  c > 0 c>0 . By Carlson’s theorem the two functions coincide in the whole domain  Re ( k ) > − 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(k)>-1/2 . 
 
 
 The polynomials  q r ​ ( s ) q_{r}(s)  enjoy the symmetries 
 

 
 
 q r − 1 ​ ( s ) \displaystyle q_{r-1}(s) 
 = q s − 1 ​ ( r ) \displaystyle=q_{s-1}(r) 
 
 
 
 q r ​ ( − s ) \displaystyle q_{r}(-s) 
 = ( − 1 ) r ​ q r ​ ( s ) . \displaystyle=(-1)^{r}q_{r}(s). 
 
 
 Therefore  Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) = n ⁡ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ q n − 1 ​ ( k + 1 ) = n ⁡ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ ( − 1 ) n − 1 ​ q n − 1 ​ ( − k − 1 ) Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)=n\;(2k-1)!!\;q_{n-1}(k+1)=n\;(2k-1)!!\;(-1)^{n-1}q_{n-1}(-k-1)  thus explaining the reflection symmetry  k → − 2 − k k\to-2-k . Recall that the Meixner-Pollaczek polynomial form an orthogonal family with respect to a positive weight on the real line. Therefore, the zeros of  q r ​ ( s ) q_{r}(s)  are purely imaginary;
they occur in conjugate pairs, with zero included if  r r  is odd.
This proves that all the zeros of  i n − 1 ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) / ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! i^{n-1}Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)/(2k-1)!!  lie on the line  Re ⁡ ( k ) = − 1 \operatorname{Re}(k)=-1 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark . 
 
 The polynomials  q r ​ ( s ) q_{r}(s) 
satisfy the difference equation 
 

 
 
 ( s + 1 ) ​ q r ​ ( s + 1 ) = 2 ​ ( r + 1 ) ​ q r ​ ( s ) + ( s − 1 ) ​ q r ​ ( s − 1 ) , (s+1)q_{r}(s+1)=2(r+1)q_{r}(s)+(s-1)q_{r}(s-1), 
 
 
 and the three-term recurrence 
 

 
 
 ( r + 2 ) ​ q r + 1 ​ ( s ) = 2 ​ s ​ q r ​ ( s ) + r ​ q r − 1 ​ ( s ) . (r+2)q_{r+1}(s)=2sq_{r}(s)+rq_{r-1}(s). 
 
 
 Since  q r − 1 ​ ( s ) = q s − 1 ​ ( r ) q_{r-1}(s)=q_{s-1}(r) , these are in fact equivalent. 
 
 
 Recalling that
 Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) = n ⁡ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ q k ​ ( n ) , Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)=n\;(2k-1)!!\;q_{k}(n), 
this yields the Harer-Zagier recursion  [ 46 ] 
 

 
 (4.5) 
 
 ( k + 2 ) ​ Q k + 1 ℂ ​ ( n ) = 2 ​ n ​ ( 2 ​ k + 1 ) ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) + k ⁡ ( 2 ​ k + 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) ​ Q k − 1 ℂ ​ ( n ) , (k+2)Q_{k+1}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)=2n(2k+1)Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)+k(2k+1)(2k-1)Q_{k-1}^{\mathbb{C}}(n), 
 
 
 and the recursion in  n n 
 

 
 (4.6) 
 
 n ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( n + 1 ) = 2 ​ ( k + 1 ) ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) + n ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( n − 1 ) . nQ_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n+1)=2(k+1)Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)+nQ_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n-1). 
 
 
 
 
 
 The Meixner-Pollaczek polynomials can be thought as continuous version of the Meixer polynomials  [ 9 ] : 
 

 
 
 P n ( λ ) ​ ( x , ϕ ) = e − i ​ n ​ ϕ ​ ( 2 ​ λ ) n n ! ​ M n ​ ( − λ + i ​ x ,  2 ​ λ , e − 2 ​ i ​ ϕ ) . P_{n}^{(\lambda)}(x;\;\phi)=e^{-in\phi}\frac{(2\lambda)_{n}}{n!}M_{n}(-\lambda+ix;\;2\lambda,e^{-2i\phi}). 
 
 
 In fact, the normalized moment ( 4.3 ) is a Meixner polynomial (see ( 3.11 )) in  n − 1 n-1  of degree  k k  or, by symmetry, a Meixner polynomial in  k k  of degree  n − 1 n-1 .
The alternative form of Theorem  4.2  using Meixner polynomials is the following. 
 
 
 Theorem  4.2 ′ . 
 
 

 
 (4.7) 
 
 Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n) 
 = n ⁡ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ M k ​ ( n − 1 , 2 , − 1 ) = n ⁡ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ M n − 1 ​ ( k , 2 , − 1 ) . \displaystyle=n\;(2k-1)!!\;M_{k}(n-1;2,-1)=n\;(2k-1)!!\;M_{n-1}(k;2,-1). 
 
 
 
 
 
 The first polynomials are 
 

 
 
 Q k ℂ ​ ( 1 ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(1) 
 = ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! \displaystyle=\;(2k-1)!! 
 
 
 
 Q k ℂ ​ ( 2 ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(2) 
 = 2 ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ⋅ ( k + 1 ) \displaystyle=2(2k-1)!!\cdot\left(k+1\right) 
 
 
 
 Q k ℂ ​ ( 3 ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(3) 
 = 3 ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ⋅ 1 3 ​ ( 2 ​ k 2 + 4 ​ k + 3 ) \displaystyle=3(2k-1)!!\cdot\frac{1}{3}\left(2k^{2}+4k+3\right) 
 
 
 
 Q k ℂ ​ ( 4 ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(4) 
 = 4 ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ⋅ 1 3 ​ ( k 3 + 3 ​ k 2 + 5 ​ k + 3 ) \displaystyle=4(2k-1)!!\cdot\frac{1}{3}\left(k^{3}+3k^{2}+5k+3\right) 
 
 
 
 Q k ℂ ​ ( 5 ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(5) 
 = 5 ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ⋅ 1 15 ​ ( 2 ​ k 4 + 8 ​ k 3 + 22 ​ k 2 + 28 ​ k + 15 ) . \displaystyle=5(2k-1)!!\cdot\frac{1}{15}\left(2k^{4}+8k^{3}+22k^{2}+28k+15\right). 
 
 
 
 
 Remark . 
 
 The normalised GUE moments can be written as products of moments  ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! (2k-1)!!  of a standard Gaussian, times a Meixner polynomial 
 

 
 
 1 n ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) = ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ M k ​ ( n − 1 ,  2 , − 1 ) . \frac{1}{n}Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)=(2k-1)!!\;M_{k}(n-1;\;2,-1). 
 
 
 It is natural to ask whether the Meixner polynomials form moment sequences of some random variables, so that one can ‘decompose’ the GUE one-point function as multiplicative convolution of a standard Gaussian and another probability distribution (product of two independent random variables). In fact, Ismail and Stanton  [ 47 ]  considered the problem of  orthogonal polynomials as moments . It turns out that the Meixner polynomials are moments of translated Beta random variables 
 

 
 
 ( 1 − c ) 1 − β ​ Γ ⁡ ( β ) Γ ⁡ ( x + β ) ​ Γ ​ ( − x ) ​ ∫ c 1 t k ​ ( 1 − t ) x + β − 1 ​ ( t − c ) − x − 1 ​ 𝑑 t = c n ​ M k ​ ( x , β , c ) , (1-c)^{1-\beta}\frac{\Gamma(\beta)}{\Gamma(x+\beta)\Gamma(-x)}\int_{c}^{1}t^{k}\;(1-t)^{x+\beta-1}(t-c)^{-x-1}dt=c^{n}M_{k}(x;\beta,c), 
 
 
 for  Re ⁡ ( x ) < 0 \operatorname{Re}(x)<0  and  Re ⁡ ( x + β ) > 0 \operatorname{Re}(x+\beta)>0 .
Note however that, in our setting, the Meixner polynomials have nonnegative argument  x = n − 1 x=n-1 ,
so that this representation of the one-point function as a ‘convolution’ is purely formal. 
 
 
 
 As a corollary of Theorem  4.2  we have the following two identities. 
 
 
 Corollary 4.3 . 
 
 Reflection formula: 
 

 
 (4.8) 
 
 1 n ​ 1 ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) = 1 k + 1 ​ 1 ( 2 ​ ( n − 1 ) − 1 ) !! ​ Q n − 1 ℂ ​ ( k + 1 ) . \frac{1}{n}\frac{1}{(2k-1)!!}Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)=\frac{1}{k+1}\frac{1}{(2(n-1)-1)!!}Q_{n-1}^{\mathbb{C}}(k+1). 
 
 
 Convolution formula: 
 

 
 (4.9) 
 
 ∑ j = 0 k ( j + 1 ) ( 2 ​ j − 1 ) !! ​ ( k − j + 1 ) ( 2 ​ ( k − j ) − 1 ) !! ​ Q j ℂ ​ ( n ) n ​ Q k − j ℂ ​ ( n ) n = 1 4 ​ 1 ( 2 ​ ( k + 2 ) − 1 ) !! ​ ( Q k + 2 ℂ ​ ( 2 ​ n + 1 ) 2 ​ n + 1 ​ ( k + 2 − 2 ​ n ) + Q k + 2 ℂ ​ ( 2 ​ n − 1 ) 2 ​ n − 1 ​ ( k + 2 + 2 ​ n ) ) . \sum_{j=0}^{k}\frac{(j+1)}{(2j-1)!!}\frac{(k-j+1)}{(2(k-j)-1)!!}\frac{Q_{j}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)}{n}\frac{Q_{k-j}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)}{n}\\
=\frac{1}{4}\frac{1}{(2(k+2)-1)!!}\left(\frac{Q_{k+2}^{\mathbb{C}}(2n+1)}{2n+1}(k+2-2n)+\frac{Q_{k+2}^{\mathbb{C}}(2n-1)}{2n-1}(k+2+2n)\right). 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The reflection formula follows from the hypergeometric representation in ( 4.3 ). To prove ( 4.9 ) we start from the convolution property of the Meixner-Pollaczek polynomials  [ 5 ] 
 

 
 
 P k ( λ + μ ) ​ ( x + y , ϕ ) = ∑ j = 0 k P j ( λ ) ​ ( x , ϕ ) ​ P k − j ( μ ) ​ ( y , ϕ ) . P^{(\lambda+\mu)}_{k}(x+y;\;\phi)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}P^{(\lambda)}_{j}(x;\;\phi)P^{(\mu)}_{k-j}(y;\;\phi). 
 
 
 It follows that 
 

 
 
 ∑ j = 0 k ( i / 2 ) j ​ π ​ ( j + 1 ) Γ ⁡ ( j + 1 / 2 ) ​ Q j ℂ ​ ( n ) ​ ( i / 2 ) k − j ​ π ​ ( k − j + 1 ) Γ ⁡ ( k − j + 1 / 2 ) ​ Q k − j ℂ ​ ( n ) = P k ( 2 ) ​ ( 2 ​ i ​ n , π / 2 ) . \sum_{j=0}^{k}\frac{(i/2)^{j}\sqrt{\pi}(j+1)}{\Gamma(j+1/2)}Q_{j}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)\frac{(i/2)^{k-j}\sqrt{\pi}(k-j+1)}{\Gamma(k-j+1/2)}Q_{k-j}^{\mathbb{C}}(n)=P^{(2)}_{k}(2in;\;\pi/2). 
 
 
 To complete the proof we use the Forward Shift Operator for the Meixner-Pollaczek polynomials [ 48 , Sec. 9, Eq. (9.7.6)] , the reflection formula ( 4.8 ), and the recursion ( 4.6 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 Remark . 
 
 The reflection formula ( 4.8 ) relates expectations of power of traces when the role of  k k  and  n n  is interchanged.
We remark that these are not the only quantities invariant under this type of reflection. The other main examples are moments of characteristic polynomials. See the work of Mehta and Normand  [ 59 , Eq. (3.15)]  and Forrester and Witte  [ 34 , Eq. (4.43)] . A generalization of such a duality to all  β \beta  was obtained in the work of Desrosiers  [ 30 ] . 
 
 
 
 
 4.2.  Laguerre unitary ensemble 

 
 The moments of the Laguerre polynomial ensemble (LUE) enjoy a polynomial property, too. They are (dual) Hahn polynomials ( 3.9 )-( 3.10 ), or their continuous versions ( 3.13 )-( 3.14 ). 
 
 
 Let  X n X_{n}  be a LUE random matrix with parameter  m m . Denote  α = m − n ≥ 0 \alpha=m-n\geq 0 .
Set  Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k}  for all  k ∈ ℂ k\in\mathbb{C}  for which the expectation exists.
Then  Q 0 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = n Q_{0}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=n  and, for  k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} , it is known that  [ 45 ] 
 

 
 (4.10) 
 
 Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = 1 k ​ ∑ i = 1 k ( − 1 ) i − 1 ​ ( m − i + 1 ) k ​ ( n − i + 1 ) k ( k − i ) ! ​ ( i − 1 ) ! . Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{i-1}\frac{(m-i+1)_{k}(n-i+1)_{k}}{(k-i)!(i-1)!}. 
 
 
 For any  k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} ,  Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)  is a symmetric polynomial in  m , n m,n  of degree  k + 1 k+1 ,
with positive integer coefficients: 
 

 
 
 Q 1 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) \displaystyle Q_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n) 
 = m ​ n \displaystyle=mn 
 
 
 
 Q 2 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) \displaystyle Q_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n) 
 = m 2 ​ n + m ​ n 2 \displaystyle=m^{2}n+mn^{2} 
 
 
 
 Q 3 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) \displaystyle Q_{3}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n) 
 = m 3 ​ n + 3 ​ m 2 ​ n 2 + m ​ n 3 + m ​ n \displaystyle=m^{3}n+3m^{2}n^{2}+mn^{3}+mn 
 
 
 
 Q 4 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) \displaystyle Q_{4}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n) 
 = m 4 ​ n + 6 ​ m 3 ​ n 2 + 6 ​ m 2 ​ n 3 + m ​ n 4 + 5 ​ m 2 ​ n + 5 ​ m ​ n 2 . \displaystyle=m^{4}n+6m^{3}n^{2}+6m^{2}n^{3}+mn^{4}+5m^{2}n+5mn^{2}. 
 
 
 In fact, for each positive integer  n n ,  Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)  is a polynomial in  k k  of degree  2 ​ ( n − 1 ) 2(n-1) . After some manipulations, the moments ( 4.10 ) can be expressed in terms of a hypergeometric function: 
 

 
 (4.11) 
 
 Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) ( k + α ) ! = m ​ n ( 1 + α ) ! ​ F 2 3 ​ ( 1 − k , 2 + k , 1 − n 2 , 2 + α , 1 ) . \frac{Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)}{(k+\alpha)!}=\frac{mn}{(1+\alpha)!}\;{}_{3}F_{2}\left(\begin{matrix}1-k,2+k,1-n\\
2,2+\alpha\end{matrix};1\right). 
 
 
 This formula can be extended for  k ∈ ℂ k\in\mathbb{C}  and satisfies  Q 0 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = n Q_{0}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=n . 
 
 
 Theorem 4.4 . 
 
 If we write  k = i ​ x − 1 / 2 k=ix-1/2 , then for  Re ⁡ ( k ) > − α − 1 \operatorname{Re}(k)>-\alpha-1 , 
 

 
 (4.12) 
 
 Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = ( k + α ) ! ( n − 1 ) ! ​ ( m − 1 ) ! ​ S n − 1 ​ ( x 2 , 3 2 , 1 2 , α + 1 2 ) , Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=\frac{(k+\alpha)!}{(n-1)!\;(m-1)!}S_{n-1}\left(x^{2};\;\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2},\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right), 
 
 
 where  S n − 1 S_{n-1}  denotes the continuous dual Hahn polynomial of degree  n − 1 n-1 .
In particular this shows that  Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) / ( k + α ) ! Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)/(k+\alpha)!  can be extended to a polynomial invariant under the reflection
 k → − 1 − k k\to-1-k  (reciprocity law) and, moreover, its complex zeros lie on the critical line  Re ( k ) = − 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(k)=-1/2 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Comparing ( 4.11 ) with the hypergeometric representation of continuous dual Hahn polynomials ( 3.13 ) we get the result for  k k  integer. The extension to complex  k k  is again an application of Carlson’s theorem.
∎ 
 
 
 
 An alternative formulation in terms of Hahn and dual Hahn polynomials ( 3.9 )-( 3.10 ) is as follows. 
 
 
 Theorem  4.4 ′ . 
 
 

 
 (4.13) 
 
 Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n) 
 = m ​ n ​ ( 2 + α ) k − 1 ​ R n − 1 ​ ( ( k − 1 ) ​ ( k + 2 ) ,  1 , 1 , − 2 − α ) \displaystyle=mn(2+\alpha)_{k-1}R_{n-1}((k-1)(k+2);\;1,1,-2-\alpha) 
 
 
 (4.14) 
 
 
 = m ​ n ​ ( 2 + α ) k − 1 ​ Q k − 1 ​ ( n − 1 ,  1 , 1 , − 2 − α ) . \displaystyle=mn(2+\alpha)_{k-1}Q_{k-1}(n-1;\;1,1,-2-\alpha). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Remark . 
 
 The difference equations / three-term recurrence relations for these polynomials (see  [ 48 , Sections 9.5 and 9.6] )
yield the Haagerup-Thorbjørnsen recursion  [ 44 ,  28 ] 
 

 
 (4.15) 
 
 ( k + 2 ) ​ Q k + 1 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = ( 2 ​ k + 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ n + α ) ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) + ( k − 1 ) ​ ( k 2 − α 2 ) ​ Q k − 1 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) . (k+2)Q_{k+1}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=(2k+1)(2n+\alpha)Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)+(k-1)(k^{2}-\alpha^{2})Q_{k-1}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n). 
 
 
 
 
 If  k k  is a positive integer, and we treat  α \alpha  as a parameter,
then  Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)  is a polynomial in  n n  of degree  k + 1 k+1 . Moreover, we can write 
 

 
 (4.16) 
 
 Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = i 1 − k k ​ n ​ ( n + α ) ​ p k − 1 ​ ( i ​ n , 1 , 1 − α , 1 , 1 + α ) , Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=\frac{i^{1-k}}{k}n(n+\alpha)p_{k-1}(in;1,1-\alpha,1,1+\alpha), 
 
 
 where  p k − 1 p_{k-1}  denotes the continuous Hahn polynomial ( 3.14 ) of degree  k − 1 k-1 . 
 
 
 Note the alternative formula: 
 

 
 
 Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = k ! ​ ( n + k − 1 n − 1 ) ​ ( m + k − 1 m − 1 ) ​ F 2 3 ​ ( 1 − k , 1 − n , 1 − m 1 − k − n , 1 − k − m , 1 ) . Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=k!\binom{n+k-1}{n-1}\binom{m+k-1}{m-1}\;{}_{3}F_{2}\left(\begin{matrix}1-k,1-n,1-m\\
1-k-n,1-k-m\end{matrix};1\right). 
 
 
 
 
 If  α \alpha  is fixed and we write  Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = a n ​ R n Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=a_{n}R_{n} ,
where  a n = n ⁡ ( n + α ) a_{n}=n(n+\alpha) , then 
 

 
 
 ( k − 1 ) ​ ( k + 2 ) ​ R n = a n + 1 ​ R n + 1 − ( a n + 1 + a n − 1 ) ​ R n + a n − 1 ​ R n − 1 . (k-1)(k+2)R_{n}=a_{n+1}R_{n+1}-(a_{n+1}+a_{n-1})R_{n}+a_{n-1}R_{n-1}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 It is very natural to consider  m m  dependent on  n n . An interesting situation (from the point of view of the large- n n  limit) is the case  m = c ​ n m=cn  with  c > 0 c>0  and fixed. The next result shows that the moments, as functions of  n n , are polynomials with all zeros on a vertical line in the complex plane. 
 
 
 Lemma 4.5 . 
 
 Let  m = c ​ n m=cn  and  k k  a positive integer. Then,  n − ( k + 1 ) ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( c ​ n , n ) n^{-(k+1)}Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(cn,n)  is a polynomial in  n − 2 n^{-2}  of degree  ⌊ ( k − 1 ) / 2 ⌋ \lfloor(k-1)/2\rfloor  with positive coefficients. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 From  [ 45 , Corollary 2.4]  we have 
 

 
 (4.17) 
 
 1 n k + 1 ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( c ​ n , n ) = ∑ σ ∈ S k c # ⁡ ( σ ) ​ n # ⁡ ( σ ) + # ⁡ ( γ k ​ σ − 1 ) − ( k + 1 ) . \frac{1}{n^{k+1}}Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(cn,n)=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k}}c^{\#(\sigma)}n^{\#(\sigma)+\#(\gamma_{k}\sigma^{-1})-(k+1)}. 
 
 
 For any permutation  σ ∈ S k \sigma\in S_{k} , one has 
 

 
 
 ( − 1 ) k − # ⁡ ( σ ) = sgn ⁡ ( σ ) (-1)^{k-\#(\sigma)}=\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma) 
 
 
 Hence 
 

 
 
 ( − 1 ) k − # ⁡ ( σ ) + k − # ⁡ ( γ k ​ σ − 1 ) = sgn ⁡ ( σ ) ​ sgn ​ ( γ k ​ σ − 1 ) = sgn ⁡ ( γ k ) = ( − 1 ) k + 1 (-1)^{k-\#(\sigma)+k-\#(\gamma_{k}\sigma^{-1})}=\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\mathrm{sgn}(\gamma_{k}\sigma^{-1})=\mathrm{sgn}(\gamma_{k})=(-1)^{k+1} 
 
 
 and so 
 

 
 
 ( − 1 ) # ⁡ ( σ ) + # ⁡ ( γ k ​ σ − 1 ) = ( − 1 ) k + 1 (-1)^{\#(\sigma)+\#(\gamma_{k}\sigma^{-1})}=(-1)^{k+1} 
 
 
 Hence  # ⁡ ( σ ) + # ⁡ ( γ k ​ σ − 1 ) − ( k + 1 ) \#(\sigma)+\#(\gamma_{k}\sigma^{-1})-(k+1)  is even and ( 4.17 ) is a polynomial in  n − 2 n^{-2} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Theorem 4.6 . 
 
 Fix  c > 0 c>0 . The zeros of the polynomials  Q k ℂ ​ ( c ​ n , n ) Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(cn,n)  as a function of  n n 
are purely imaginary and satisfy the interlacing property. 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  q k ​ ( n ) = Q k ℂ ​ ( c ​ n , n ) / n q_{k}(n)=Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(cn,n)/n . Then for each  k k ,  q k ​ ( n ) q_{k}(n)  is a polynomial of
degree  k k , with positive coefficients, and only powers  n k , n k − 2 , … n^{k},n^{k-2},\dots  (see Lemma  4.5 ).
It follows that if we define  p k ​ ( x ) = i k ​ q k ​ ( − i ​ x ) p_{k}(x)=i^{k}q_{k}(-ix) , then  p k ​ ( x ) p_{k}(x)  is a polynomial of degree  k k ,
with alternating signs, and satisfies the (Haagerup-Thorbjørnsen) recursion 
 

 
 (4.18) 
 
 ( k + 1 ) ​ p k ​ ( x ) = ( c + 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) ​ x ​ p k − 1 ​ ( x ) − ( k − 2 ) ​ ( ( k − 1 ) 2 + ( c − 1 ) 2 ​ x 2 ) ​ p k − 2 ​ ( x ) . (k+1)p_{k}(x)=(c+1)(2k-1)xp_{k-1}(x)-(k-2)((k-1)^{2}+(c-1)^{2}x^{2})p_{k-2}(x). 
 
 
 It now follows from  [ 55 , Corollary 2.4]  that  { p k ​ ( x ) } \{p_{k}(x)\}  is a ‘Sturm sequence’ of polynomials. Hence the  p k p_{k} ’s have only real zeros, and they satisfy the interlacing property.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 4.3.  Jacobi unitary ensemble 

 
 Let  X n X_{n}  be a JUE random matrix of size  n n  with parameters  α 1 = m 1 − n \alpha_{1}=m_{1}-n ,  α 2 = m 2 − n \alpha_{2}=m_{2}-n . It turns out that the suitable statistics in this ensemble are  differences of consecutive moments   Δ ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \Delta Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) , defined as 
 

 
 
 Q k ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k} 
 
 
 
 Δ ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle\Delta Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = Q k ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) − Q k + 1 ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle=Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)-Q_{k+1}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 
 
 for all  k ∈ ℂ k\in\mathbb{C}  for which the expectations exist. 
 
 
 Theorem 4.7 . 
 
 In terms of Wilson polynomials, writing  k = i ​ x − 1 / 2 k=ix-1/2 , for  Re ⁡ ( k ) > − α 2 − 1 \operatorname{Re}(k)>-\alpha_{2}-1 , 
 

 
 (4.19) 
 
 Δ ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) = ( k + α 2 ) ! ( k + α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n ) ! ​ ( α 1 + n ) ​ ( α 1 + α 2 + n ) ! ( n − 1 ) ! ​ ( α 2 + n − 1 ) ! ( − 1 ) n − 1 ​ W n − 1 ​ ( x 2 , 3 2 , 1 2 , α 2 + 1 2 , 1 2 − α 1 − α 2 − 2 ​ n ) . \Delta Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)=\frac{(k+\alpha_{2})!}{(k+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n)!}\frac{(\alpha_{1}+n)\;(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+n)!}{(n-1)!\;(\alpha_{2}+n-1)!}\\
(-1)^{n-1}W_{n-1}\left(x^{2};\;\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2},\alpha_{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-2n\right). 
 
 
 This shows that  Δ ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) ​ ( ( k + α 1 + α 2 ) ! / ( k + α 1 ) ! ) \Delta Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)((k+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})!/(k+\alpha_{1})!)  can be extended to a polynomial invariant under the reflection  k → − 1 − k k\to-1-k  (reciprocity law) and, moreover, its complex zeros lie on the critical line  Re ( k ) = − 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(k)=-1/2 . 
 
 
 
 In this case, our strategy is to look for a three-term recursion for  Δ ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \Delta Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)  when  k k  is an integer. In fact,
adapting a method due to Ledoux  [ 52 , Eq. (30)-(31)] , we find the following recurrence relation for the JUE which is the analogue of the Harer-Zagier and Haagerup-Thorbjørnsen recursions. 
 
 
 Proposition 4.8   (Three term recurrence relation for JUE) . 
 
 Let  k ∈ ℤ k\in\mathbb{Z} . Then, 
 

 
 (4.20) 
 
 R k ​ Δ ​ Q k + 1 ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) + S k ​ Δ ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) + T k ​ Δ ​ Q k − 1 ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) = 0 , \displaystyle R_{k}\Delta Q_{k+1}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)+S_{k}\Delta Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)+T_{k}\Delta Q_{k-1}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)=0, 
 
 
 with ‘initial conditions’ 
 

 
 (4.21) 
 
 Δ ​ Q 0 ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle\Delta Q_{0}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = n ⁡ ( α 1 + n ) α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n \displaystyle=\frac{n(\alpha_{1}+n)}{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n} 
 
 
 (4.22) 
 
 Δ ​ Q 1 ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle\Delta Q_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = n ⁡ ( α 1 + α 2 + n ) ​ ( α 1 + n ) ​ ( α 2 + n ) ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n − 1 ) ​ ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n ) ​ ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n + 1 ) . \displaystyle=\frac{n(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+n)(\alpha_{1}+n)(\alpha_{2}+n)}{(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n-1)(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n)(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n+1)}. 
 
 
 The coefficient  R k R_{k} ,  S k S_{k} , and  T k T_{k}  are given by 
 

 
 
 R k ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle R_{k}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = ( k + 2 ) ​ ( ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n ) 2 − ( k + 1 ) 2 ) , \displaystyle=(k+2)((\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n)^{2}-(k+1)^{2}), 
 
 
 
 S k ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle S_{k}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = − ( 2 ​ k + 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ n ​ ( α 1 + α 2 + n ) + α 2 2 + α 1 ​ α 2 − k ⁡ ( k + 1 ) ) , \displaystyle=-(2k+1)(2n(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+n)+\alpha_{2}^{2}+\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}-k(k+1)), 
 
 
 
 T k ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle T_{k}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = ( k − 1 ) ​ ( α 2 2 − k 2 ) . \displaystyle=(k-1)(\alpha_{2}^{2}-k^{2}). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof of Theorem  4.7 . 
 
 The proof is immediate when  k k  is a nonnegative integer by observing that ( 4.20 ) is the discrete S-L problem for Wilson polynomials, and by checking the initial conditions. For  k k  complex we can use the same method of Theorem  4.2 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark . 
 
 Using the hypergeometric representation of Wilson polynomials ( 3.12 ) we have the explicit formula 
 

 
 
 Δ ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) = Δ ​ Q 1 ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) ​ ( k + α 2 ) ! ( 1 + α 2 ) ! ​ ( 1 + α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n ) ! ( k + α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n ) ! × F 3 4 ​ ( 1 − k , 2 + k , 1 − n , 1 − n − α 1 2 , 2 + α 2 , 2 − α 1 − α 2 − 2 ​ n , 1 ) . \Delta Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)=\Delta Q_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)\frac{(k+\alpha_{2})!}{(1+\alpha_{2})!}\frac{(1+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n)!}{(k+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n)!}\\
\times\;{}_{4}F_{3}\left(\begin{matrix}1-k,2+k,1-n,1-n-\alpha_{1}\\
2,2+\alpha_{2},2-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-2n\end{matrix};1\right). 
 
 
 To our knowledge, this hypergeometric representation is new. 
 
 
 
 Remark . 
 
 Ledoux  [ 52 ]  obtained a fourth order recursion for moment differences of the Jacobi ensemble, but the ensemble he considers is shifted compared to ours. In our notation, the moments  L ⁡ ( k ) L(k)  considered in  [ 52 ]  can be written as 
 

 
 
 L ⁡ ( k ) = ∫ 0 1 ( 2 ​ x − 1 ) k ​ ρ n ( 2 ) ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x = ∑ j = 0 k ( k j ) ​ 2 j ​ ( − 1 ) k − j ​ ∫ 0 1 x j ​ ρ n ( 2 ) ​ ( x ) L(k)=\int_{0}^{1}(2x-1)^{k}\rho^{(2)}_{n}(x)dx=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}2^{j}(-1)^{k-j}\int_{0}^{1}x^{j}\rho^{(2)}_{n}(x) 
 
 
 for which it is shown that  L ⁡ ( k ) − L ⁡ ( k + 2 ) L(k)-L(k+2)  satisfies a fourth order recursion. Using  ( 2 ​ x − 1 ) k − ( 2 ​ x − 1 ) k + 2 = 4 ​ ( 2 ​ x − 1 ) k ​ ( x − x 2 ) (2x-1)^{k}-(2x-1)^{k+2}=4(2x-1)^{k}(x-x^{2})  we obtain 
 

 
 
 L ⁡ ( k ) − L ⁡ ( k + 2 ) = ∑ j = 0 k ( k j ) ​ 2 j + 2 ​ ( − 1 ) k − j ​ Δ ​ Q j + 1 ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) . L(k)-L(k+2)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{k}{j}2^{j+2}(-1)^{k-j}\Delta Q_{j+1}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n). 
 
 
 It follows that Ledoux’s moment differences can be expressed as a linear combination of hypergeometric functions. 
 
 
 
 The difference of moments  Δ ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \Delta Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)  can alternatively be written in terms of Racah polynomials. 
 
 
 Theorem  4.7 ′ . 
 
 The JUE difference of moments is ( α 1 + α 2 + n ∉ ℤ \alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+n\notin\mathbb{Z} ) 
 

 
 (4.23) 
 
 Δ ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) = ( − 1 ) n − 1 ​ n ​ ( n + α 1 ) ​ ( n + α 2 ) ​ ( n + α 1 + α 2 ) × sin ⁡ ( π ⁡ ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n − 1 ) ) sin ⁡ ( π ⁡ ( α 1 + α 2 + n ) ) ​ ( 2 + α 2 ) k − 1 ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n − 1 ) k + 2 × R n − 1 ​ ( ( k − 1 ) ​ ( k + 2 ) ,  1 , − α 1 − 2 ​ n , 1 − α 1 − α 2 − 2 ​ n , 1 + α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n ) . \Delta Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)=(-1)^{n-1}n(n+\alpha_{1})(n+\alpha_{2})(n+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})\\
\small\times\frac{\sin\left(\pi\left(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n-1\right)\right)}{\sin\left(\pi\left(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+n\right)\right)}\frac{\left(2+\alpha_{2}\right)_{k-1}}{\left(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n-1\right)_{k+2}}\\
\small\times R_{n-1}\left(\left(k-1\right)\left(k+2\right);\;1,-\alpha_{1}-2n,1-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-2n,1+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n\right). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Using the recurrence relation ( 4.20 ), one can verify the following identity between positive and negative moments (this is the analogue of ( 2.3 )). 
 
 
 Proposition 4.9   (Reciprocity law for JUE) . 
 
 

 
 (4.24) 
 
 Δ ​ Q − ( k + 1 ) ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) = ( ∏ j = − k k α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n − j α 2 − j ) ​ Δ ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) . \Delta Q_{-(k+1)}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)=\left(\prod_{j=-k}^{k}\frac{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n-j}{\alpha_{2}-j}\right)\Delta Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n). 
 
 
 
 
 
 For instance, ( α 1 = m 1 − n \alpha_{1}=m_{1}-n ,  α 2 = m 2 − n \alpha_{2}=m_{2}-n ): 
 

 
 
 Δ ​ Q 0 ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle\Delta Q_{0}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = n ​ m 1 α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n \displaystyle=\frac{nm_{1}}{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n} 
 
 
 
 Δ ​ Q − 1 ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle\Delta Q_{-1}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = n ​ m 1 α 2 \displaystyle=\frac{nm_{1}}{\alpha_{2}} 
 
 
 
 Δ ​ Q 1 ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle\Delta Q_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = n ​ m 1 ​ m 2 ​ ( m 1 + m 2 − n ) ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n ) ​ ( ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n ) 2 − 1 ) \displaystyle=\frac{nm_{1}m_{2}(m_{1}+m_{2}-n)}{(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n)\left((\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n)^{2}-1\right)} 
 
 
 
 Δ ​ Q − 2 ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle\Delta Q_{-2}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = n ​ m 1 ​ m 2 ​ ( m 1 + m 2 − n ) α 2 ​ ( α 2 2 − 1 ) \displaystyle=\frac{nm_{1}m_{2}(m_{1}+m_{2}-n)}{\alpha_{2}\left(\alpha_{2}^{2}-1\right)} 
 
 
 
 Δ ​ Q 2 ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle\Delta Q_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = n ​ m 1 ​ m 2 ​ ( m 1 + m 2 − n ) ​ ( m 2 ​ ( m 1 + m 2 − n ) + n ​ m 1 − 2 ) ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n ) ​ ( ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n ) 2 − 1 ) ​ ( ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n ) 2 − 4 ) \displaystyle=\frac{nm_{1}m_{2}(m_{1}+m_{2}-n)\left(m_{2}(m_{1}+m_{2}-n)+nm_{1}-2\right)}{(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n)\left((\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n)^{2}-1\right)\left((\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n)^{2}-4\right)} 
 
 
 
 Δ ​ Q − 3 ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle\Delta Q_{-3}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = n ​ m 1 ​ m 2 ​ ( m 1 + m 2 − n ) ​ ( m 2 ​ ( m 1 + m 2 − n ) + n ​ m 1 − 2 ) α 2 ​ ( α 2 2 − 1 ) ​ ( α 2 2 − 4 ) \displaystyle=\frac{nm_{1}m_{2}(m_{1}+m_{2}-n)\left(m_{2}(m_{1}+m_{2}-n)+nm_{1}-2\right)}{\alpha_{2}\left(\alpha_{2}^{2}-1\right)\left(\alpha_{2}^{2}-4\right)}

 4.4.  Generating functions 

 
 It is sometimes convenient to define the moments of random matrices in terms of their generating function.
The first example of such a generating function was constructed by Harer and Zagier for the GUE of fixed size  n n  (see Eq. ( 4.25 ) below). This convergent series is a rational function. As emphasised by Morozov and Shakirov  [ 65 ] , from the point of view of random matrices and enumeration problems, this is a highly non-trivial result: a generating function for moments at all genera appears to be rational. The generating function of covariances of the GUE computed in  [ 65 ]  turns out to be again an elementary function. The generating function of higher order cumulants of the GUE have been studied recently by Dubrovin and Yang  [ 31 ]  who expressed them in terms of traces of  2 × 2 2\times 2  matrix-valued series. 
 
 
 One of the advantages of the representation of the moments in terms of hypergeometric OP’s discussed in the present work, is that we can write explicit formulae for the generating functions of the moments of GUE and LUE for fixed  n n   and/or   k k . Remarkably, these closed expressions are elementary functions. 
 
 
 Proposition 4.10 . 
 
 Let  Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(n)  and  Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(m,n)  be the moments of the GUE and LUE, respectively. Then 
 
 GUE:  
 
 

 
 (4.25) 
 
 ∑ k = 0 ∞ Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ t k = 1 2 ​ t ​ ( ( 1 + t 1 − t ) n − 1 ) \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(n)}{(2k-1)!!}\,t^{k}=\frac{1}{2t}\left(\left(\frac{1+t}{1-t}\right)^{n}-1\right) 
 
 
 

 
 (4.26) 
 
 ∑ n = 1 ∞ Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ z n = z 1 − z 2 ​ ( 1 + z 1 − z ) k + 1 \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(n)}{(2k-1)!!}\,z^{n}=\frac{z}{1-z^{2}}\left(\frac{1+z}{1-z}\right)^{k+1} 
 
 
 

 
 (4.27) 
 
 ∑ n ≥ 1 , k ≥ 0 Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ t k ​ z n = z 1 − z ​ 1 ( 1 − t ) − z ⁡ ( 1 + t ) . \sum_{n\geq 1,k\geq 0}\frac{Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(n)}{(2k-1)!!}\,t^{k}z^{n}=\frac{z}{1-z}\frac{1}{(1-t)-z(1+t)}. 
 
 
 
 LUE:  
 
 

 
 (4.28) 
 
 ∑ k = 1 ∞ Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) ( k − 1 ) ! ​ t k k ! = t ​ e t ​ L n − 1 ( 1 ) ​ ( − t ) ​ L m − 1 ( 1 ) ​ ( − t ) . \sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(m,n)}{(k-1)!}\,\frac{t^{k}}{k!}=te^{t}L_{n-1}^{(1)}(-t)L_{m-1}^{(1)}(-t). 
 
 
 

 
 (4.29) 
 
 ∑ n = 1 ∞ Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) ​ ( m − 1 ) ! ( k + α ) ! ​ z n n ! = z ( 1 − z ) α + k + 1 ​ F 1 2 ​ ( − k , 1 − k 2 , z ) \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(m,n)\frac{(m-1)!}{(k+\alpha)!}\,\frac{z^{n}}{n!}=\frac{z}{(1-z)^{\alpha+k+1}}\;{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}-k,1-k\\
2\end{matrix};z\right) 
 
 
 

 
 (4.30) 
 
 ∑ n , m , k ≥ 1 Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) ( k − 1 ) ! ​ z 1 n ​ z 2 m ​ t k k ! = z 1 ​ z 2 ​ t ( 1 − z 1 ) 2 ​ ( 1 − z 2 ) 2 ​ exp ⁡ ( 3 ​ z 1 ​ z 2 − 2 ​ ( z 1 + z 2 ) + 1 ( 1 − z 1 ) ​ ( 1 − z 2 ) ​ t ) . \sum_{n,m,k\geq 1}\frac{Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(m,n)}{(k-1)!}\,\frac{z_{1}^{n}z_{2}^{m}t^{k}}{k!}=\frac{z_{1}z_{2}t}{(1-z_{1})^{2}(1-z_{2})^{2}}\,\exp\left(\frac{3z_{1}z_{2}-2(z_{1}+z_{2})+1}{(1-z_{1})(1-z_{2})}t\right). 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The sum ( 4.25 ) can be computed from ( 4.7 ) using the formula of the generating function of Meixner polynomials  [ 48 , Eq. (9.10.11)] : 
 

 
 
 ∑ k = 1 ∞ k n ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ t k − 1 = ( 1 + t 1 − t ) n − 1 . \sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{k}{n}\frac{Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(n)}{(2k-1)!!}t^{k-1}=\left(\frac{1+t}{1-t}\right)^{n-1}. 
 
 
 The formula for generating function ( 4.25 ) follows from the identity 
 

 
 
 n t ​ d d ​ t ​ ∑ k = 0 ∞ Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ t k = ∑ k = 1 ∞ k n ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ t k − 1 . \frac{n}{t}\frac{d}{dt}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(n)}{(2k-1)!!}\,t^{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{k}{n}\frac{Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(n)}{(2k-1)!!}t^{k-1}. 
 
 
 
 
 The generating function ( 4.26 ) for fixed  k k , is a direct consequence of the representation of the moments in terms of Meixner-Pollaczek polynomials ( 4.4 ) and their generating function  [ 48 , Eq. (9.7.11)] . 
 
 
 Finally, the joint generating function ( 4.27 ) is the resummation in  n n  of ( 4.25 ) (or the resummation in  k k  of ( 4.26 )). 
 
 
 For the LUE we use the generating series of continuous (dual) Hahn polynomials.
From the representation of the LUE moments as continuous Hahn ( 4.16 ), using  [ 48 , Eq. (9.5.11)]  we get 
 

 
 
 ∑ k = 1 ∞ Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) n ​ m ​ ( k − 1 ) ! ​ t k − 1 k ! \displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(m,n)}{nm\,(k-1)!}\,\frac{t^{k-1}}{k!} 
 = F 1 1 ​ ( 1 − n 2 , − t ) ​ F 1 1 ​ ( 1 + m 2 , t ) \displaystyle={}_{1}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}1-n\\
2\end{matrix};-t\right){}_{1}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}1+m\\
2\end{matrix};t\right) 
 
 
 
 
 = F 1 1 ​ ( 1 − n 2 , − t ) ​ F 1 1 ​ ( 1 − m 2 , − t ) ​ e t . \displaystyle={}_{1}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}1-n\\
2\end{matrix};-t\right){}_{1}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}1-m\\
2\end{matrix};-t\right)e^{t}. 
 
 
 Note that the hypergeometric functions on the right-hand side are terminating series. In fact, they are Laguerre polynomials  [ 48 , Eq. (9.12.1)] , thus proving ( 4.28 ). 
 
 
 For ( 4.29 ) we use the representation in terms of continuous dual Hahn polynomials ( 4.12 ) and the formula of the generating series  [ 48 , Eq. (9.3.11)] . We have 
 

 
 
 ∑ n = 1 ∞ Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) ​ ( m − 1 ) ! ( k + α ) ! ​ z n n ! = z ( 1 − z ) α − k ​ F 1 2 ​ ( k + 2 , k + 1 2 , z ) , \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(m,n)\frac{(m-1)!}{(k+\alpha)!}\,\frac{z^{n}}{n!}=\frac{z}{(1-z)^{\alpha-k}}\;{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}k+2,k+1\\
2\end{matrix};z\right), 
 
 
 which is equal to ( 4.29 ) by Euler’s tranformation. Note that the hypergeometric series is terminating. In fact, one could also write it in terms of Jacobi polynomials. 
 
 
 The joint generating series in ( 4.30 ) is a resummation of ( 4.28 ) over  n n  and  m m  using the known formula for the generating function of Laguerre polynomials  [ 48 , Eq. (9.12.10)] .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 Remark . 
 
 The series ( 4.25 ) was computed by Harer and Zagier  [ 46 ]  using different methods.
It is surprising that, although the three-term recurrence in  k k  and the generating function were known, nobody recognized the moments of the GUE as Meixner polynomials. The generating function of the GUE for fixed  k k  (Eq. ( 4.26 )) does not seem to appear in the previous literature. The joint series ( 4.27 ) appears in the work of Morozov and Shakirov  [ 65 ]  who stressed the nontrivial fact that it is a rational function in both variables. 
 
 
 The generating functions ( 4.28 )-( 4.29 )-( 4.30 ) for the LUE seem to be new. It is remarkable, again, that these series sum to elementary functions. 
 
 
 
 
 
 5.  Large- n n  asymptotics of the spectral zeta functions 

 
 It is a classical result that, after rescaling, the one-point function  ρ n ( β ) ​ ( x ) \rho_{n}^{(\beta)}(x)  of the random matrix ensembles considered in this paper weakly converges to a compactly supported probability measure, as  n n  goes to infinity. The limit  ρ ∞ ​ ( x ) \rho_{\infty}(x)  is known as  equilibrium measure  of the ensemble and does not depend on the Dyson index  β \beta . In formulae, for all  k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} , 
 

 
 
 lim n → ∞ 1 n k + 1 ​ 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k = ∫ x k ​ ρ ∞ ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x . \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n^{k+1}}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k}=\int x^{k}\rho_{\infty}(x)dx. 
 
 
 This suggests to define the limit zeta function  ζ ∞ ​ ( s ) \zeta_{\infty}(s)  as the analytic continuation of  ∫ | x | − s ​ ρ ∞ ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x \int|x|^{-s}\rho_{\infty}(x)dx . The limit zeta functions for the classical ensembles turn out to be meromorphic functions. For the LUE and JUE,  ζ ∞ ​ ( s ) \zeta_{\infty}(s)  has infinitely many nontrivial zeros, and they all lie on a critical line. 
 
 
 We discuss the three classical ensembles separately. For notational convenience we consider the GUE, LUE and JUE, although the results hold true for any  β \beta -ensemble. 
 
 
 5.1.  Gaussian ensemble 

 
 The equilibrium measure is given by the semicircular law 
 

 
 (5.1) 
 
 ρ ∞ ​ ( x ) = 1 2 ​ π ​ 4 − x 2 ​  1 x ∈ ( − 2 , 2 ) . \rho_{\infty}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{4-x^{2}}\;1_{x\in(-2,2)}. 
 
 
 After a suitable rescaling, in the large- n n  limit, the integer moments converge to the Catalan numbers: 
 

 
 (5.2)
