 lim n → ∞ 1 n k + 1 ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) = ∫ x 2 ​ k ​ ρ ∞ ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x = 1 k + 1 ​ ( 2 ​ k k ) . \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n^{k+1}}Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(n)=\int x^{2k}\rho_{\infty}(x)dx=\frac{1}{k+1}\binom{2k}{k}. 
 
 
 This formula can be analytically continued and suggests to define the limit GUE zeta function as the analytic continuation of  ∫ | x | − s ​ ρ ∞ ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x \int|x|^{-s}\rho_{\infty}(x)dx : 
 

 
 (5.3) 
 
 ζ ∞ ​ ( s ) = 2 − s ​ Γ ​ ( 1 − s 2 ) π ​ Γ ​ ( 2 − s 2 ) . \zeta_{\infty}(s)=\frac{2^{-s}\;\Gamma\left(\frac{1-s}{2}\right)}{\sqrt{\pi}\;\Gamma\left(2-\frac{s}{2}\right)}. 
 
 
 This function has alternating simple poles and zeros on the positive integers, with no other zeros in the rest of the complex plane. The large- n n  limit is more interesting for matrices in Laguerre and Jacobi ensembles. 
 
 
 
 5.2.  Laguerre ensemble 

 
 Let  X n X_{n}  be in the Laguerre unitary ensemble. Set  α = m − n = ( c − 1 ) ​ n \alpha=m-n=(c-1)n , with  c ≥ 1 c\geq 1 . Define the equilibrium measure 
 

 
 (5.4) 
 
 ρ ∞ ​ ( x ) = 1 2 ​ π ​ x ​ ( x + − x ) ​ ( x − x − ) ​  1 x ∈ ( x − , x + ) \rho_{\infty}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi x}\sqrt{(x_{+}-x)(x-x_{-})}\;1_{x\in(x_{-},x_{+})} 
 
 
 where  x ± = ( 1 ± c ) 2 ≥ 0 x_{\pm}=(1\pm\sqrt{c})^{2}\geq 0  (this is the celebrated Marchenko-Pastur distribution).
Then, 
 

 
 (5.5) 
 
 lim n → ∞ 1 n k + 1 ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( ( c − 1 ) ​ n , n ) = ∫ ℝ x k ​ ρ ∞ ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x , \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n^{k+1}}Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}((c-1)n,n)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{k}\rho_{\infty}(x)dx, 
 
 
 Define the limit LUE zeta function as 
 

 
 (5.6) 
 
 ζ ∞ ​ ( s ) = ∫ ℝ | x | − s ​ ρ ∞ ​ ( x ) ​ d ⁡ x \zeta_{\infty}(s)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|x|^{-s}\rho_{\infty}(x)\operatorname{d}x 
 
 
 with  ρ ∞ ​ ( x ) \rho_{\infty}(x)  in ( 5.4 ). From the finite- n n  functional equation  ξ n ​ ( s ) = ξ n ​ ( 1 − s ) \xi_{n}(s)=\xi_{n}(1-s) , we see that a good definition for the limit  n → ∞ n\to\infty  is 
 

 
 
 ξ ∞ ​ ( s ) = ( x − ​ x + ) s / 2 ​ ζ ∞ ​ ( s ) . \xi_{\infty}(s)=(x_{-}x_{+})^{s/2}\zeta_{\infty}(s). 
 
 
 
 
 Proposition 5.1 . 
 
 Assume  c > 1 c>1 . Then, the functional equation  ξ ∞ ​ ( s ) = ξ ∞ ​ ( 1 − s ) \xi_{\infty}(s)=\xi_{\infty}(1-s)  holds, and the zeros of the  ζ ∞ ​ ( s ) \zeta_{\infty}(s)  all lie on the critical line  Re ⁡ ( s ) = 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(s)=1/2 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 To prove the functional equation it suffices to use the change of coordinates  y = ( x + ​ x − ) / x y=(x_{+}x_{-})/x  in the integral ( 5.6 ), and notice that  x − ​ x + = ( c − 1 ) 2 x_{-}x_{+}=(c-1)^{2} . Alternatively, a calculation using Euler’s integral formula and Pfaff transformation, reveals that 
 

 
 
 ζ ∞ ​ ( s ) \displaystyle\zeta_{\infty}(s) 
 = 1 16 ​ ( x + − x − ) 2 ( x − ) s + 1 ​ F 1 2 ​ ( 3 / 2 , s + 1 3 , x − − x + x − ) \displaystyle=\frac{1}{16}\frac{(x_{+}-x_{-})^{2}}{(x_{-})^{s+1}}\;{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}3/2,s+1\\
3\end{matrix};\frac{x_{-}-x_{+}}{x_{-}}\right) 
 
 
 (5.7) 
 
 
 = c ​ ( 1 − c ) − s − 1 ​ F 1 2 ​ ( 3 4 + 1 2 ​ ( s − 1 2 ) , 3 4 − 1 2 ​ ( s − 1 2 ) 2 , − 4 ​ c ( 1 − c ) 2 ) . \displaystyle=c(1-c)^{-s-1}\;{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{3}{4}+\frac{1}{2}\left(s-\frac{1}{2}\right),\frac{3}{4}-\frac{1}{2}\left(s-\frac{1}{2}\right)\\
2\end{matrix};-\frac{4c}{(1-c)^{2}}\right). 
 
 
 Then apply Euler’s transformation formula to show the functional equation. 
 
 
 To show that the zeros of  ξ ∞ ​ ( s ) \xi_{\infty}(s)  are on the critical line we use an argument based on Sturm-Liouville theory (we borrowed this argument from a similar problem in a paper by Biane  [ 16 ] ). 
 
 
 First, observe that the integral ( 5.6 ) can not vanish for  s s  real. We want to show that the zeros of 
 

 
 
 F 1 2 ​ ( 3 4 + μ , 3 4 − μ 2 , − 4 ​ c ( 1 − c ) 2 ) , {}_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{3}{4}+\mu,\frac{3}{4}-\mu\\
2\end{matrix};-\frac{4c}{(1-c)^{2}}\right), 
 
 
 where  2 ​ μ = s − 1 / 2 2\mu=s-1/2 , lie on the imaginary axis.
The function  y ⁡ ( z ) = F 1 2 ​ ( 3 / 4 + μ , 3 / 4 − μ , 2 , − z ) y(z)={}_{2}F_{1}(3/4+\mu,3/4-\mu;2;-z)  satisfies the hypergeometric equation 
 

 
 
 z ⁡ ( 1 + z ) ​ y ′′ + ( 2 + 5 ​ z / 2 ) ​ y ′ + ( 9 / 16 − μ 2 ) ​ y = 0 . z(1+z)y^{\prime\prime}+(2+5z/2)y^{\prime}+(9/16-\mu^{2})y=0. 
 
 
 Thus, if  μ \mu  is such that  y ⁡ ( 4 ​ c / ( 1 − c ) 2 ) = 0 y(4c/(1-c)^{2})=0 , then  y ⁡ ( x ) y(x)  is a solution to the Sturm-Liouville problem 
 

 
 
 ( p ⁡ ( x ) ​ y ′ ​ ( x ) ) ′ + q ⁡ ( x ) ​ y ​ ( x ) = μ 2 ​ w ​ ( x ) ​ y ​ ( x ) (p(x)y^{\prime}(x))^{\prime}+q(x)y(x)=\mu^{2}w(x)y(x) 
 
 
 on  [ 4 ​ c / ( 1 − c ) 2 , ∞ ) [4c/(1-c)^{2},\infty) , with Dirichlet boundary conditions,
where  p ⁡ ( x ) = x 2 ​ ( 1 + x ) 1 / 2 p(x)=x^{2}(1+x)^{1/2} ,  q ⁡ ( x ) = ( 9 / 16 ) ​ w ​ ( x ) q(x)=(9/16)w(x)  and  w ( x ) = x ( 1 + x ) − 1 / 2 w(x)=x(1+x)^{-1/2} .
It then follows from the Sturm-Liouville theory that the eigenvalues  μ 2 \mu^{2}  are real which can only happen if  μ \mu  is real or purely imaginary. Since we have excluded the real case, we conclude that  2 ​ μ = s − 1 / 2 2\mu=s-1/2  is purely imaginary: the zeros of  ζ ∞ ​ ( s ) \zeta_{\infty}(s)  all lie on the critical line  Re ⁡ ( s ) = 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(s)=1/2 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 5.3.  Jacobi ensemble 

 
 Let  X n X_{n}  be in the JUE.
Set  α 1 , 2 = ( c 1 , 2 − 1 ) ​ n \alpha_{1,2}=(c_{1,2}-1)n , with  c 1 , 2 ≥ 1 c_{1,2}\geq 1 . Then, the equilibrium measure is 
 

 
 (5.8) 
 
 ρ ∞ ​ ( x ) = c 1 + c 2 2 ​ π ​ x ​ ( 1 − x ) ​ ( x + − x ) ​ ( x − x − ) ​  1 x ∈ ( x − , x + ) \rho_{\infty}(x)=\frac{c_{1}+c_{2}}{2\pi x(1-x)}\sqrt{(x_{+}-x)(x-x_{-})}\;1_{x\in(x_{-},x_{+})} 
 
 
 where  x ± = ( c 1 ± c 2 ​ ( c 1 + c 2 − 1 ) c 1 + c 2 ) 2 x_{\pm}=\left(\frac{\sqrt{c_{1}}\pm\sqrt{c_{2}(c_{1}+c_{2}-1)}}{c_{1}+c_{2}}\right)^{2} , and 
 

 
 (5.9) 
 
 lim n → ∞ 1 n k + 1 ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( ( c 1 − 1 ) ​ n , ( c 2 − 1 ) ​ n , n ) = ∫ ℝ x k ​ ( 1 − x ) ​ ρ ∞ ​ ( x ) ​ d ⁡ x \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n^{k+1}}Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}((c_{1}-1)n,(c_{2}-1)n,n)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{k}(1-x)\rho_{\infty}(x)\operatorname{d}x 
 
 
 Define the limit JUE zeta function as 
 

 
 (5.10) 
 
 ζ ∞ ​ ( s ) = ∫ ℝ | x | − s ​ ρ ∞ ​ ( x ) ​ d ⁡ x \zeta_{\infty}(s)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|x|^{-s}\rho_{\infty}(x)\operatorname{d}x 
 
 
 with  ρ ∞ ​ ( x ) \rho_{\infty}(x)  in ( 5.8 ). Again, the finite- n n  functional equation  ξ n ​ ( s ) = ξ n ​ ( 1 − s ) \xi_{n}(s)=\xi_{n}(1-s) , suggests the definition of 
 

 
 
 ξ ∞ ​ ( s ) = ( x − ​ x + ) s / 2 ​ ( ζ ∞ ​ ( s ) − ζ ∞ ​ ( s − 1 ) ) . \xi_{\infty}(s)=(x_{-}x_{+})^{s/2}\left(\zeta_{\infty}(s)-\zeta_{\infty}(s-1)\right). 
 
 
 
 
 Proposition 5.2 . 
 
 Assume  c 1 , 2 > 1 c_{1,2}>1 . Then, the functional equation  ξ ∞ ​ ( s ) = ξ ∞ ​ ( 1 − s ) \xi_{\infty}(s)=\xi_{\infty}(1-s)  holds, and the complex zeros of  ξ ∞ ​ ( s ) \xi_{\infty}(s)  all lie on the critical line  Re ⁡ ( s ) = 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(s)=1/2 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Using Euler’s integral formula, we have 
 

 
 (5.11) 
 
 ζ ∞ ​ ( s ) = c 1 + c 2 16 ​ ( x + − x − ) 2 ( x − ) s + 1 ​ F 1 2 ​ ( 3 / 2 , s + 1 3 , x − − x + x − ) . \zeta_{\infty}(s)=\frac{c_{1}+c_{2}}{16}\frac{(x_{+}-x_{-})^{2}}{(x_{-})^{s+1}}\;{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}3/2,s+1\\
3\end{matrix};\frac{x_{-}-x_{+}}{x_{-}}\right). 
 
 
 The proof of Proposition  5.1  is easily adapted.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 6.  Beyond random matrices: Wronskians of orthogonal polynomials 

 
 6.1.  Mellin transform of orthogonal polynomials 

 
 Bump and Ng  [ 20 ]  and Bump, Choi, Kurlberg and Vaaler  [ 21 ]  made the remarkable discovery that the Mellin transforms of Hermite and Laguerre functions have zeros on the critical line  Re ⁡ ( s ) = 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(s)=1/2 . Their proof is based on the observation that the Mellin transform preserves orthogonality. Hence, Mellin transforms of orthogonal polynomials are themselves orthogonal with respect to some inner product. Later  [ 23 ,  24 ]  it was noticed that those orthogonal functions are hypergeometric OP’s (multiplied by some nonnegative integrable weight). 
 
 
 Consider, for concreteness, the Hermite polynomials  H n ​ ( x ) H_{n}(x)  and the normalised Hermite wavefunctions  ϕ n ( x ) = ( 2 n n ! π ) − 1 / 2 e − x 2 / 2 H n ( x ) \phi_{n}(x)=(2^{n}n!\sqrt{\pi})^{-1/2}e^{-x^{2}/2}H_{n}(x) .
The following proposition follows from the result of Bump  et al.   [ 20 ,  21 ] . 
 
 
 Proposition 6.1   (Mellin transform of Hermite functions) . 
 
 For all integers  n ≥ 0 n\geq 0 
 

 
 
 ϕ 2 ​ n ∗ ​ ( s ) \displaystyle\phi_{2n}^{*}(s) 
 = i n ​ 2 n − 1 + s 2 ​ n ! ( 2 ​ n ) ! ​ π ​ Γ ​ ( s 2 ) ​ P n ( 1 4 ) ​ ( − i ​ ( s − 1 / 2 ) 2 , π 2 ) \displaystyle=i^{n}2^{n-1+\frac{s}{2}}\frac{n!}{\sqrt{(2n)!\sqrt{\pi}}}\Gamma\left(\frac{s}{2}\right)P^{\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}_{n}\left(-i\frac{(s-1/2)}{2};\;\frac{\pi}{2}\right) 
 
 
 
 ϕ 2 ​ n + 1 ∗ ​ ( s ) \displaystyle\phi_{2n+1}^{*}(s) 
 = i n ​ 2 n + s 2 ​ n ! ( 2 ​ n + 1 ) ! ​ π ​ Γ ​ ( s + 1 2 ) ​ P n ( 3 4 ) ​ ( − i ​ ( s − 1 / 2 ) 2 , π 2 ) . \displaystyle=i^{n}2^{n+\frac{s}{2}}\frac{n!}{\sqrt{(2n+1)!\sqrt{\pi}}}\Gamma\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)P^{\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)}_{n}\left(-i\frac{(s-1/2)}{2};\;\frac{\pi}{2}\right). 
 
 
 
 
 
 The averaged spectral zeta function of unitary invariant ensembles of random matrices can be interpreted as
Mellin transform of Wronskians of adjacent Hermite, Laguerre or Jacobi wavefunctions. Given our results,
it is natural to ask whether more general Wronskians have the property that their Mellin transforms can be written in terms of hypergeometric OP’s. 
 
 
 
 6.2.  Mellin transforms of products and Wronskians of classical orthogonal polynomials 

 
 In this section we will use repeatedly the properties ( B.2 )-( B.3 )-( B.4 ) of the Mellin transform. 
 
 
 The Wronskian of smooth functions  f 1 ​ ( x ) ​ … , f m ​ ( x ) f_{1}(x)\dots,f_{m}(x)  is defined as 
 

 
 
 Wr ⁡ ( f 1 ​ ( x ) , … , f m ​ ( x ) ) = det ( f j ( i − 1 ) ​ ( x ) ) i , j = 1 m . \operatorname{Wr}(f_{1}(x),\dots,f_{m}(x))=\det\left(f^{(i-1)}_{j}(x)\right)_{i,j=1}^{m}. 
 
 
 Note the homogeneity property 
 

 
 
 Wr ⁡ ( g ⁡ ( x ) ​ f 1 ​ ( x ) , … , g ⁡ ( x ) ​ f m ​ ( x ) ) = ( g ⁡ ( x ) ) m ​ Wr ⁡ ( f 1 ​ ( x ) , … , f m ​ ( x ) ) \operatorname{Wr}(g(x)f_{1}(x),\dots,g(x)f_{m}(x))=(g(x))^{m}\operatorname{Wr}(f_{1}(x),\dots,f_{m}(x)) 
 
 
 
 
 Consider the Hermite, Laguerre and Jacobi polynomials defined in ( 3.5 )-( 3.6 )-( 3.7 ), and the associated normalised wavefunctions 
 

 
 (6.1) 
 
 ϕ n ​ ( x ) = { ( 2 n n ! π ) − 1 / 2 e − x 2 / 2 H n ( x ) (Hermite) n ! Γ ⁡ ( n + α + 1 ) x α / 2 e − x / 2 L n ( α ) ( x ) χ ℝ + ( x ) (Laguerre) n ! ​ ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n + 1 ) ​ Γ ​ ( α 1 + α 2 + n + 1 ) Γ ⁡ ( α 1 + n + 1 ) ​ Γ ​ ( α 2 + n + 1 ) × ( 1 − x ) α 1 / 2 ​ x α 2 / 2 ​ P n ( α 1 , α 2 ) ​ ( x ) ​ χ [ 0 , 1 ] ​ ( x ) (Jacobi) . \phi_{n}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad}cr}\displaystyle(2^{n}n!\sqrt{\pi})^{-1/2}e^{-x^{2}/2}H_{n}(x)&\text{(Hermite)}\\
\\
\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{n!}{\Gamma(n+\alpha+1)}}x^{\alpha/2}e^{-x/2}L_{n}^{(\alpha)}(x)\,\chi_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(x)&\text{(Laguerre)}\\
\\
\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{n!(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n+1)\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+n+1)}{\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+n+1)\Gamma(\alpha_{2}+n+1)}}&\text{}\\
\,\small\times\,(1-x)^{\alpha_{1}/2}x^{\alpha_{2}/2}P_{n}^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})}(x)\,\chi_{[0,1]}(x)\hfil\hskip 9.24994pt&\text{(Jacobi)}.\end{array}\right. 
 
 
 The wavefunctions are orthonormal 
 

 
 
 ∫ I ϕ n ​ ( x ) ​ ϕ m ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x = δ n , m , \int_{I}\phi_{n}(x)\phi_{m}(x)dx=\delta_{n,m}, 
 
 
 where  I = ℝ , ℝ + I=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}_{+} , and  [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1]  for Hermite, Laguerre, and Jacobi, respectively.
We set 
 

 
 
 ω n , ℓ ​ ( x ) \displaystyle\omega_{n,\ell}(x) 
 = ϕ n ​ ( x ) ​ ϕ n + ℓ ​ ( x ) \displaystyle=\phi_{n}(x)\phi_{n+\ell}(x) 
 
 
 
 W n , ℓ ​ ( x ) \displaystyle W_{n,\ell}(x) 
 = Wr ⁡ ( ϕ n ​ ( x ) , ϕ n + ℓ ​ ( x ) ) . \displaystyle=\operatorname{Wr}(\phi_{n}(x),\phi_{n+\ell}(x)). 
 
 
 
 
 Theorem 6.2 . 
 
 Let  ϕ n ​ ( x ) \phi_{n}(x)  be Hermite wavefunctions ( 6.1 ). Then, 
 
 i)  
 
 the Mellin transform of the products is 
 

 
 (6.2) 
 
 ω n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) = i n ​ 2 ℓ 2 − s ​ n ! ( n + ℓ ) ! ​ Γ ⁡ ( s ) Γ ⁡ ( s − ℓ + 1 2 ) ​ P n ( ℓ + 1 2 ) ​ ( − i ​ s 2 , π 2 ) ; \omega_{n,\ell}^{*}(s)=i^{n}2^{\frac{\ell}{2}-s}\sqrt{\frac{n!}{(n+\ell)!}}\frac{\Gamma(s)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{s-\ell+1}{2}\right)}\,P_{n}^{\left(\frac{\ell+1}{2}\right)}\left(-\frac{is}{2};\frac{\pi}{2}\right); 
 
 
 
 ii)  
 
 the Mellin transform of the Wronskians is 
 

 
 (6.3) 
 
 W n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s − 1 ) = 2 ​ ℓ s − 1 ​ ω n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) . W^{*}_{n,\ell}(s-1)=\frac{2\ell}{s-1}\omega^{*}_{n,\ell}(s). 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Part i): Given the three-term recurrence of the Meixner-Pollaczek polynomials [ 48 , Sec. 9, Eq. (9.7.3)] , it is sufficient to show that  ω n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) \omega_{n,\ell}^{*}(s)  satisfies the recurrence 
 

 
 (6.4) 
 
 ( n + 1 ) ​ ( n + ℓ + 1 ) ​ ω n + 1 , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) − s ​ ω n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) − n ⁡ ( n + ℓ ) ​ ω n − 1 , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) = 0 . \sqrt{(n+1)(n+\ell+1)}\omega_{n+1,\ell}^{*}(s)-s\omega_{n,\ell}^{*}(s)-\sqrt{n(n+\ell)}\omega_{n-1,\ell}^{*}(s)=0. 
 
 
 Using the three-term recurrence of the Hermite functions 
 

 
 (6.5) 
 
 n + 1 ​ ϕ n + 1 ​ ( x ) − 2 ​ x ​ ϕ n ​ ( x ) + n ​ ϕ n − 1 ​ ( x ) = 0 \sqrt{n+1}\phi_{n+1}(x)-\sqrt{2}x\phi_{n}(x)+\sqrt{n}\phi_{n-1}(x)=0 
 
 
 we have 
 

 
 
 ( n + 1 ) ​ ( n + ℓ + 1 ) ​ ω n + 1 , ℓ ​ ( x ) = 2 ​ x 2 ​ ω n , ℓ ​ ( x ) + n ⁡ ( n + ℓ ) ​ ω n − 1 , ℓ ​ ( x ) − 2 ​ x ​ ( n + ℓ ​ ω n , ℓ − 1 ​ ( x ) + n ​ ω n − 1 , ℓ + 1 ​ ( x ) ) . \sqrt{(n+1)(n+\ell+1)}\omega_{n+1,\ell}(x)=2x^{2}\omega_{n,\ell}(x)+\sqrt{n(n+\ell)}\omega_{n-1,\ell}(x)\\
-\sqrt{2}x\left(\sqrt{n+\ell}\omega_{n,\ell-1}(x)+\sqrt{n}\omega_{n-1,\ell+1}(x)\right). 
 
 
 We now take the Mellin transform of both sides. Using the equation 
 

 
 (6.6) 
 
 ϕ n ′ ​ ( x ) + x ​ ϕ n ​ ( x ) − 2 ​ n ​ ϕ n − 1 ​ ( x ) = 0 , \phi^{\prime}_{n}(x)+x\phi_{n}(x)-\sqrt{2n}\phi_{n-1}(x)=0, 
 
 
 we get 
 

 
 
 ( n + 1 ) ​ ( n + ℓ + 1 ) ​ ω n + 1 , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) = − ℳ ⁡ [ x ​ ω n , ℓ ′ ​ ( x ) ; s ] + n ⁡ ( n + ℓ ) ​ ω n − 1 , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) . \sqrt{(n+1)(n+\ell+1)}\omega_{n+1,\ell}^{*}(s)=-\mathcal{M}\left[x\omega_{n,\ell}^{\prime}(x);s\right]+\sqrt{n(n+\ell)}\omega_{n-1,\ell}^{*}(s). 
 
 
 The fact that  ℳ ⁡ [ x ​ ω n , ℓ ′ ​ ( x ) ; s ] = − s ​ ω n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) \mathcal{M}\left[x\omega^{\prime}_{n,\ell}(x);s\right]=-s\omega_{n,\ell}^{*}(s)  follows from integration by parts (or property ( B.4 ) of the Mellin transform, with  m = 1 m=1 ). We have proved ( 6.4 ). To complete the proof of ( 6.2 ) we compute the initial conditions 
 

 
 
 ω 0 , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) = 2 ℓ 2 − s ℓ ! ​ Γ ⁡ ( s ) Γ ⁡ ( s − ℓ + 1 2 ) , and ω 1 , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) = 2 ℓ 2 − s ( ℓ + 1 ) ! ​ Γ ⁡ ( s ) Γ ⁡ ( s − ℓ + 1 2 ) ​ s . \omega_{0,\ell}^{*}(s)=\frac{2^{\frac{\ell}{2}-s}}{\sqrt{\ell!}}\frac{\Gamma(s)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{s-\ell+1}{2}\right)},\quad\text{and}\quad\omega_{1,\ell}^{*}(s)=\frac{2^{\frac{\ell}{2}-s}}{\sqrt{(\ell+1)!}}\frac{\Gamma(s)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{s-\ell+1}{2}\right)}\,s. 
 
 
 
 
 Part ii): Note that 
 

 
 
 W n , ℓ ​ ( x ) = 2 ​ ( n + ℓ ) ​ ω n , ℓ − 1 ​ ( x ) − 2 ​ n ​ ω n − 1 , ℓ + 1 ​ ( x ) W_{n,\ell}(x)=\sqrt{2(n+\ell)}\omega_{n,\ell-1}(x)-\sqrt{2n}\omega_{n-1,\ell+1}(x) 
 
 
 where we have used the identity ( 6.6 ).
Taking the Mellin transform of both sides,
substituting the identity ( 6.2 ), and using the Forward Shift Operator for the Meixner-Pollaczek polynomials [ 48 , Sec. 9, Eq. (9.7.6)]  we complete the proof of ( 6.3 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 The following analogue of Proposition  6.1  for the Laguerre wavefunction is essentially due to Bump  et al.   [ 21 ]  and Coffey  [ 23 ] . 
 
 
 Proposition 6.3 . 
 
 Let  ϕ n ​ ( x ) \phi_{n}(x)  be Laguerre wavefunctions ( 6.1 ). Then 
 

 
 
 ϕ n ∗ ​ ( s ) = ( − i ) n ​ 2 s + α / 2 ​ n ! Γ ⁡ ( n + α + 1 ) ​ Γ ​ ( s + α / 2 ) ​ P n ( 1 + α 2 ) ​ ( 1 i ​ ( s − 1 / 2 ) , π 2 ) . \phi_{n}^{*}(s)=(-i)^{n}2^{s+\alpha/2}\sqrt{\frac{n!}{\Gamma(n+\alpha+1)}}\Gamma(s+\alpha/2)P_{n}^{(\frac{1+\alpha}{2})}\left(\frac{1}{i}(s-1/2);\;\frac{\pi}{2}\right). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Theorem 6.4 . 
 
 Let  ϕ n ​ ( x ) \phi_{n}(x)  be Laguerre wavefunctions. Then, 
 
 i)  
 
 the Mellin transform of the products is 
 

 
 (6.7) 
 
 ω n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) = ( − 1 ) ℓ ​ Γ ⁡ ( s ) ​ Γ ​ ( s + α ) Γ ⁡ ( s − ℓ ) ​ n ! ​ ( n + ℓ ) ! ​ Γ ​ ( n + α + 1 ) ​ Γ ​ ( n + ℓ + α + 1 ) × S n ​ ( − ( s − 1 / 2 ) 2 , ℓ + 1 / 2 , 1 / 2 , α + 1 / 2 ) ; \omega_{n,\ell}^{*}(s)=(-1)^{\ell}\frac{\Gamma(s)\Gamma(s+\alpha)}{\Gamma(s-\ell)\sqrt{n!(n+\ell)!\Gamma(n+\alpha+1)\Gamma(n+\ell+\alpha+1)}}\\
\small\times S_{n}\left(-\left(s-1/2\right)^{2};\ell+1/2,1/2,\alpha+1/2\right); 
 
 
 
 ii)  
 
 the Mellin transform of the Wronskians is 
 

 
 (6.8) 
 
 W n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s − 1 ) = ℓ s − 1 ​ ω n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) . W^{*}_{n,\ell}(s-1)=\frac{\ell}{s-1}\omega_{n,\ell}^{*}(s). 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Given the three-term recurrence of the continuous dual Hahn polynomials [ 48 , Sec. 9, Eq. (9.3.4)] , we need to show that  ω n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) \omega_{n,\ell}^{*}(s)  satisfies 
 

 
 (6.9) 
 
 A n ​ ( n + 1 ) ​ ( n + α + 1 ) ​ ω n + 1 , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) = ( A n + C n − ℓ ⁡ ( ℓ + 1 ) + s ⁡ ( s − 1 ) ) ​ ω n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) − C n ​ ( n + ℓ ) ​ ( n + ℓ + α ) ​ ω n − 1 , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) , \sqrt{A_{n}(n+1)(n+\alpha+1)}\,\omega_{n+1,\ell}^{*}(s)=(A_{n}+C_{n}-\ell(\ell+1)+s(s-1))\omega_{n,\ell}^{*}(s)\\
-\sqrt{C_{n}(n+\ell)(n+\ell+\alpha)}\omega_{n-1,\ell}^{*}(s), 
 
 
 with  A n = ( n + ℓ + 1 ) ​ ( n + ℓ + α + 1 ) A_{n}=(n+\ell+1)(n+\ell+\alpha+1)  and  C n = n ⁡ ( n + α ) C_{n}=n(n+\alpha) .
Using the relations 
 

 
 (6.10) 
 
 ( n + 1 ) ​ ( n + α + 1 ) ​ ϕ n + 1 ​ ( x ) \displaystyle\sqrt{(n+1)(n+\alpha+1)}\phi_{n+1}(x) 
 = ( 2 ​ n + α + 1 − x ) ​ ϕ n ​ ( x ) − n ⁡ ( n + α ) ​ ϕ n − 1 ​ ( x ) , \displaystyle=(2n+\alpha+1-x)\phi_{n}(x)-\sqrt{n(n+\alpha)}\phi_{n-1}(x), 
 
 
 (6.11) 
 
 x ​ ϕ n ′ ​ ( x ) \displaystyle x\phi^{\prime}_{n}(x) 
 = 1 2 ​ ( 2 ​ n + α − x ) ​ ϕ n ​ ( x ) − n ⁡ ( n + α ) ​ ϕ n − 1 ​ ( x ) , \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(2n+\alpha-x)\phi_{n}(x)-\sqrt{n(n+\alpha)}\phi_{n-1}(x), 
 
 
 we have the identity 
 

 
 
 A n ​ ( n + 1 ) ​ ( n + α + 1 ) ​ ω n + 1 , ℓ ​ ( x ) = ( A n + C n − ℓ ⁡ ( ℓ + 1 ) ) ​ ω n , ℓ ​ ( x ) + ( x 2 ​ ω n , ℓ ′ ​ ( x ) ) ′ − C n ​ ( n + ℓ ) ​ ( n + ℓ + α ) ​ ω n − 1 , ℓ ​ ( x ) . \sqrt{A_{n}(n+1)(n+\alpha+1)}\,\omega_{n+1,\ell}(x)=(A_{n}+C_{n}-\ell(\ell+1))\omega_{n,\ell}(x)+\left(x^{2}\omega_{n,\ell}^{\prime}(x)\right)^{\prime}\\
-\sqrt{C_{n}(n+\ell)(n+\ell+\alpha)}\omega_{n-1,\ell}(x). 
 
 
 Taking the Mellin transform of both sides and using ( B.2 )-( B.4 ) we get ( 6.9 ). The initial conditions are 
 

 
 
 ω 0 , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) \displaystyle\omega_{0,\ell}^{*}(s) 
 = ( − 1 ) ℓ ​ Γ ⁡ ( s ) ​ Γ ​ ( s + α ) Γ ⁡ ( s − ℓ ) ​ Γ ⁡ ( ℓ + 1 ) ​ Γ ​ ( α + 1 ) ​ Γ ​ ( ℓ + α + 1 ) , \displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell}\frac{\Gamma(s)\Gamma(s+\alpha)}{\Gamma(s-\ell)\sqrt{\Gamma(\ell+1)\Gamma(\alpha+1)\Gamma(\ell+\alpha+1)}}, 
 
 
 
 ω 1 , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) \displaystyle\omega_{1,\ell}^{*}(s) 
 = ( − 1 ) ℓ ​ Γ ⁡ ( s ) ​ Γ ​ ( s + α ) ​ ( s 2 − s + ( α + 1 ) ​ ( ℓ + 1 ) ) Γ ⁡ ( s − ℓ ) ​ Γ ⁡ ( ℓ + 2 ) ​ Γ ​ ( α + 2 ) ​ Γ ​ ( ℓ + α + 2 ) . \displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell}\frac{\Gamma(s)\Gamma(s+\alpha)\left(s^{2}-s+(\alpha+1)(\ell+1)\right)}{\Gamma(s-\ell)\sqrt{\Gamma(\ell+2)\Gamma(\alpha+2)\Gamma(\ell+\alpha+2)}}. 
 
 
 These complete the proof of ( 6.7 ). Similarly to the Hermite case, Eq. ( 6.8 ) can be proved by using ( 6.10 )-( 6.11 ), and the elementary properties of the Mellin transform ( B.2 )-( B.4 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Using the very same method one can show that the Mellin transform of products and Wronskians of two Jacobi wavefunctions is essentially a Wilson polynomial. The proof follows the same lines as the Hermite and Laguerre cases and is omitted. 
 
 
 Theorem 6.5 . 
 
 Let  ϕ n ​ ( x ) \phi_{n}(x)  be Jacobi wavefunctions ( 6.1 ). Then, 
 
 i)  
 
 the Mellin transform of the products is 
 

 
 
 ω n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) \displaystyle\omega_{n,\ell}^{*}(s) 
 = ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n + 1 ) ​ Γ ​ ( α 1 + α 2 + n + 1 ) n ! ​ Γ ​ ( α 1 + n + 1 ) ​ Γ ​ ( α 2 + n + 1 ) \displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n+1)\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+n+1)}{n!\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+n+1)\Gamma(\alpha_{2}+n+1)}} 
 
 
 
 
 × ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ ( n + ℓ ) + 1 ) ​ Γ ​ ( α 1 + α 2 + n + ℓ + 1 ) ( n + ℓ ) ! ​ Γ ​ ( α 1 + n + ℓ + 1 ) ​ Γ ​ ( α 2 + n + ℓ + 1 ) \displaystyle\small\times\sqrt{\frac{(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2(n+\ell)+1)\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+n+\ell+1)}{(n+\ell)!\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+n+\ell+1)\Gamma(\alpha_{2}+n+\ell+1)}} 
 
 
 
 
 × ( − 1 ) n ​ Γ ​ ( α 1 + n + ℓ + 1 ) ​ Γ ⁡ ( s ) ​ Γ ​ ( α 2 + s ) Γ ⁡ ( s − ℓ ) ​ Γ ​ ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n + ℓ + 1 ) \displaystyle\small\times(-1)^{n}\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+n+\ell+1)\frac{\Gamma(s)\Gamma(\alpha_{2}+s)}{\Gamma(s-\ell)\Gamma(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n+\ell+1)} 
 
 
 (6.12) 
 
 
 × W n ​ ( − ( s − 1 2 ) 2 , ℓ + 1 2 , 1 2 , α 2 + 1 2 , − α 1 − α 2 − 2 ​ n − ℓ − 1 2 ) , \displaystyle\small\times W_{n}\left(-\left(s-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2};\;\ell+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},\alpha_{2}+\frac{1}{2},-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-2n-\ell-\frac{1}{2}\right), 
 
 
 
 ii)  
 
 the difference of Mellin transforms of the Wronskians is 
 

 
 (6.13) 
 
 W n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) − W n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s − 1 ) = ℓ s − 1 ​ ω n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) . W^{*}_{n,\ell}(s)-W^{*}_{n,\ell}(s-1)=\frac{\ell}{s-1}\omega_{n,\ell}^{*}(s). 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 We can also calculate the Mellin transform of a single Jacobi wavefunction in terms of a continuous Hahn polynomial. The interesting case turns out to be for weights with slightly shifted parameters. 
 
 
 Theorem 6.6 . 
 
 Consider the functions 
 

 
 (6.14) 
 
 ϕ ~ n ​ ( x ) = x α 2 − 1 2 ​ ( 1 − x ) α 1 − 1 2 ​ P n ( α 1 , α 2 ) ​ ( x ) ​ χ [ 0 , 1 ] ​ ( x ) \tilde{\phi}_{n}(x)=x^{\frac{\alpha_{2}-1}{2}}(1-x)^{\frac{\alpha_{1}-1}{2}}P^{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})}_{n}(x)\chi_{[0,1]}(x) 
 
 
 Then the Mellin transform can be written in terms of continuous Hahn polynomials: 
 

 
 (6.15) 
 
 ϕ ~ n ∗ ​ ( s ) = Γ ⁡ ( s + α 2 − 1 2 + n ) Γ ⁡ ( s + α 1 + α 2 2 + n ) ​ Γ ​ ( α 1 + 1 2 ) ​ ( − i ) n × p n ( − i ( s − 1 ) , ( α 2 + 1 ) / 2 , − ( α 1 + α 2 ) / 2 − n , ( α 2 + 1 ) / 2 , − ( α 1 + α 2 ) / 2 − n ) . \begin{split}&\tilde{\phi}^{*}_{n}(s)=\frac{\Gamma\left(s+\frac{\alpha_{2}-1}{2}+n\right)}{\Gamma\left(s+\frac{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}{2}+n\right)}\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha_{1}+1}{2}\right)(-i)^{n}\\
&\times p_{n}(-i(s-1),(\alpha_{2}+1)/2,-(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})/2-n,(\alpha_{2}+1)/2,-(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})/2-n).\end{split} 
 
 
 These polynomials have zeros on the vertical line  Re ⁡ ( s ) = 1 \mathrm{Re}(s)=1  and satisfy an orthogonality condition. They are invariant under the reflection  s → 2 − s s\to 2-s  (up to a change of sign if  n n  is odd). 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 To identify the continuous Hahn polynomial, one employs the standard expansion of Jacobi polynomials and calculates the Mellin transform integrating term by term. The result is a hypergeometric sum which can be identified with definition  3.13 , leading to ( 6.15 ). The difficulty in establishing the conclusion is that these polynomials are only known to be orthogonal when the parameters have positive real part. 
 
 
 We proceed by expressing the right-hand side of ( 6.15 ) in terms of Wilson polynomials and apply a result of Neretin  [ 67 ] . We claim that for generic parameters  a , b ∈ ℂ a,b\in\mathbb{C} , we have 
 

 
 (6.16) 
 
 p 2 ​ n ​ ( x , a , b , a , b ) \displaystyle p_{2n}(x,a,b,a,b) 
 ∝ W n ​ ( x 2 , 0 , 1 / 2 , a , b ) \displaystyle\propto W_{n}(x^{2},0,1/2,a,b) 
 
 
 (6.17) 
 
 p 2 ​ n + 1 ​ ( x , a , b , a , b ) \displaystyle p_{2n+1}(x,a,b,a,b) 
 ∝ x ​ W n ​ ( x 2 , 1 , 1 / 2 , a , b ) . \displaystyle\propto xW_{n}(x^{2},1,1/2,a,b). 
 
 
 up to a constant independent of  x x . If  a a  and  b b  have positive real part, this follows by writing the orthogonality condition for the Wilson polynomials with the parameters given in ( 6.16 ) or ( 6.17 ). Use of the duplication formula for the Gamma function shows that the weight reduces to  | Γ ⁡ ( a + i ​ x ) | 2 ​ | Γ ⁡ ( b + i ​ x ) | 2 |\Gamma(a+ix)|^{2}\,|\Gamma(b+ix)|^{2}  which is the weight function for continuous Hahn polynomials. If  a a  or  b b  have negative real part the identity follows by analytic continuation. Now inserting ( 6.16 ) and ( 6.17 ) into ( 6.15 ) and applying the orthogonality ( C.2 ) completes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Going back to random matrices, we obtain exact ordinary differential equations for the one-point functions of the classical ensembles.
Denote by  ρ n ( 2 ) ​ ( x ) \rho_{n}^{(2)}(x)  the one-point function of the ensembles GUE, LUE, or JUE. As already discussed in the Introducion,  ρ n ( 2 ) ​ ( x ) \rho^{(2)}_{n}(x)  can be represented as the Wronskian of two adjacent wavefunctions. 
 
 
 The following proposition is a corollary of the previous theorems on Mellin transforms of Wronskians. For GUE and LUE we recover a result obtained and used earlier by Götze and Tikhomirov  [ 42 , Lemma 2.1 and Lemma 3.1] . For the JUE a similar result does not seem to have been published. 
 
 
 Proposition 6.7 . 
 
 The one-point correlation function  ρ n ( 2 ) ​ ( x ) \rho_{n}^{(2)}(x)  satisfies the differential equation  D ​ ρ n ( 2 ) ​ ( x ) = 0 D\rho_{n}^{(2)}(x)=0 , where 
 

 
 
 D ​ y = { y ′′′ + ( 4 ​ n − x 2 ) ​ y ′ + x ​ y ( GUE ) x 2 ​ y ′′′ + 4 ​ x ​ y ′′ + ( x − a ) ​ ( b − x ) ​ y ′ + ( 1 + b 2 − α 2 x ) ​ y , ( LUE ) x 2 ​ ( ( 1 − x ) 3 ​ y ) ′′′′ + x ​ ( ( 4 + x ) ​ ( 1 − x ) 2 ​ y ) ′′ + ( 1 − x ) ​ A ​ ( x ) ​ y ′ + B ⁡ ( x ) ​ y , ( JUE ) \displaystyle Dy=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad}cr}\displaystyle y^{\prime\prime\prime}+(4n-x^{2})y^{\prime}+xy&(\mathrm{GUE})\\
\\
\displaystyle x^{2}y^{\prime\prime\prime}+4xy^{\prime\prime}+(x-a)(b-x)y^{\prime}+\left(\frac{1+b}{2}-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{x}\right)y,&(\mathrm{LUE})\\
\\
\displaystyle x^{2}((1-x)^{3}y)^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}+x((4+x)(1-x)^{2}y)^{\prime\prime}+(1-x)A(x)y^{\prime}+B(x)y,&(\mathrm{JUE})\end{array}\right. 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 
 a = m + n − 4 ​ m ​ n + 2 , b = m + n + 4 ​ m ​ n + 2 , a=m+n-\sqrt{4mn+2},\quad b=m+n+\sqrt{4mn+2}, 
 
 
 and 
 

 
 
 A ⁡ ( x ) = − ( ( α 1 + α 2 ) 2 − 2 ) ​ x 2 − 4 ​ n ​ x ​ ( x − 1 ) ​ ( α 1 + α 2 + n ) + 2 ​ α 2 ​ x ​ ( α 1 + α 2 ) − α 2 2 + 2 , A(x)=-\left((\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})^{2}-2\right)x^{2}-4nx(x-1)(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+n)+2\alpha_{2}x(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})-\alpha_{2}^{2}+2, 
 
 
 

 
 
 B ⁡ ( x ) = x 2 ​ ( ( α 1 + 2 ​ n ) 2 − 4 ) − 6 ​ n ​ x ​ ( α 1 + n ) + 2 ​ x − 2 + α 2 ​ ( 2 ​ x − 1 ) ​ ( x − 1 ) ​ ( α 1 + 2 ​ n ) + 2 ​ n ​ ( α 1 + n ) − α 2 2 x ​ ( 1 − x ) 3 . B(x)=x^{2}((\alpha_{1}+2n)^{2}-4)-6nx(\alpha_{1}+n)+2x-2\\
+\alpha_{2}(2x-1)(x-1)(\alpha_{1}+2n)+2n(\alpha_{1}+n)-\frac{\alpha_{2}^{2}}{x}(1-x)^{3}. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We present the proof for the GUE.
The one-point function  ρ n ( 2 ) ​ ( x ) \rho^{(2)}_{n}(x)  of the GUE is proportional to the Wronskian of consecutive Hermite functions  W n − 1 , 1 ​ ( x / 2 ) = Wr ⁡ ( ϕ n − 1 ​ ( x / 2 ) , ϕ n ​ ( x / 2 ) ) W_{n-1,1}(x/\sqrt{2})=\operatorname{Wr}(\phi_{n-1}(x/\sqrt{2}),\phi_{n}(x/\sqrt{2})) . To prove the Theorem for the GUE it is therefore sufficient to show that the function  W n , 1 ​ ( x ) W_{n,1}(x)  satisfies the third-order differential equation 
 

 
 
 W n , 1 ′′′ ​ ( x ) + 4 ​ ( 2 ​ ( n + 1 ) − x 2 ) ​ W n , 1 ′ ​ ( x ) + 4 ​ x ​ W n , 1 ′′′ ​ ( x ) = 0 . W_{n,1}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)+4(2(n+1)-x^{2})W_{n,1}^{\prime}(x)+4xW_{n,1}^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)=0. 
 
 
 The Harer-Zagier recurrence relation ( 4.5 ) for the moments of the GUE is in fact a difference equation for the Mellin transform  W n , 1 ∗ ​ ( s ) W_{n,1}^{*}(s) 
 

 
 (6.18) 
 
 s ⁡ ( s + 1 ) ​ ( s + 2 ) ​ W n , 1 ∗ ​ ( s ) + 8 ​ ( s + 2 ) ​ ( n + 1 ) ​ W n , 1 ∗ ​ ( s + 2 ) − 4 ​ ( s + 5 ) ​ W n , 1 ∗ ​ ( s + 4 ) = 0 , s(s+1)(s+2)W_{n,1}^{*}(s)+8(s+2)(n+1)W_{n,1}^{*}(s+2)-4(s+5)W_{n,1}^{*}(s+4)=0, 
 
 
 Using the properties ( B.2 )-( B.4 ) of the Mellin transform we get the claim. 
 
 
 For the LUE and JUE the proof follows the same steps starting from the recurrence relations ( 4.15 ) and ( 4.20 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark . 
 
 It is natural to ask whether Wronskians of nonadjacent wavefunctions satisfy similar differential equations. The Mellin transforms of those Wronskians are essentially hypergeometric OP’s (see Theorems  6.2 ,  6.4 , and  6.5 ). Hence, they satisfy a discrete Sturm-Liouville problem. Upon inversion of the Mellin transform this discrete problem correspond to a differential equation. 
 
 
 We discuss, for concreteness the case of Wronskian of Hermite wavefunctions  W n , ℓ ​ ( x ) = Wr ⁡ ( ϕ n ​ ( x ) , ϕ n + ℓ ​ ( x ) ) W_{n,\ell}(x)=\operatorname{Wr}(\phi_{n}(x),\phi_{n+\ell}(x)) . Its Mellin transform is given in ( 6.2 )-( 6.3 ). From the difference equation of Meixner-Pollaczek polynomials 
 

 
 (6.19) 
 
 ( ℓ − s − 2 ) ​ P n ( ℓ + 1 2 ) ​ ( − i ⁡ ( s + 1 ) 2 , π 2 ) − 2 ​ ( 2 ​ n + s + 1 ) ​ P n ( ℓ + 1 2 ) ​ ( − i ⁡ ( s + 3 ) 2 , π 2 ) + ( ℓ + s + 4 ) ​ P n ( ℓ + 1 2 ) ​ ( − i ⁡ ( s + 5 ) 2 , π 2 ) = 0 , (\ell-s-2)P_{n}^{\left(\frac{\ell+1}{2}\right)}\left(-\frac{i(s+1)}{2};\frac{\pi}{2}\right)-2(2n+s+1)P_{n}^{\left(\frac{\ell+1}{2}\right)}\left(-\frac{i(s+3)}{2};\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\\
+(\ell+s+4)P_{n}^{\left(\frac{\ell+1}{2}\right)}\left(-\frac{i(s+5)}{2};\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=0, 
 
 
 the Mellin transform of the Wronskian satisfies the difference equation 
 

 
 (6.20) 
 
 s ⁡ ( s + 1 ) ​ ( s + 2 ) ​ ( s + 3 ) ​ ( ℓ − s − 2 ) ​ W n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) − 4 ​ ( s + 2 ) ​ ( s + 3 ) ​ ( 2 ​ n + ℓ + 1 ) ​ ( s − ℓ + 2 ) ​ W n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s + 2 ) + 4 ​ ( ( s + 4 ) 2 − ℓ 2 ) ​ ( s − ℓ + 2 ) ​ W n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s + 4 ) = 0 . s(s+1)(s+2)(s+3)(\ell-s-2)W_{n,\ell}^{*}(s)-4(s+2)(s+3)(2n+\ell+1)(s-\ell+2)W_{n,\ell}^{*}(s+2)\\
+4((s+4)^{2}-\ell^{2})(s-\ell+2)W_{n,\ell}^{*}(s+4)=0. 
 
 
 This implies that, for generic  n n  and  ℓ \ell ,  W n , ℓ ​ ( x ) W_{n,\ell}(x)  satisfies a fifth-order differential equation. When when  ℓ = 1 \ell=1 , formula ( 6.20 ) simplifies as ( 6.18 ),
which corresponds to the third-order equation of Proposition  6.7 . 
 
 
 
 
 6.3.  Convolution of hypergeometric OP’s 

 
 If  ϕ n ​ ( x ) \phi_{n}(x)  are the Hermite, Laguerre, or Jacobi functions and  ω n , ℓ ​ ( x ) = ϕ n ​ ( x ) ​ ϕ n + ℓ ​ ( x ) \omega_{n,\ell}(x)=\phi_{n}(x)\phi_{n+\ell}(x) , by the convolution property ( B.5 ) of the Mellin transform, we have 
 

 
 (6.21) 
 
 1 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ∫ c − i ​ ∞ c + i ​ ∞ ϕ n ∗ ​ ( s − u ) ​ ϕ n + ℓ ∗ ​ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u = ω n , ℓ ∗ ​ ( s ) , \frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}\phi_{n}^{*}(s-u)\phi_{n+\ell}^{*}(u)du=\omega_{n,\ell}^{*}(s), 
 
 
 with  c c  in the fundamental strip of convergence of the Mellin transform. Note that  ϕ n ​ ( x ) \phi_{n}(x)  is in  L 2 ​ ( ℝ + ) L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})  so that the fundamental strip always contains the line  1 2 + i ​ ℝ \frac{1}{2}+i\mathbb{R} . Given that  ϕ n ∗ \phi_{n}^{*} ,  ϕ n + ℓ ∗ \phi_{n+\ell}^{*}  and  ω n , ℓ ∗ \omega_{n,\ell}^{*}  have hypergeometric OP’s factors, the above formula is a ‘convolution formula’ for hypergeoemetric OP’s. Note that this is different from the usual (discrete) convolutions formulas of orthogonal polynomials. 
 
 
 When  ϕ n ​ ( x ) \phi_{n}(x)  is a Hermite wavefunction,  ϕ n ∗ ​ ( s ) \phi_{n}^{*}(s)  has a Meixner-Pollaczeck polynomial factor whose parameter depends on the parity of  n n . Using the explicit expressions in Proposition  6.1  and Theorem  6.2  we can write the special cases of ( 6.21 ): 
 

 
 (6.22) 
 
 1 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ∫ 1 2 − i ​ ∞ 1 2 + i ​ ∞ Γ ⁡ ( s − u 2 ) ​ Γ ​ ( u 2 ) ​ P m ( 1 4 ) ​ ( s − u − 1 / 2 2 ​ i , π 2 ) ​ P n ( 1 4 ) ​ ( u − 1 / 2 2 ​ i , π 2 ) ​ 𝑑 u = 2 ℓ − m − n − 3 2 ​ s + 2 ​ i 2 ​ r − m − n ​ π ​ ( 2 ​ r ) ! m ! ​ n ! ​ Γ ⁡ ( s ) Γ ⁡ ( s + 1 2 − ℓ ) ​ P 2 ​ r ( ℓ + 1 2 ) ​ ( s 2 ​ i , π 2 ) \frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\frac{1}{2}-i\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}+i\infty}\Gamma\left(\frac{s-u}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{u}{2}\right)P^{\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}_{m}\left(\frac{s-u-1/2}{2i};\;\frac{\pi}{2}\right)P^{\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}_{n}\left(\frac{u-1/2}{2i};\;\frac{\pi}{2}\right)du\\
=2^{\ell-m-n-\frac{3}{2}s+2}i^{2r-m-n}\frac{\sqrt{\pi}(2r)!}{m!n!}\frac{\Gamma(s)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{s+1}{2}-\ell\right)}\,P_{2r}^{\left(\ell+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\left(\frac{s}{2i};\frac{\pi}{2}\right) 
 
 
 

 
 (6.23) 
 
 1 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ∫ 1 2 − i ​ ∞ 1 2 + i ​ ∞ Γ ⁡ ( s − u + 1 2 ) ​ Γ ​ ( u + 1 2 ) ​ P m ( 3 4 ) ​ ( s − u − 1 / 2 2 ​ i , π 2 ) ​ P n ( 3 4 ) ​ ( u − 1 / 2 2 ​ i , π 2 ) ​ 𝑑 u = 2 ℓ − m − n − 3 2 ​ s ​ i 2 ​ r − m − n + 1 ​ π ​ ( 2 ​ r + 1 ) ! m ! ​ n ! ​ Γ ⁡ ( s ) Γ ⁡ ( s + 1 2 − ℓ ) ​ P 2 ​ r + 1 ( ℓ + 1 2 ) ​ ( s 2 ​ i , π 2 ) \frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\frac{1}{2}-i\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}+i\infty}\Gamma\left(\frac{s-u+1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{u+1}{2}\right)P^{\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)}_{m}\left(\frac{s-u-1/2}{2i};\;\frac{\pi}{2}\right)P^{\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)}_{n}\left(\frac{u-1/2}{2i};\;\frac{\pi}{2}\right)du\\
=2^{\ell-m-n-\frac{3}{2}s}i^{2r-m-n+1}\frac{\sqrt{\pi}(2r+1)!}{m!n!}\frac{\Gamma(s)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{s+1}{2}-\ell\right)}\,P_{2r+1}^{\left(\ell+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\left(\frac{s}{2i};\frac{\pi}{2}\right) 
 
 
 

 
 (6.24) 
 
 1 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ∫ 1 2 − i ​ ∞ 1 2 + i ​ ∞ Γ ⁡ ( s − u + 1 2 ) ​ Γ ​ ( u 2 ) ​ P m ( 3 4 ) ​ ( s − u − 1 / 2 2 ​ i , π 2 ) ​ P n ( 1 4 ) ​ ( u − 1 / 2 2 ​ i , π 2 ) ​ 𝑑 u = 2 ℓ − m − n − 3 2 ​ s + 3 2 ​ i 2 ​ r − m − n ​ π ​ ( 2 ​ r ) ! m ! ​ n ! ​ Γ ⁡ ( s ) Γ ⁡ ( s 2 − ℓ ) ​ P 2 ​ r ( ℓ + 1 ) ​ ( s 2 ​ i , π 2 ) \frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\frac{1}{2}-i\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}+i\infty}\Gamma\left(\frac{s-u+1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{u}{2}\right)P^{\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)}_{m}\left(\frac{s-u-1/2}{2i};\;\frac{\pi}{2}\right)P^{\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}_{n}\left(\frac{u-1/2}{2i};\;\frac{\pi}{2}\right)du\\
=2^{\ell-m-n-\frac{3}{2}s+\frac{3}{2}}i^{2r-m-n}\frac{\sqrt{\pi}(2r)!}{m!n!}\frac{\Gamma(s)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{s}{2}-\ell\right)}\,P_{2r}^{\left(\ell+1\right)}\left(\frac{s}{2i};\frac{\pi}{2}\right) 
 
 
 with  r = min ⁡ ( m , n ) r=\min(m,n)  and  ℓ = | m − n | \ell=|m-n| . 
 
 
 In a similar way, from Proposition  6.3  and Theorem  6.4 , the convolution property ( 6.21 ) gives the identity 
 

 
 (6.25) 
 
 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ 1 2 − i ​ ∞ 1 2 + i ​ ∞ Γ ⁡ ( s − u + α 2 ) ​ Γ ​ ( u + α 2 ) ​ P m ( α + 1 2 ) ​ ( s − u − 1 / 2 i , π 2 ) ​ P n ( α + 1 2 ) ​ ( s − 1 / 2 i , π 2 ) ​ 𝑑 u = ( − 1 ) m ​ n + ℓ m ! ​ n ! ​ Γ ⁡ ( s ) ​ Γ ​ ( s + α ) 2 s + α ​ Γ ​ ( s − ℓ ) ​ S r ​ ( − ( s − 1 2 ) 2 , ℓ + 1 2 , 1 2 , α + 1 2 ) \frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{\frac{1}{2}-i\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}+i\infty}\Gamma\left(s-u+\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(u+\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)P^{\left(\frac{\alpha+1}{2}\right)}_{m}\left(\frac{s-u-1/2}{i};\;\frac{\pi}{2}\right)P^{\left(\frac{\alpha+1}{2}\right)}_{n}\left(\frac{s-1/2}{i};\;\frac{\pi}{2}\right)du\\
=\frac{(-1)^{mn+\ell}}{m!n!}\frac{\Gamma(s)\Gamma(s+\alpha)}{2^{s+\alpha}\Gamma(s-\ell)}S_{r}\left(-\left(s-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2};\ell+\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right) 
 
 
 with  r = min ⁡ ( m , n ) r=\min(m,n)  and  ℓ = | m − n | \ell=|m-n| . 
 
 
 
 
 7.  Higher order cumulants 

 
 It is tempting to look for reciprocity formulae for cumulants of higher order (covariances, etc.).
Write the moments as  Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k} , second order moments as  Q k , l ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k ​ Tr ​ X n l Q_{k,l}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{l} ,
and covariances as  C k , l ℂ = Q k , l ℂ ​ ( m , n ) − Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) ​ Q l ℂ ​ ( m , n ) C_{k,l}^{\mathbb{C}}=Q_{k,l}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)-Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)Q_{l}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n) . 
 
 
 Positive and negative moments of LUE matrices satisfy recursion relations known as  loop equations . The following lemma can be proved using standard methods in random matrix theory (see, e.g.  [ 33 ]  for similar loop equations for positive moments of the GUE). 
 
 
 Lemma 7.1   (Loop equations for positive and negative moments of LUE) . 
 
 For  k 1 , … , k v ∈ ℕ k_{1},\dots,k_{v}\in\mathbb{N} , the positive and negative moments satisfy the relations (loop equations): 
 

 
 (7.1) 
 
 ∑ ℓ = 0 k 1 − 1 𝔼 Tr X n ℓ Tr X n k 1 − ℓ + 1 Tr X n k 2 ⋯ Tr X n k v + ∑ j = 2 v k j 𝔼 Tr X n k 1 + k j − 1 ∏ i = 2 i ≠ j v Tr X n k i = 𝔼 Tr X n k 1 Tr X n k 2 ⋯ Tr X n k v − α 𝔼 Tr X n k 1 − 1 Tr X n k 2 ⋯ Tr X n k v , \sum_{\ell=0}^{k_{1}-1}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{\ell}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k_{1}-\ell+1}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k_{2}}\cdots\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k_{v}}+\sum_{j=2}^{v}k_{j}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k_{1}+k_{j}-1}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=2\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{v}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k_{i}}\\
=\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k_{1}}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k_{2}}\cdots\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k_{v}}-\alpha\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k_{1}-1}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k_{2}}\cdots\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k_{v}}, 
 
 
 

 
 (7.2) 
 
 ∑ ℓ = 0 k 1 − 1 𝔼 Tr X n − ℓ − 1 Tr X n − k 1 + ℓ Tr X n − k 2 ⋯ Tr X n − k v + ∑ j = 2 v k j 𝔼 Tr X n − k 1 − k j − 1 ∏ i = 2 i ≠ j v Tr X n − k i = − 𝔼 Tr X n − k 1 Tr X n − k 2 ⋯ Tr X n − k v + α 𝔼 Tr X n − k 1 − 1 Tr X n − k 2 ⋯ Tr X n − k v , \sum_{\ell=0}^{k_{1}-1}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-\ell-1}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-k_{1}+\ell}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-k_{2}}\cdots\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-k_{v}}+\sum_{j=2}^{v}k_{j}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-k_{1}-k_{j}-1}\!\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=2\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{v}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-k_{i}}\\
=-\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-k_{1}}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-k_{2}}\cdots\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-k_{v}}+\alpha\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-k_{1}-1}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-k_{2}}\cdots\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{-k_{v}}, 
 
 
 provided they exist. 
 
 
 
 Proposition 7.2   (Reflection symmetry for LUE covariances) . 
 
 

 
 (7.3) 
 
 C − k , − 1 ℂ = C k , 1 ℂ α 2 ( α 2 − 1 ) ⋯ ( α 2 − k 2 ) C_{-k,-1}^{\mathbb{C}}=\frac{C_{k,1}^{\mathbb{C}}}{\alpha^{2}(\alpha^{2}-1)\cdots(\alpha^{2}-k^{2})} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Note that  Q 0 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = n Q_{0}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=n ,  Q 1 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = m ​ n Q_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=mn  and  Q − 1 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = n / α Q_{-1}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=n/\alpha .
The proof of ( 7.3 ) uses the loop equations ( 7.1 )-( 7.2 ) together with the reciprocity law ( 2.3 ) for moments, as follows.
By the loop equations, 
 

 
 
 Q k , 1 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = m ​ Q k , 0 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) + k ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = Q 1 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) + k ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) Q_{k,1}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=mQ_{k,0}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)+kQ_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=Q_{1}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)+kQ_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n) 
 
 
 and 
 

 
 
 α ​ Q − k , − 1 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = Q − k , 0 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) + k ​ Q − k − 1 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = m ​ Q − k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) + k ​ Q − k − 1 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) . \alpha Q_{-k,-1}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=Q_{-k,0}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)+kQ_{-k-1}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=mQ_{-k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)+kQ_{-k-1}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n). 
 
 
 The first gives 
 

 
 
 C k , 1 ℂ = k ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) . C_{k,1}^{\mathbb{C}}=kQ_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n). 
 
 
 Using  Q − 1 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = n / α Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{-1}(m,n)=n/\alpha , the second gives 
 

 
 
 Q − k , − 1 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) = Q − 1 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) ​ Q − k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) + k ​ Q − k − 1 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) / α , Q_{-k,-1}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=Q_{-1}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)Q_{-k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)+kQ_{-k-1}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)/\alpha, 
 
 
 that is  C − k , − 1 ℂ = k ​ Q − k − 1 ℂ ​ ( m , n ) / α C_{-k,-1}^{\mathbb{C}}=kQ_{-k-1}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)/\alpha .
Now ( 2.3 ) gives 
 

 
 
 C − k , − 1 ℂ = k ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( m , n ) α 2 ( α 2 − 1 ) ⋯ ( α 2 − k 2 ) = C k , 1 ℂ α 2 ( α 2 − 1 ) ⋯ ( α 2 − k 2 ) C_{-k,-1}^{\mathbb{C}}=\frac{kQ_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)}{\alpha^{2}(\alpha^{2}-1)\cdots(\alpha^{2}-k^{2})}=\frac{C_{k,1}^{\mathbb{C}}}{\alpha^{2}(\alpha^{2}-1)\cdots(\alpha^{2}-k^{2})} 
 
 
 as required.
∎ 
 
 
 
 There exists also a precise reflection symmetry for the covariances of one-cut  β \beta -ensembles at leading order in  n n . Suppose that the eigenvalues  x 1 , … , x n x_{1},\dots,x_{n}  of  X n X_{n}  have a joint probability density proportional to 
 

 
 (7.4) 
 
 ∏ 1 ≤ j < k ≤ n | x k − x j | β ​ ∏ i = 1 n e − n ​ β ​ V ​ ( x i ) ​ d ​ x i ( β > 0 ) \prod_{1\leq j<k\leq n}|x_{k}-x_{j}|^{\beta}\prod_{i=1}^{n}e^{-n\beta V(x_{i})}dx_{i}\quad(\beta>0) 
 
 
 on the real line. Special cases of one-cut  β \beta -ensembles are the Laguerre and Jacobi ensembles defined by the weights ( 3.2 ) with  α = ( c − 1 ) ​ n \alpha=(c-1)n  and  α 1 , 2 = ( c 1 , 2 − 1 ) ​ n \alpha_{1,2}=(c_{1,2}-1)n , with  c , c 1 , c 2 > 0 c,c_{1},c_{2}>0  (see discussion in Section  5 ).
Denote the covariances by 
 

 
 (7.5) 
 
 C k , l = 𝔼 ​ Tr ​ X n k ​ Tr ​ X n l − 𝔼 ​ Tr ​ X n k ​ 𝔼 ​ Tr ​ X n l . C_{k,l}=\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{l}-\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{l}. 
 
 
 The two-point connected correlator 
 

 
 (7.6) 
 
 G 2 ​ ( z , w ) = 𝔼 ​ Tr ​ 1 z − X n ​ Tr ​ 1 w − X n − 𝔼 ​ Tr ​ 1 z − X n ​ 𝔼 ​ Tr ​ 1 w − X n . G_{2}(z,w)=\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}\frac{1}{z-X_{n}}\operatorname{Tr}\frac{1}{w-X_{n}}-\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}\frac{1}{z-X_{n}}\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}\frac{1}{w-X_{n}}. 
 
 
 is the generating function of covariances of positive and negative moments 
 

 
 (7.7) 
 
 G 2 ​ ( z , w ) \displaystyle G_{2}(z,w) 
 = ∑ k , l = 0 ∞ C k , l ​ z − ( k + 1 ) ​ w − ( l + 1 ) \displaystyle=\sum_{k,l=0}^{\infty}C_{k,l}z^{-(k+1)}w^{-(l+1)} 
 
 as  z , w → ∞ z,w\to\infty 
 
 
 (7.8) 
 
 
 = ∑ k , l = 0 ∞ C − ( k + 1 ) , − ( l + 1 ) ​ z k ​ w l \displaystyle=\sum_{k,l=0}^{\infty}C_{-(k+1),-(l+1)}z^{k}w^{l} 
 
 as  z , w → 0 . \displaystyle\text{as $z,w\to 0$}. 
 
 
 
 
 For one-cut  β \beta -ensembles the large  n n  limit of  G 2 ​ ( z , w ) G_{2}(z,w)  exists and depends only on the edges of the cut  [ 6 ,  11 ,  13 ,  26 ,  27 ] , see Eq. ( 7.13 ) below. (On the other hand, for multicut ensembles the asymptotics of  G 2 ​ ( z , w ) G_{2}(z,w)  is more delicate due to the presence of oscillating terms  [ 2 ,  12 ] ). In the Laguerre ensemble set  α = m − n = ( c − 1 ) ​ n \alpha=m-n=(c-1)n , with  c > 1 c>1 . The edges  x ± x_{\pm}  of the cut are strictly positive, see ( 5.4 ). In the Jacobi ensemble set  α 1 , 2 = ( c 1 , 2 − 1 ) \alpha_{1,2}=(c_{1,2}-1) , with  c 2 > 1 c_{2}>1 , so that the cut  [ x − , x + ] [x_{-},x_{+}]  is contained in the interval  ( 0 , 1 ] (0,1] , see ( 5.8 ). 
 
 
 Theorem 7.3   (Covariances of Laguerre and Jacobi ensembles at leading order in  n n ) . 
 
 Let  X n X_{n}  be in the Laguerre (resp. Jacobi) ensemble with  c > 1 c>1  (resp.  c 2 > 1 c_{2}>1 ). Denote the edges of the cut by  0 < x − < x + 0<x_{-}<x_{+} . Then, for all  β > 0 \beta>0 , 
 

 
 (7.9) 
 
 lim n → ∞ C − k , − l = ( 1 x − ​ x + ) k + l ​ lim n → ∞ C k , l . \lim_{n\to\infty}C_{-k,-l}=\left(\frac{1}{x_{-}x_{+}}\right)^{k+l}\lim_{n\to\infty}C_{k,l}. 
 
 
 More explicitly: 
 

 
 (7.10) 
 
 lim n → ∞ C − k , − l \displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}C_{-k,-l} 
 = ( 1 c − 1 ) 2 ​ ( k + l ) ​ lim n → ∞ C k , l \displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{c-1}\right)^{2(k+l)}\lim_{n\to\infty}C_{k,l} 
 (Laguerre) 
 
 
 (7.11) 
 
 lim n → ∞ C − k , − l \displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}C_{-k,-l} 
 = ( c 1 + c 2 c 2 − 1 ) 2 ​ ( k + l ) ​ lim n → ∞ C k , l \displaystyle=\left(\frac{c_{1}+c_{2}}{c_{2}-1}\right)^{2(k+l)}\lim_{n\to\infty}C_{k,l} 
 (Jacobi) . \displaystyle\text{(Jacobi)}. 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By the one-cut property, the limit 
 

 
 (7.12) 
 
 G 2 , 0 ​ ( z , w ) = lim n → ∞ G ⁡ ( z , w ) , G_{2,0}(z,w)=\lim_{n\to\infty}G(z,w), 
 
 
 is given by the explicit formula 
 

 
 (7.13) 
 
 G 2 , 0 ​ ( z , w ) = 1 β ​ 1 ( z − w ) 2 ​ ( z ​ w − ( x − + x + ) ​ ( z + w ) / 2 + x − ​ x + ( z − x − ) ​ ( z − x + ) ​ ( w − x − ) ​ ( w − x + ) − 1 ) . G_{2,0}(z,w)=\frac{1}{\beta}\frac{1}{(z-w)^{2}}\left(\frac{zw-(x_{-}+x_{+})(z+w)/2+x_{-}x_{+}}{\sqrt{(z-x_{-})(z-x_{+})(w-x_{-})(w-x_{+})}}-1\right). 
 
 
 Moreover, since the cut does not contain zero, the negative covariances  lim n → ∞ C − k , − l \lim_{n\to\infty}C_{-k,-l}  exist.
From ( 7.13 ) it is easy to verify the following functional equation 
 

 
 (7.14) 
 
 G 2 , 0 ​ ( x − ​ x + z , x − ​ x + w ) = ( x − ​ x + z ​ w ) 2 ​ G 2 , 0 ​ ( z , w ) . G_{2,0}\left(\frac{x_{-}x_{+}}{z},\frac{x_{-}x_{+}}{w}\right)=\left(\frac{x_{-}x_{+}}{zw}\right)^{2}G_{2,0}(z,w). 
 
 
 Using ( 7.7 )-( 7.8 ), the claim follows.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 8.  Orthogonal and Symplectic ensembles 

 
 We will now discuss analogous results for the orthogonal and symplectic ensembles of random matrices, corresponding to averages over the density ( 3.1 ) with  β = 1 \beta=1  or  β = 4 \beta=4  respectively. Our aim will be to isolate the polynomial factors of the moments (Mellin transforms), again for the Gaussian, Laguerre and Jacobi ensembles. These ensembles are characterized by their joint eigenvalue distribution as in ( 3.1 ) with corresponding weight functions ( 3.2 ).
We will consider various expectation values of power of traces
with respect to ( 3.1 ) with  β = 1 \beta=1  and  4 4 .
We will use the shorthand GSE and GOE to mean Gaussian Symplectic Ensemble, GSE ( β = 4 \beta=4 ), Gaussian Orthogonal Ensemble, GOE ( β = 1 \beta=1 ) and similarly for the Laguerre (LSE / LOE) and Jacobi (JSE / JOE) cases. As in the complex case, we will denote by  α = m − n \alpha=m-n  in the Laguerre case, and  α 1 = m 1 − n \alpha_{1}=m_{1}-n  and  α 2 = m 2 − n \alpha_{2}=m_{2}-n  in the Jacobi case, which we treat as fixed  n n -independent parameters. 
 
 
 Moments of real and quaternionic Gaussian ensembles have already received some attention in the literature, however much less is known compared with the complex case  β = 2 \beta=2 . One of the first explicit formulas was derived by Goulden and Jackson  [ 40 ]  who were motivated by the fact that, for  β = 1 \beta=1 , moments of Gaussian matrices describe the genus expansion of  non-orientable  surfaces. An important development was achieved in the work of Ledoux  [ 53 ]  who discovered recursion relations for moments of the GOE and GSE (which can be viewed as real and quaternionic analogues of the Harer-Zagier recurrence relations). This was extended to the Laguerre ensemble in  [ 27 ] . Results holding for complex moments were obtained in  [ 62 ]  . 
 
 
 8.1.  Recurrence relations and hypergeometric representations 

 
 We define 
 

 
 
 Q k ℝ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(n) 
 = 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n 2 ​ k \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{2k} 
 if  X n ∼ GOE X_{n}\sim\mathrm{GOE} 
 
 
 
 Q k ℍ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(n) 
 = 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n 2 ​ k \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{2k} 
 if  X n ∼ GSE X_{n}\sim\mathrm{GSE} 
 
 
 
 Q k ℝ ​ ( m , n ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(m,n) 
 = 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k} 
 if  X n ∼ LOE X_{n}\sim\mathrm{LOE} 
 
 
 
 Q k ℍ ​ ( m , n ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(m,n) 
 = 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k} 
 if  X n ∼ LSE X_{n}\sim\mathrm{LSE} 
 
 
 
 Q k ℝ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k} 
 if  X n ∼ JOE X_{n}\sim\mathrm{JOE} 
 
 
 
 Q k ℍ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = 𝔼 ​ Tr ⁡ X n k \displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}X_{n}^{k} 
 if  X n ∼ JSE X_{n}\sim\mathrm{JSE} 
 
 
 
 Δ ​ Q k ℝ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle\Delta Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = Q k ℝ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) − Q k + 1 ℝ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle=Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)-Q_{k+1}^{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 
 
 
 Δ ​ Q k ℍ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle\Delta Q_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = Q k ℍ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) − Q k + 1 ℍ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle=Q_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)-Q_{k+1}^{\mathbb{H}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 
 
 for all  k ∈ ℂ k\in\mathbb{C}  for which the expectations exist.
Moments of the classical orthogonal ensembles satisfy recursions similar to those of the unitary ensembles. To our knowledge this was first noticed by Ledoux for the GOE  [ 53 ]  and extended to the LOE in  [ 28 ] .
The first question is whether moments of orthogonal / symplectic ensembles enjoy reflection symmetries and have orthogonal polynomial factors as in the unitary case. This is not the case as can be ascertained from the following observation. The Harer-Zagier recursion for moments  ℚ k ℂ ​ ( n ) \mathbb{Q}^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(n)  of the GUE is a three terms recursion in  k k  which can be interpreted as the discrete S-L problem of some families of hypergeometric (Meixner / Meixner-Pollaczek) polynomials.
Moments of the classical orthogonal ensembles satisfy recursion formulae too.
For the GOE, Ledoux  [ 53 ]  discovered that  Q k ℝ ​ ( n ) Q^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}(n)  satisfy a five term recurrence relation which cannot be interpreted as a
S-L equation (a second order difference equation). In fact, Ledoux also found an alternative inhomogeneous recursion formula for  Q k ℝ ​ ( n ) Q^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}(n)  coupled with the moments of the GUE that is somewhat more convenient for some application. An analogue of this coupled recursion was obtained later for the LOE  [ 28 ] . 
 
 
 These results suggest that  suitable combinations of moments, rather than the moments themselves, have nice hypergeometric polynomial factors  similar to the unitary cases. 
 
 
 Moments of the symplectic ensembles can be analysed similarly given the duality relations between moments of GSE, LSE and JSE of size  n n  and the (formal) moments of the GOE, LOE and JSE of size  − 2 ​ n -2n 
 

 
 (8.1) 
 
 Q k ℍ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(n) 
 = ( − 1 ) k + 1 ​ 2 − 1 ​ Q k ℝ ​ ( − 2 ​ n ) \displaystyle=(-1)^{k+1}2^{-1}Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(-2n) 
 
 
 (8.2) 
 
 Q k ℍ ​ ( m , n ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(m,n) 
 = ( − 1 ) k + 1 ​ 2 − 1 ​ Q k ℝ ​ ( − 2 ​ m , − 2 ​ n ) \displaystyle=(-1)^{k+1}2^{-1}Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(-2m,-2n) 
 
 
 (8.3) 
 
 Q k ℍ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = − 2 − 1 ​ Q k ℝ ​ ( − 2 ​ α 1 , − 2 ​ α 2 , − 2 ​ n ) . \displaystyle=-2^{-1}Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(-2\alpha_{1},-2\alpha_{2},-2n). 
 
 
 For the GOE/GSE the duality was put forward by Mulase and Waldron in terms diagrammatic expansion of Gaussian integrals  [ 66 ] . See also  [ 53 ,  19 ] .
This duality between orthogonal and symplectic Laguerre ensembles appeared in the paper of Hanlon, Stanley and Stembridge  [ 45 , Corollary 4.2] . The duality in the Jacobi ensembles has been observed by Forrester, Rahman and Witte  [ 37 , Eq. (4.15)] . See also  [ 32 ]  and   [ 38 , Appendix B] . 
 
 
 Theorem 8.1 . 
 
 The combinations of GOE and GSE moments 
 

 
 (8.4) 
 
 S k ℝ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(n) 
 = Q k + 1 ℝ ​ ( n ) − ( 4 ​ n − 2 ) ​ Q k ℝ ​ ( n ) − 8 ​ k ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) ​ Q k − 1 ℝ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle=Q^{\mathbb{R}}_{k+1}(n)-(4n-2)Q^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}(n)-8k(2k-1)Q^{\mathbb{R}}_{k-1}(n) 
 
 
 (8.5) 
 
 S k ℍ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle S_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(n) 
 = 2 ​ Q k + 1 ℍ ​ ( n ) − ( 16 ​ n + 4 ) ​ Q k ℍ ​ ( n ) − 16 ​ k ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) ​ Q k − 1 ℍ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle=2Q^{\mathbb{H}}_{k+1}(n)-(16n+4)Q^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}(n)-16k(2k-1)Q^{\mathbb{H}}_{k-1}(n) 
 
 
 have Meixner polynomial factors: 
 

 
 (8.6) 
 
 S k ℝ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(n) 
 = − 3 ​ n ​ ( n − 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ M n − 2 ​ ( k , 3 , − 1 ) \displaystyle=-3n(n-1)\;(2k-1)!!\;M_{n-2}(k;3,-1) 
 
 
 (8.7) 
 
 
 = − 3 ​ n ​ ( n − 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ M k ​ ( n − 2 , 3 , − 1 ) \displaystyle=-3n(n-1)\;(2k-1)!!\;M_{k}(n-2;3,-1) 
 
 
 (8.8) 
 
 S k ℍ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle S_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(n) 
 = − 6 ​ n ​ ( 2 ​ n + 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ M 2 ​ n − 1 ​ ( k , 3 , − 1 ) \displaystyle=-6n(2n+1)\;(2k-1)!!\;M_{2n-1}(k;3,-1) 
 
 
 (8.9) 
 
 
 = − 6 ​ n ​ ( 2 ​ n + 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ M k ​ ( 2 ​ n − 1 , 3 , − 1 ) . \displaystyle=-6n(2n+1)\;(2k-1)!!\;M_{k}(2n-1;3,-1). 
 
 
 In particular, for any integer  n n ,  S k ℝ ​ ( n ) / ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(n)/(2k-1)!!  and  S k ℍ ​ ( n ) / ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! S_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(n)/(2k-1)!!  are Meixner-Pollaczek polynomials in  x = − i ⁡ ( k + 3 / 2 ) x=-i(k+3/2) 
 

 
 (8.10) 
 
 S k ℝ ​ ( n ) ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! \displaystyle\frac{S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(n)}{(2k-1)!!} 
 = − 6 ​ i n − 2 ​ P n − 2 ( 3 / 2 ) ​ ( x , π / 2 ) \displaystyle=-6i^{n-2}\;P^{(3/2)}_{n-2}(x;\pi/2) 
 
 
 (8.11) 
 
 S k ℍ ​ ( n ) ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! \displaystyle\frac{S_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(n)}{(2k-1)!!} 
 = − 6 ​ i 2 ​ n − 1 ​ P 2 ​ n − 1 ( 3 / 2 ) ​ ( x , π / 2 ) \displaystyle=-6i^{2n-1}\;P^{(3/2)}_{2n-1}(x;\pi/2) 
 
 
 invariant up to a change of sign under the reflection  k → − 3 − k k\to-3-k , with complex zeros on the vertical line  Re ( k ) = − 3 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(k)=-3/2 . 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We first prove ( 8.6 ). We read from Ledoux paper  [ 53 , Theorem 3] 
 

 
 
 S k ℝ ​ ( n ) = Q k + 1 ℂ ​ ( n ) − ( 4 ​ n − 3 ) ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) − 2 ​ k ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) ​ Q k − 1 ℂ ​ ( n ) . \displaystyle S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(n)=Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k+1}(n)-(4n-3)Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(n)-2k(2k-1)Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k-1}(n). 
 
 
 Using the polynomial property of  Q k ℂ ​ ( n ) Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(n) , this remainder can be expressed as a sum of two Meixner polynomials 
 

 
 
 S k ℝ ​ ( n ) = 3 ​ n ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ ( M n − 1 ​ ( k , 2 , − 1 ) − M n − 1 ​ ( k + 1 , 2 , − 1 ) ) \displaystyle S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(n)=3n\;(2k-1)!!\;\left(M_{n-1}(k;2,-1)-M_{n-1}(k+1;2,-1)\right) 
 
 
 The representation ( 8.6 ) in terms of a single Meixner polynomial follows using the Forward Shift Operator  [ 48 , Eq. (9.10.6)] . 
 
 
 To prove ( 8.8 ) the starting point is again a result in Ledoux paper  [ 53 , Theorem 5] 
 

 
 
 S k ℍ ​ ( n ) = Q k + 1 ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ n + 1 ) − ( 8 ​ n + 2 ) ​ Q k ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ n + 1 ) − 8 ​ k ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) ​ Q k − 1 ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ n + 1 ) , \displaystyle S_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(n)=Q_{k+1}^{\mathbb{R}}(2n+1)-(8n+2)Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(2n+1)-8k(2k-1)Q_{k-1}^{\mathbb{R}}(2n+1), 
 
 
 which can be written in the insightful form 
 

 
 
 S k ℍ ​ ( n ) = S k ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ n + 1 ) . \displaystyle S_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(n)=S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(2n+1). 
 
 
 The representation ( 8.8 ) is now a consequence of ( 8.6 ). Alternatively, by the duality relation ( 8.1 ) 
 

 
 
 S k ℍ ​ ( n ) = ( − 1 ) k ​ ( Q k + 1 ℝ ​ ( − 2 ​ n ) + ( 8 ​ n + 2 ) ​ Q k ℝ ​ ( − 2 ​ n ) − 8 ​ k ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) ​ Q k − 1 ℝ ​ ( − 2 ​ n ) ) , \displaystyle S_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(n)=(-1)^{k}\left(Q_{k+1}^{\mathbb{R}}(-2n)+(8n+2)Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(-2n)-8k(2k-1)Q_{k-1}^{\mathbb{R}}(-2n)\right), 
 
 
 that is 
 

 
 
 S k ℍ ​ ( n ) = ( − 1 ) k ​ S k ℝ ​ ( − 2 ​ n ) . \displaystyle S_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(n)=(-1)^{k}S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(-2n). 
 
 
 Using the self-duality of Meixner polynomials, we write 
 

 
 
 S k ℍ ​ ( n ) = ( − 1 ) k + 1 ​ 6 ​ n ​ ( 2 ​ n + 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ M k ​ ( − 2 ​ n − 2 , 3 , − 1 ) . \displaystyle S_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(n)=(-1)^{k+1}6n(2n+1)\;(2k-1)!!\;M_{k}(-2n-2;3,-1). 
 
 
 Now we use the symmetry  ( − 1 ) k + 1 ​ M k ​ ( − 2 ​ n − 2 , 3 , − 1 ) = M k ​ ( 2 ​ n + 1 , 3 , − 1 ) (-1)^{k+1}M_{k}(-2n-2;3,-1)=M_{k}(2n+1;3,-1) , and the self-duality again to conclude the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 The S-L problem satisfied by the Meixner polynomials is a three term recursion formula for  S k ℝ ​ ( n ) S^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}(n)  and  S k ℍ ​ ( n ) S^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}(n) , 
 

 
 (8.12) 
 
 ( k + 3 ) ​ S k + 1 ℝ ​ ( n ) = ( 2 ​ k + 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ n − 1 ) ​ S k ℝ ​ ( n ) + k ⁡ ( 2 ​ k + 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) ​ S k − 1 ℝ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle(k+3)S^{\mathbb{R}}_{k+1}(n)=(2k+1)(2n-1)S^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}(n)+k(2k+1)(2k-1)S^{\mathbb{R}}_{k-1}(n) 
 
 
 (8.13) 
 
 ( k + 3 ) ​ S k + 1 ℍ ​ ( n ) = ( 2 ​ k + 1 ) ​ ( 4 ​ n + 1 ) ​ S k ℍ ​ ( n ) + k ⁡ ( 2 ​ k + 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) ​ S k − 1 ℍ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle(k+3)S^{\mathbb{H}}_{k+1}(n)=(2k+1)(4n+1)S^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}(n)+k(2k+1)(2k-1)S^{\mathbb{H}}_{k-1}(n) 
 
 
 These recursions, which are very similar to the Harer-Zagier formula, become five term recurrences for the moments  Q k ℝ ​ ( n ) Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(n)  (this is Ledoux recursion  [ 53 , Theorem 2] ) and  Q k ℍ ​ ( n ) Q_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(n) . 
 
 
 Corollary 8.2 . 
 
 

 
 
 ( k + 1 ) ​ Q k ℝ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle(k+1)Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(n) 
 = ( 4 ​ k − 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ n − 1 ) ​ Q k − 1 ℝ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle=(4k-1)(2n-1)Q_{k-1}^{\mathbb{R}}(n) 
 
 
 
 
 + ( 2 ​ k − 3 ) ​ ( 10 ​ k 2 − 9 ​ k − 8 ​ n 2 + 8 ​ n ) ​ Q k − 2 ℝ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle+(2k-3)(10k^{2}-9k-8n^{2}+8n)Q_{k-2}^{\mathbb{R}}(n) 
 
 
 
 
 − 5 ​ ( 2 ​ k − 3 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 4 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 5 ) ​ ( 2 ​ n − 1 ) ​ Q k − 3 ℝ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle-5(2k-3)(2k-4)(2k-5)(2n-1)Q_{k-3}^{\mathbb{R}}(n) 
 
 
 (8.14) 
 
 
 − 2 ​ ( 2 ​ k − 3 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 4 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 5 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 6 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 7 ) ​ Q k − 4 ℝ ​ ( n ) . \displaystyle-2(2k-3)(2k-4)(2k-5)(2k-6)(2k-7)Q_{k-4}^{\mathbb{R}}(n). 
 
 
 

 
 
 ( k + 1 ) ​ Q k ℍ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle(k+1)Q_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(n) 
 = ( 4 ​ k − 1 ) ​ ( 4 ​ n + 1 ) ​ Q k − 1 ℍ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle=(4k-1)(4n+1)Q_{k-1}^{\mathbb{H}}(n) 
 
 
 
 
 + ( 2 ​ k − 3 ) ​ ( 10 ​ k 2 − 9 ​ k − 32 ​ n 2 − 16 ​ n ) ​ Q k − 2 ℍ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle+(2k-3)(10k^{2}-9k-32n^{2}-16n)Q_{k-2}^{\mathbb{H}}(n) 
 
 
 
 
 − 5 ​ ( 2 ​ k − 3 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 4 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 5 ) ​ ( 4 ​ n + 1 ) ​ Q k − 3 ℍ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle-5(2k-3)(2k-4)(2k-5)(4n+1)Q_{k-3}^{\mathbb{H}}(n) 
 
 
 (8.15) 
 
 
 − 2 ​ ( 2 ​ k − 3 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 4 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 5 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 6 ) ​ ( 2 ​ k − 7 ) ​ Q k − 4 ℍ ​ ( n ) . \displaystyle-2(2k-3)(2k-4)(2k-5)(2k-6)(2k-7)Q_{k-4}^{\mathbb{H}}(n). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Theorem 8.3 . 
 
 Set  α = m − n \alpha=m-n , and consider the following combinations of moments for the LOE and the LSE. 
 

 
 (8.16) 
 
 S k ℝ ​ ( m , n ) = Q k + 1 ℝ ​ ( m , n ) − 2 ​ ( m + n − 1 ) ​ Q k ℝ ​ ( m , n ) − ( 1 − α 2 + 4 ​ k ​ ( k − 1 ) ) ​ Q k − 1 ℝ ​ ( m , n ) S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(m,n)=Q^{\mathbb{R}}_{k+1}(m,n)-2(m+n-1)Q^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}(m,n)\\
-(1-\alpha^{2}+4k(k-1))Q^{\mathbb{R}}_{k-1}(m,n) 
 
 
 

 
 (8.17) 
 
 S k ℍ ​ ( m , n ) = 2 ​ Q k + 1 ℍ ​ ( m , n ) − ( 8 ​ m + 8 ​ n + 4 ) ​ Q k ℍ ​ ( m , n ) − ( 2 − 8 ​ α 2 + 8 ​ k ​ ( k − 1 ) ) ​ Q k − 1 ℍ ​ ( m , n ) S_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(m,n)=2Q^{\mathbb{H}}_{k+1}(m,n)-(8m+8n+4)Q^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}(m,n)\\
-(2-8\alpha^{2}+8k(k-1))Q^{\mathbb{H}}_{k-1}(m,n) 
 
 
 Then,  S k ℝ ​ ( m , n 𝐶𝐿𝑂𝑆𝐸 S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(m,n  and  S k ℍ ​ ( m , n ) S_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(m,n)  can be written in terms of dual Hahn polynomials (as functions of  k k ) or Hahn polynomials (as functions of  n n ): 
 

 
 (8.18) 
 
 S k ℝ ​ ( m , n ) ( k + α ) ! \displaystyle\frac{S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(m,n)}{(k+\alpha)!} 
 = − 6 ( n − 2 ) ! ​ ( m − 2 ) ! ​ S n − 2 ​ ( x 2 , 5 2 , 1 2 , α + 1 2 ) \displaystyle=-\frac{6}{(n-2)!(m-2)!}\;S_{n-2}\left(x^{2};\;\frac{5}{2},\frac{1}{2},\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right) 
 
 
 (8.19) 
 
 
 = − 3 ​ n ​ m ​ ( n − 1 ) ​ ( m − 1 ) ( α + 2 ) ! ​ Q k − 2 ​ ( n − 2 ,  2 , 2 , − 3 − α ) \displaystyle=-\frac{3nm(n-1)(m-1)}{(\alpha+2)!}\;Q_{k-2}(n-2;\;2,2,-3-\alpha) 
 
 
 (8.20) 
 
 S k ℍ ​ ( m , n ) ( k + 2 ​ α ) ! \displaystyle\frac{S_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(m,n)}{(k+2\alpha)!} 
 = − 24 ​ n ​ m ( 2 ​ n ) ! ​ ( 2 ​ m ) ! ​ S 2 ​ n − 1 ​ ( x 2 , 5 2 , 1 2 , 2 ​ α + 1 2 ) \displaystyle=-\frac{24nm}{(2n)!(2m)!}\;S_{2n-1}\left(x^{2},\frac{5}{2},\frac{1}{2},2\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right) 
 
 
 (8.21) 
 
 
 = − 12 ​ n ​ m ​ ( 2 ​ n + 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ m + 1 ) ( 2 ​ α + 2 ) ! ​ Q k − 2 ​ ( 2 ​ n − 1 ,  2 , 2 , − 3 − 2 ​ α ) \displaystyle=-\frac{12nm(2n+1)(2m+1)}{(2\alpha+2)!}\;Q_{k-2}(2n-1;\,2,2,-3-2\alpha) 
 
 
 where  k = i ​ x − 1 / 2 k=ix-1/2 . In particular this shows that the polynomials  S k ℝ ​ ( m , n ) / ( k + α ) ! S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(m,n)/(k+\alpha)!  and  S k ℍ ​ ( m , n ) / ( k + 2 ​ α ) ! S_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(m,n)/(k+2\alpha)!  are invariant under the reflection  k → − 1 − k k\to-1-k  (reciprocity law) and, moreover, their zeros lie on the critical line  Re ( k ) = − 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(k)=-1/2 . 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By the inhomogeneous recursion for moments of the LOE  [ 28 , Theorem 3.5] ,  S k ℝ ​ ( m , n ) S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(m,n)  is a combination of moments of the LUE 
 

 
 
 S k ℝ ​ ( m , n ) = 3 k − 1 ​ ( ( m + n − k − 1 ) ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( m − 1 , n − 1 ) − Q k + 1 ℂ ​ ( m − 1 , n − 1 ) ) , \displaystyle S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(m,n)=\frac{3}{k-1}((m+n-k-1)Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(m-1,n-1)-Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k+1}(m-1,n-1)), 
 
 
 that can be expressed in terms of continuous dual Hahn polynomials 
 

 
 
 S k ℝ ( m , n ) = 3 ​ ( k + α ) ! ( k − 1 ) ​ ( m − 2 ) ! ​ ( n − 2 ) ! × ( ( m + n − k − 1 ) ​ S n − 2 ​ ( x 2 , 3 2 , 1 2 , α + 1 2 ) − ( m − n + k + 1 ) ​ S n − 2 ​ ( ( x − i ) 2 , 3 2 , 1 2 , α + 1 2 ) ) S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(m,n)=\frac{3(k+\alpha)!}{(k-1)(m-2)!(n-2)!}\times\\
\biggl((m+n-k-1)S_{n-2}\left(x^{2};\;\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2},\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)-(m-n+k+1)S_{n-2}\left((x-i)^{2};\;\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2},\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\right)\biggr) 
 
 
 The final result ( 8.18 ) can be obtained by using the Forward Shift Operator of the continuous dual Hahn polynomials  [ 48 , Eq. (9.3.7)] .
From the hypergeometric representation of Hahn polynomials we get ( 8.19 ). 
 
 
 For the symplectic moments, note that, by duality ( 8.2 ) between LOE and LSE moments, 
 

 
 
 S k ℍ ​ ( m , n ) = ( − 1 ) k ​ S k ℝ ​ ( − 2 ​ m , − 2 ​ n ) . S_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(m,n)=(-1)^{k}S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(-2m,-2n). 
 
 
 This proves ( 8.21 ). We use the identity  ( 3 − 2 ​ α ) k − 2 ​ Q k − 2 ​ ( − 2 ​ n − 2 ,  2 , 2 , − 3 + 2 ​ α ) = ( − 1 ) k ​ ( 3 + 2 ​ α ) k − 2 ​ Q k − 2 ​ ( 2 ​ n − 1 ,  2 , 2 , − 3 − 2 ​ α ) (3-2\alpha)_{k-2}Q_{k-2}(-2n-2;\;2,2,-3+2\alpha)=(-1)^{k}(3+2\alpha)_{k-2}Q_{k-2}(2n-1;\;2,2,-3-2\alpha)  and find ( 8.21 ).
Now, this can be written as a polynomial in  k k  which can be cast as ( 8.20 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 Remark . 
 
 As in the Gaussian case, there is a reflection formula under the transformation  2 ​ m + 1 → − 2 ​ m 2m+1\to-2m  and  2 ​ n + 1 → − 2 ​ n 2n+1\to-2n 
 

 
 (8.22) 
 
 S ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ m + 1 , 2 ​ n + 1 ) = ( − 1 ) k ​ S ℝ ​ ( − 2 ​ m , − 2 ​ n ) . S^{\mathbb{R}}(2m+1,2n+1)=(-1)^{k}S^{\mathbb{R}}(-2m,-2n). 
 
 
 
 
 
 The S-L problem of continuous dual Hahn polynomials corresponds to the three term recursions (in  k k ) 
 

 
 (8.23) 
 
 ( k + 3 ) ​ S k + 1 ℝ ​ ( m , n ) \displaystyle(k+3)S^{\mathbb{R}}_{k+1}(m,n) 
 = ( 2 ​ k + 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ n + α − 1 ) ​ S k ℝ ​ ( m , n ) + ( k − 2 ) ​ ( k 2 − α 2 ) ​ S k − 1 ℝ ​ ( m , n ) \displaystyle=(2k+1)(2n+\alpha-1)S^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}(m,n)+(k-2)(k^{2}-\alpha^{2})S^{\mathbb{R}}_{k-1}(m,n) 
 
 
 (8.24) 
 
 ( k + 3 ) ​ S k + 1 ℍ ​ ( m , n ) \displaystyle(k+3)S^{\mathbb{H}}_{k+1}(m,n) 
 = ( 2 ​ k + 1 ) ​ ( 4 ​ n + 2 ​ α + 1 ) ​ S k ℍ ​ ( m , n ) + ( k − 2 ) ​ ( k 2 − 4 ​ α 2 ) ​ S k − 1 ℍ ​ ( m , n ) \displaystyle=(2k+1)(4n+2\alpha+1)S^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}(m,n)+(k-2)(k^{2}-4\alpha^{2})S^{\mathbb{H}}_{k-1}(m,n) 
 
 
 which are similar to the Haagerup-Thorbjørnsen formula for the moments of the LUE. Writing  S k ℝ ​ ( m , n ) S^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}(m,n)  and  S k ℍ ​ ( m , n ) S^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}(m,n)  in terms of the moments we obtain the following five term recursions. To our knowledge these recursion formulae are new. 
 
 
 Corollary 8.4 . 
 
 

 
 (8.25) 
 
 ( k + 1 ) ​ Q k ℝ ​ ( m , n ) \displaystyle(k+1)Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(m,n) 
 = A ​ Q k − 1 ℝ ​ ( m , n ) + B ​ Q k − 2 ℝ ​ ( m , n ) + C ​ Q k − 3 ℝ ​ ( m , n ) + D ​ Q k − 4 ℝ ​ ( m , n ) . \displaystyle=AQ_{k-1}^{\mathbb{R}}(m,n)+BQ_{k-2}^{\mathbb{R}}(m,n)+CQ_{k-3}^{\mathbb{R}}(m,n)+DQ_{k-4}^{\mathbb{R}}(m,n). 
 
 
 with 
 

 
 
 A \displaystyle A 
 = ( 4 ​ k − 1 ) ​ ( m + n − 1 ) \displaystyle=(4k-1)(m+n-1) 
 
 
 
 B \displaystyle B 
 = − ( 4 ​ k − 6 ) ​ ( m + n − 1 ) 2 + ( k − 4 ) ​ ( ( k − 2 ) 2 − α 2 ) + ( k + 1 ) ​ ( ( 2 ​ k − 3 ) 2 − α 2 ) \displaystyle=-(4k-6)(m+n-1)^{2}+(k-4)\left((k-2)^{2}-\alpha^{2}\right)+(k+1)\left((2k-3)^{2}-\alpha^{2}\right) 
 
 
 
 C \displaystyle C 
 = − ( m + n − 1 ) ​ ( ( 2 ​ k − 3 ) ​ ( ( 2 ​ k − 5 ) 2 − α 2 ) − ( 2 ​ k − 8 ) ​ ( ( k − 2 ) 2 − α 2 ) ) \displaystyle=-(m+n-1)\left((2k-3)\left((2k-5)^{2}-\alpha^{2}\right)-(2k-8)\left((k-2)^{2}-\alpha^{2}\right)\right) 
 
 
 
 D \displaystyle D 
 = − ( k − 4 ) ​ ( ( k − 2 ) 2 − α 2 ) ​ ( ( 2 ​ k − 7 ) 2 − α 2 ) . \displaystyle=-(k-4)\left((k-2)^{2}-\alpha^{2}\right)\left((2k-7)^{2}-\alpha^{2}\right). 
 
 
 and 
 

 
 (8.26) 
 
 ( k + 1 ) ​ Q k ℍ ​ ( m , n ) \displaystyle(k+1)Q_{k}^{\mathbb{H}}(m,n) 
 = A ​ Q k − 1 ℍ ​ ( m , n ) + B ​ Q k − 2 ℍ ​ ( m , n ) + C ​ Q k − 3 ℍ ​ ( m , n ) + D ​ Q k − 4 ℍ ​ ( m , n ) . \displaystyle=AQ_{k-1}^{\mathbb{H}}(m,n)+BQ_{k-2}^{\mathbb{H}}(m,n)+CQ_{k-3}^{\mathbb{H}}(m,n)+DQ_{k-4}^{\mathbb{H}}(m,n). 
 
 
 with 
 

 
 
 A \displaystyle A 
 = ( 4 ​ k − 1 ) ​ ( 2 ​ m + 2 ​ n + 1 ) \displaystyle=(4k-1)(2m+2n+1) 
 
 
 
 B \displaystyle B 
 = − ( 4 ​ k − 6 ) ​ ( 2 ​ m + 2 ​ n + 1 ) 2 + ( k − 4 ) ​ ( ( k − 2 ) 2 − 4 ​ α 2 ) + ( k + 1 ) ​ ( ( 2 ​ k − 3 ) 2 − 4 ​ α 2 ) \displaystyle=-(4k-6)(2m+2n+1)^{2}+(k-4)\left((k-2)^{2}-4\alpha^{2}\right)+(k+1)\left((2k-3)^{2}-4\alpha^{2}\right) 
 
 
 
 C \displaystyle C 
 = − ( 2 ​ m + 2 ​ n + 1 ) ​ ( ( 2 ​ k − 3 ) ​ ( ( 2 ​ k − 5 ) 2 − 4 ​ α 2 ) − ( 2 ​ k − 8 ) ​ ( ( k − 2 ) 2 − 4 ​ α 2 ) ) \displaystyle=-(2m+2n+1)\left((2k-3)\left((2k-5)^{2}-4\alpha^{2}\right)-(2k-8)\left((k-2)^{2}-4\alpha^{2}\right)\right) 
 
 
 
 D \displaystyle D 
 = − ( k − 4 ) ​ ( ( k − 2 ) 2 − 4 ​ α 2 ) ​ ( ( 2 ​ k − 7 ) 2 − 4 ​ α 2 ) . \displaystyle=-(k-4)\left((k-2)^{2}-4\alpha^{2}\right)\left((2k-7)^{2}-4\alpha^{2}\right). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Using methods similar to those in  [ 28 ]  it is possible to write a recursion formula for the moments of the JOE. Denote 
 

 
 
 S k ℝ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) = ( 2 ​ k + 4 − α 1 − α 2 − 2 ​ n ) ​ ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ ( n + k + 1 ) ) ​ Δ ​ Q k + 1 ℝ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) + 2 ​ ( α 1 ​ α 2 − α 1 − α 2 + α 2 2 − 4 ​ k ​ ( 1 + k ) − 2 ​ n + 2 ​ ( α 1 + α 2 ) ​ n + 2 ​ n 2 ) ​ Δ ​ Q k ℝ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) − ( α 2 2 − ( 1 − 2 ​ k ) 2 ) ​ Δ ​ Q k − 1 ℝ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) . S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)=(2k+4-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-2n)(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2(n+k+1))\Delta Q_{k+1}^{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)\\
+2(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{2}^{2}-4k(1+k)-2n+2(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})n+2n^{2})\Delta Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)\\
-(\alpha_{2}^{2}-(1-2k)^{2})\Delta Q_{k-1}^{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n). 
 
 
 
 
 Proposition 8.5   (Recursion for moments of the JOE) . 
 
 Set  α 1 = m 1 − n \alpha_{1}=m_{1}-n  and  α 2 = m 2 − n \alpha_{2}=m_{2}-n .
Then the differences of adjacent moments  Δ ​ Q k ℝ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \Delta Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)  of the JOE satisfy the following inhomogeneous three term recursion 
 

 
 (8.27) 
 
 S k ℝ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) = 3 k − 1 ​ ( ( ( α 1 + α 2 ) ​ ( α 2 − k − 1 ) + 2 ​ ( α 1 + α 2 − k − 1 ) ​ n + 2 ​ n 2 ) ​ Δ ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n − 1 ) CLOSE OPEN − ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n ) ​ ( α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n − k − 3 ) ​ Δ ​ Q k + 1 ℂ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n − 1 ) ) , S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)=\\
\frac{3}{k-1}\Bigl(\left((\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})(\alpha_{2}-k-1)+2(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}-k-1)n+2n^{2}\right)\Delta Q_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n-1)\\
-(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n)(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n-k-3)\Delta Q_{k+1}^{\mathbb{C}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n-1)\Bigr), 
 
 
 
 
 
 As in the Gaussian and Laguerre cases, the above recursion formula suggests that  S k ℝ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) , and not the moments themselves, have a nice polynomial property. This is the content of the next theorem whose proof goes along the same lines as the Gaussian and Laguerre cases. 
 
 
 Theorem 8.6 . 
 
 Set  k = i ​ x − 1 / 2 k=ix-1/2 . The combination  S k ℝ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)  has a Wilson polynomial factor 
 

 
 (8.28) 
 
 S k ℝ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) = ( − 1 ) n − 1 ​ 6 ( n − 2 ) ! ​ ( α 1 + n ) ​ ( α 1 + n − 1 ) ​ ( α 1 + α 2 + n ) ! ( α 2 + n − 2 ) ! × ( α 2 + k ) ! ( α 1 + α 2 + k + 2 ​ n − 1 ) ! ​ W n − 2 ​ ( x 2 , 5 2 , 1 2 , α 2 + 1 2 , 3 2 − α 1 − α 2 − 2 ​ n ) . S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)=(-1)^{n-1}\frac{6}{(n-2)!}\frac{(\alpha_{1}+n)(\alpha_{1}+n-1)(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+n)!}{(\alpha_{2}+n-2)!}\\
\times\frac{(\alpha_{2}+k)!}{(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+k+2n-1)!}W_{n-2}\left(x^{2};\;\frac{5}{2},\frac{1}{2},\alpha_{2}+\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-2n\right). 
 
 
 In particular,  S k ℝ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) ​ ( ( α 1 + α 2 + k + 2 ​ n − 1 ) ! / ( α 2 + k ) ! ) S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)((\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+k+2n-1)!/(\alpha_{2}+k)!)  is a polynomial of degree  2 ​ ( n − 2 ) 2(n-2)  in  k k , invariant under the reflection  k → − 1 − k k\to-1-k , with zeros on the vertical line  Re ( k ) = − 1 / 2 \operatorname{Re}(k)=-1/2 . 
 
 
 
 
 8.2.  Symplectic Ensembles 

 
 The goal of this section is to establish the following polynomial property for the moments of the symplectic ensembles. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 4 .  The  2 ​ ( n − 1 ) 2(n-1)  complex zeros of the symplectic polynomial  p k ℍ ​ ( n ) p^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}(n)  for the GSE with  n = 30 n=30  (left) and  n = 50 n=50  (right). 
 
 
 Theorem 8.7 . 
 
 The rescaled moments 
 

 
 (8.29) 
 
 p n ℍ ​ ( k ) = { c n ​ 1 Γ ⁡ ( k + 1 / 2 ) ​ Q k ℍ ​ ( n ) GSE c n , m ​ 2 k Γ ⁡ ( 2 ​ α + k + 2 ) ​ Q k ℍ ​ ( m , n ) LSE c n , α 1 , α 2 ​ Γ ⁡ ( 1 + 2 ​ α 1 + 2 ​ α 2 + 4 ​ n + k ) Γ ⁡ ( 2 ​ α 1 + k + 2 ) ​ Δ ​ Q k ℍ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) JSE p^{\mathbb{H}}_{n}(k)=\begin{cases}c_{n}\dfrac{1}{\Gamma(k+1/2)}Q^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}(n)&\mathrm{GSE}\\
\\
c_{n,m}\dfrac{2^{k}}{\Gamma(2\alpha+k+2)}Q^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}(m,n)&\mathrm{LSE}\\
\\
c_{n,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\dfrac{\Gamma(1+2\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}+4n+k)}{\Gamma(2\alpha_{1}+k+2)}\Delta Q^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)&\mathrm{JSE}\end{cases} 
 
 
 are monic polynomials in  k k  of degree  2 ​ ( n − 1 ) 2(n-1)  in the GSE, and degree  4 ​ ( n − 1 ) 4(n-1)  in the LSE and JSE cases. The normalizing constants are 
 

 
 
 { c n = 2 2 ​ ( 1 − n ) ​ Γ ​ ( 2 ​ n ) ​ π c n , m = Γ ⁡ ( 2 ​ m ) ​ Γ ​ ( 2 ​ n ) c n , α 1 , α 2 = Γ ⁡ ( 2 ​ n + 2 ​ α 1 ) ​ Γ ​ ( 2 ​ α 2 + 4 ) ​ Γ ​ ( 2 ​ n ) Γ ⁡ ( 2 ​ α 1 + 2 ​ α 2 + 2 ​ n + 2 ) ​ Γ ​ ( 2 ​ α 2 + 2 ​ n + 2 ) ​ ( 2 ​ α 2 + 2 ) . \begin{cases}c_{n}=2^{2(1-n)}\Gamma(2n)\sqrt{\pi}\\
c_{n,m}=\Gamma(2m)\Gamma(2n)\\
c_{n,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}=\dfrac{\Gamma(2n+2\alpha_{1})\Gamma(2\alpha_{2}+4)\Gamma(2n)}{\Gamma(2\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}+2n+2)\Gamma(2\alpha_{2}+2n+2)(2\alpha_{2}+2)}.\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We will discuss the Gaussian case in detail, as the Laguerre and Jacobi cases follow a similar pattern. By  [ 62 , Eq. (33)] , we have the explicit formula 
 

 
 (8.30) 
 
 Q k ℍ ​ ( n ) = 2 − k − 1 ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( 2 ​ n ) − a n ​ ∑ j = 1 n ∑ i = 0 n − j ( k i ) ​ ( k i + j ) ​ ( n − i − j + 1 ) ( k − 1 / 2 ) Q^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}(n)=2^{-k-1}Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(2n)-a_{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-j}{k\choose i}{k\choose i+j}(n-i-j+1)_{(k-1/2)} 
 
 
 where  Q k ℂ ​ ( 2 ​ n ) Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(2n)  denotes the moments of the GUE (see Section  4.1 ) and 
 

 
 
 a n = Γ ⁡ ( n + 1 ) ​ Γ ​ ( n ) π ​ Γ ​ ( 2 ​ n ) ​ 4 1 − n . a_{n}=\frac{\Gamma(n+1)\Gamma(n)}{\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma(2n)4^{1-n}}. 
 
 
 Although formula ( 8.30 ) was only stated in  [ 62 ]  for  k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} , it naturally defines a meromorphic continuation to  k ∈ ℂ k\in\mathbb{C} , as follows. As a function of  k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} , we have that  Q 2 ​ k ℂ ​ ( 2 ​ n ) / ( Γ ⁡ ( k + 1 / 2 ) ) Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{2k}(2n)/(\Gamma(k+1/2))  is a polynomial of degree  2 ​ n − 1 2n-1  in  k k  (see equation ( 4.4 )) and hence is defined for any  k ∈ ℂ k\in\mathbb{C} . It remains to study the second term in ( 8.30 ). Note that 
 

 
 
 ( n − i − j + 1 ) ( k − 1 / 2 ) Γ ⁡ ( k + 1 / 2 ) \frac{(n-i-j+1)_{(k-1/2)}}{\Gamma(k+1/2)} 
 
 
 is a polynomial of degree  n − i − j n-i-j , while  ( k i ) {k\choose i}  and  ( k i + j ) {k\choose i+j}  are polynomials of degree  i i  and  i + j i+j  respectively. Hence  Q k ℍ ​ ( n ) / Γ ⁡ ( k + 1 / 2 ) Q^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}(n)/\Gamma(k+1/2)  is a finite sum of polynomials in  k k  and is therefore a polynomial. To compute degrees, notice that the highest degree term in the summand of ( 8.30 ) occurs when  2 ​ i + j + n − i − j = n + i 2i+j+n-i-j=n+i  is maximal, namely when  i = n − 1 i=n-1  implying a degree of  2 ​ n − 1 2n-1 . That the degree of the combined polynomials ( i.e.  unitary plus symplectic contribution) is really  2 ​ n − 2 2n-2  is a consequence of the following cancellation. Setting  j = 1 j=1  and  i = n − 1 i=n-1  in the summand of ( 8.30 ) and dividing by  Γ ⁡ ( k + 1 / 2 ) \Gamma(k+1/2)  gives the polynomial 
 

 
 
 a n ​ ( k n − 1 ) ​ ( k n ) a_{n}{k\choose n-1}{k\choose n} 
 
 
 Then Stirling’s formula gives the estimate 
 

 
 
 a n ​ ( k n − 1 ) ​ ( k n ) = k 2 ​ n − 1 ( 2 ​ n − 1 ) ! ​ 4 1 − n + O ⁡ ( k 2 ​ n − 2 ) , k → ∞ a_{n}{k\choose n-1}{k\choose n}=\frac{k^{2n-1}}{(2n-1)!4^{1-n}}+O(k^{2n-2}),\qquad k\to\infty 
 
 
 Similarly, consider the complex moments 
 

 
 
 1 2 ​ Γ ​ ( k + 1 / 2 ) ​ Q k ℂ ​ ( 2 ​ n ) = 1 π ​ ∑ j = 0 2 ​ n − 1 2 j − 1 ​ ( k j ) ​ ( 2 ​ n j + 1 ) \frac{1}{2\;\Gamma(k+1/2)}Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(2n)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\sum_{j=0}^{2n-1}2^{j-1}{k\choose j}{2n\choose j+1} 
 
 
 which has the same leading coefficient (setting  j = 2 ​ n − 1 j=2n-1 ) as 
 

 
 
 1 π ​ ( k 2 ​ n − 1 ) ​ 2 2 ​ n − 2 = k 2 ​ n − 1 ​ 4 n − 1 ( 2 ​ n − 1 ) ! + O ⁡ ( k 2 ​ n − 2 ) , k → ∞ \frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}{k\choose 2n-1}2^{2n-2}=\frac{k^{2n-1}4^{n-1}}{(2n-1)!}+O(k^{2n-2}),\qquad k\to\infty 
 
 
 Hence the terms of order  k 2 ​ n − 1 k^{2n-1}  in ( 8.30 ) cancel, yielding a polynomial of degree  2 ​ n − 2 2n-2 . To compute the normalizing factor  c n c_{n}  requires studying terms of order  k 2 ​ n − 2 k^{2n-2} . This is a straightforward but tedious task and we omit the details. The only contributions to the monomial  k 2 ​ n − 2 k^{2n-2}  come from ( 8.2 ) when  j = 2 ​ n − 1 j=2n-1  and  j = 2 ​ n − 2 j=2n-2 , and from the double sum in ( 8.30 ) with indices  ( i , j ) = ( n − 1 , 1 ) (i,j)=(n-1,1)  and  ( i , j ) = ( n − 2 , 1 ) , ( n − 2 , 2 ) (i,j)=(n-2,1),(n-2,2) . Then studying the asymptotics of these five terms as  k → ∞ k\to\infty  with Stirling’s formula gives the result. For the Laguerre and Jacobi cases, this computation can be repeated with the formulae  [ 62 , Eq. (89) and Eq. (98)]  which have an identical structure to ( 8.30 ) and is therefore omitted.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Below are the first few polynomials  p n ℍ ​ ( k ) p^{\mathbb{H}}_{n}(k)  for the GSE, whose zeros appear to settle onto an explicit contour in the complex plane as  n n  becomes larger (see Fig.  4 ). 
 

 
 
 p 1 ℍ ​ ( k ) = 1 p 2 ℍ ​ ( k ) = k 2 + 5 ​ k + 3 p 3 ℍ ​ ( k ) = k 4 + 10 ​ k 3 + 38 ​ k 2 + 41 ​ k + 45 2 p 4 ℍ ​ ( k ) = k 6 + 15 ​ k 5 + 109 ​ k 4 + 393 ​ k 3 + 637 ​ k 2 + 735 ​ k + 315 . \begin{split}p^{\mathbb{H}}_{1}(k)&=1\\
p^{\mathbb{H}}_{2}(k)&=k^{2}+5k+3\\
p^{\mathbb{H}}_{3}(k)&=k^{4}+10k^{3}+38k^{2}+41k+\frac{45}{2}\\
p^{\mathbb{H}}_{4}(k)&=k^{6}+15k^{5}+109k^{4}+393k^{3}+637k^{2}+735k+315.\end{split} 
 
 
 
 
 
 8.3.  Orthogonal Ensembles 

 
 In this section we will study the Mellin transform of the one-point correlation function  ρ n ( β ) ​ ( x ) \rho_{n}^{(\beta)}(x)  with  β = 1 \beta=1 . One can expect this case to be more complicated in general, since now ( 3.1 ) contains a non-analytic term (the absolute value of the Vandermonde determinant, which happened to be a polynomial in the cases  β = 2 \beta=2  and  β = 4 \beta=4 ). In the case of  n n  odd we are saved by a remarkable duality principle for the Mellin transform, relating the orthogonal and symplectic ensembles. This duality involves a simple correction term which is a single hypergeometric OP. 
 
 
 The case of  n n  even has a different analytic structure, evident already at  n = 2 n=2 . Indeed, it was known since the beginnings of random matrix theory that the parity  n n  plays an important role for ensembles with orthogonal symmetry (see  [ 58 , Chapter 6]  for example or more recently  [ 35 ] ), with most authors assuming  n n  to be even for simplicity. Here it is the converse, we describe the analytic structure for  n n  odd and give an explicit analytic continuation. First we need a proposition relating the orthogonal and symplectic ensembles. 
 
 
 Proposition 8.8 . 
 
 Given the notation of Section  3 , let  p n ​ ( x ) p_{n}(x)  denote the degree  n n  monic polynomial orthogonal with respect to the weight  w 2 ​ ( x ) w_{2}(x)  on the interval  I I . Then the one-point eigenvalue density ( 3.3 ) satisfies the following duality 
 

 
 (8.31) 
 
 ρ 2 ​ n + 1 ( 1 ) ​ ( x ) = 2 ​ ρ ~ n ( 4 ) ​ ( x ) + w 1 ​ ( x ) ​ p 2 ​ n ​ ( x ) ∫ I w 1 ​ ( t ) ​ p 2 ​ n ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t \rho^{(1)}_{2n+1}(x)=2\tilde{\rho}^{(4)}_{n}(x)+\frac{w_{1}(x)p_{2n}(x)}{\int_{I}w_{1}(t)p_{2n}(t)\,dt} 
 
 
 where  ρ ~ ( 4 ) ​ ( x ) \tilde{\rho}^{(4)}(x)  is the  β = 4 \beta=4  eigenvalue density with respect to the modified weights 
 

 
 (8.32) 
 
 w ~ 4 ​ ( x ) = { e − x 2 / 2 Hermite x α + 1 ​ e − x Laguerre x α 1 + 1 ​ ( 1 − x ) α 2 + 1 Jacobi . \tilde{w}_{4}(x)=\begin{cases}e^{-x^{2}/2}&\text{Hermite}\\
x^{\alpha+1}e^{-x}&\text{Laguerre}\\
x^{\alpha_{1}+1}(1-x)^{\alpha_{2}+1}&\text{Jacobi}.\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Remark . 
 
 We give a complete proof of Propostition  8.8  in Appendix  A , which is based on the skew-orthogonal polynomial formalism developed in  [ 3 ] . In the specific case of the GOE, formula ( 8.31 ) was mentioned in  [ 53 ] . Actually, the statement of Proposition  8.8  is implicit in Forrester’s book  [ 36 , (6.120)-(6.122)] . It is worth emphasizing that this duality goes beyond the one-point function and can be formulated as a duality between the correlation kernels of  n n -odd orthogonal and symplectic ensembles. This suggests a possibly simpler route to studying correlation functions of  n n -odd orthogonal ensembles, but this lies beyond the scope of the current investigation. 
 
 
 
 We now study the consequences of the duality ( 8.31 ) for the Mellin transforms of the orthogonal ensembles. 
 
 
 Theorem 8.9   (Duality in the  n n -odd orthogonal ensemble) . 
 
 In the three orthogonal ensembles the following identity holds for all  k ∈ ℂ k\in\mathbb{C}  and  n ∈ ℕ n\in\mathbb{N} : 
 

 
 (8.33) 
 
 Q k ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ n + 1 ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(2n+1) 
 = 2 k + 1 ​ Q k ℍ ​ ( n ) + 4 k ​ Γ ​ ( k + 1 / 2 ) ​ f k ​ ( n ) \displaystyle=2^{k+1}Q^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}(n)+4^{k}\Gamma\left(k+1/2\right)f_{k}(n) 
 
 
 (8.34) 
 
 Q k ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ m + 1 , 2 ​ n + 1 ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(2m+1,2n+1) 
 = 2 k + 1 ​ Q k ℍ ​ ( m , n ) + 2 k ​ Γ ​ ( k + m − n + 1 / 2 ) ​ f k ​ ( m , n ) \displaystyle=2^{k+1}Q^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}\left(m,n\right)+2^{k}\Gamma\left(k+m-n+1/2\right)f_{k}(m,n) 
 
 
 (8.35) 
 
 Q k ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ α 1 , 2 ​ α 2 , 2 ​ n + 1 ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(2\alpha_{1},2\alpha_{2},2n+1) 
 = 2 ​ Q k ℍ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) + Γ ⁡ ( k + α 1 + 1 / 2 ) Γ ⁡ ( k + α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n + 1 ) ​ f k ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle=2Q^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}\left(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n\right)+\dfrac{\Gamma\left(k+\alpha_{1}+1/2\right)}{\Gamma\left(k+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n+1\right)}f_{k}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)\  
 
 
 where 
 

 
 
 { f k ​ ( n ) = c n ​ P n ( 1 / 4 ) ​ ( − i ⁡ ( k + 1 / 4 ) , π / 2 ) f k ​ ( m , n ) = c n , m ​ P 2 ​ n ( m − n + 1 / 2 ) ​ ( − i ​ k , π / 2 ) f k ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) = c n , α 1 , α 2 ​ p 2 ​ n ​ ( − i ​ k , α 1 + 1 2 , − α 1 − α 2 − 2 ​ n , α 1 + 1 2 , − α 1 − α 2 − 2 ​ n ) . \begin{cases}f_{k}(n)=c_{n}P^{(1/4)}_{n}(-i(k+1/4);\pi/2)\\
\\
f_{k}(m,n)=c_{n,m}P^{(m-n+1/2)}_{2n}(-ik;\pi/2)\\
\\
