f_{k}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n)=c_{n,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}p_{2n}(-ik;\alpha_{1}+\frac{1}{2},-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-2n,\alpha_{1}+\frac{1}{2},-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-2n).\end{cases} 
 
 
 In each case, the  f k f_{k}  is a hypergeometric orthogonal polynomial from the Askey scheme: The  P n ( λ ) ​ ( x , ϕ ) P^{(\lambda)}_{n}(x,\phi)  are the Meixner-Pollaczek polynomials, while  p n ​ ( x , a , b , c , d ) p_{n}(x;a,b,c,d)  are the continuous Hahn polynomials, see ( 3.14 ). The
normalization constants are 
 

 
 
 { c n = i n ​ n ! Γ ⁡ ( n + 1 / 2 ) c n , m = ( − 1 ) n ​ n ! Γ ⁡ ( m + 1 / 2 ) c n , α 1 , α 2 = ( − 1 ) n ​ n ! ​ Γ ​ ( α 1 + 1 / 2 ) ​ Γ ​ ( n + α 1 + α 2 + 1 ) Γ ⁡ ( n + α 1 + 1 / 2 ) ​ Γ ​ ( n + α 2 + 1 / 2 ) . \begin{cases}c_{n}=\dfrac{i^{n}n!}{\Gamma\left(n+1/2\right)}\\
c_{n,m}=\dfrac{(-1)^{n}n!}{\Gamma\left(m+1/2\right)}\\
c_{n,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}=\dfrac{(-1)^{n}n!\Gamma\left(\alpha_{1}+1/2\right)\Gamma\left(n+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(n+\alpha_{1}+1/2\right)\Gamma\left(n+\alpha_{2}+1/2\right)}.\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Remark . 
 
 The results ( 8.33 )-( 8.35 ) combined with Theorem  8.7  imply a polynomial property for the moments of the orthogonal ensemble, though not as cleanly as in the symplectic case. It is not possible to normalize  Q k ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ n + 1 ) Q^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}(2n+1)  and obtain a polynomial in  k k , unlike in the symplectic and unitary cases (e.g. the second term in ( 8.33 ) is always exponentially larger in  k k  than the first). 
 
 
 
 The content of Theorems  8.1  and  8.3  is that the combinations  S k ℝ ​ ( n ) S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(n)  and  S k ℝ ​ ( m , n ) S_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(m,n)  of moments of the orthogonal ensembles (with fixed  n n ) have hypergeometric orthogonal polynomial factors.
Putting together the dualities in the  n n -odd orthogonal ensembles of Theorem  8.9  and the classical duality between symplectic moments and formal orthogonal moments ( 8.1 )-( 8.2 )-( 8.3 ), we find that the combinations of moments (with fixed  k k ) 
 

 
 
 T k ℝ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle T_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(n) 
 = Q k ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ n + 1 ) + ( − 2 ) k ​ Q k ℝ ​ ( − 2 ​ n ) \displaystyle=Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(2n+1)+(-2)^{k}Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(-2n) 
 
 
 
 T k ℝ ​ ( m , n ) \displaystyle T_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(m,n) 
 = Q k ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ m + 1 , 2 ​ n + 1 ) + ( − 2 ) k ​ Q k ℝ ​ ( − 2 ​ m , − 2 ​ n ) \displaystyle=Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(2m+1,2n+1)+(-2)^{k}Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(-2m,-2n) 
 
 
 
 T k ℝ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle T_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = Q k ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ α 1 , 2 ​ α 2 , 2 ​ n + 1 ) + Q k ℝ ​ ( − 2 ​ α 1 , − 2 ​ α 2 , − 2 ​ n ) \displaystyle=Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(2\alpha_{1},2\alpha_{2},2n+1)+Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(-2\alpha_{1},-2\alpha_{2},-2n) 
 
 
 do have hypergeometric polynomial factors. 
 
 
 Corollary 8.10 . 
 
 

 
 (8.36) 
 
 T k ℝ ​ ( n ) \displaystyle T_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(n) 
 = ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ M n ​ ( k ,  1 / 2 , − 1 ) \displaystyle=(2k-1)!!\;M_{n}\left(k;\;1/2,-1\right) 
 
 
 (8.37) 
 
 T k ℝ ​ ( m , n ) \displaystyle T_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(m,n) 
 = 2 m + n + k π ​ Γ ⁡ ( k + α + 1 / 2 ) ( 2 ​ n − 1 ) !! ​ ( 2 ​ m − 1 ) !! ​ S n ​ ( ( i ​ k ) 2 ,  1 / 2 , 0 , α + 1 / 2 ) \displaystyle=\frac{2^{m+n+k}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\Gamma(k+\alpha+1/2)}{(2n-1)!!(2m-1)!!}\;S_{n}((ik)^{2};\;1/2,0,\alpha+1/2) 
 
 
 

 
 
 T k ℝ ​ ( α 1 , α 2 , n ) \displaystyle T_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},n) 
 = Γ ⁡ ( k + α 1 + 1 / 2 ) ​ Γ ​ ( α 1 + 1 / 2 ) ​ Γ ​ ( n + α 1 + α 2 + 1 ) Γ ⁡ ( k + α 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n + 1 ) ​ Γ ​ ( n + α 1 + 1 / 2 ) ​ Γ ​ ( n + α 2 + 1 / 2 ) \displaystyle=\dfrac{\Gamma\left(k+\alpha_{1}+1/2\right)\Gamma\left(\alpha_{1}+1/2\right)\Gamma\left(n+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(k+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+2n+1\right)\Gamma\left(n+\alpha_{1}+1/2\right)\Gamma\left(n+\alpha_{2}+1/2\right)} 
 
 
 (8.38) 
 
 
 ( 2 ​ ( n + α 2 ) − 1 ) !! ( 2 ​ α 2 − 1 ) !! ​ ( 2 ​ n − 1 ) !! ​ ( − 1 ) n ​ W n ​ ( ( i ​ k ) 2 , 1 2 , 0 , α 1 + 1 2 , − α 1 − α 2 − 2 ​ n ) . \displaystyle\frac{(2(n+\alpha_{2})-1)!!}{(2\alpha_{2}-1)!!(2n-1)!!}(-1)^{n}W_{n}\left((ik)^{2};\;\frac{1}{2},0,\alpha_{1}+\frac{1}{2},-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-2n\right). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof of Theorem  8.9 . 
 
 We multiply both sides of identity ( 8.31 ) by  x k x^{k}  (or  | x | 2 ​ k |x|^{2k}  for the GOE) and integrate over  I I . By the correspondence ( 3.4 ) this gives 
 

 
 (8.39) 
 
 Q k ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ n + 1 ) = 2 ​ Q ~ k ℍ ​ ( n ) + ψ n ​ ( k ) Q^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}(2n+1)=2\tilde{Q}^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}(n)+\psi_{n}(k) 
 
 
 where  Q ~ k ℍ ​ ( n ) \tilde{Q}^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}(n)  are moments defined with respect to the modified weights ( 8.32 ). Such moments are easily expressed in terms of the usual  Q k ℍ ​ ( n ) Q^{\mathbb{H}}_{k}(n)  by multiplying by  2 k 2^{k}  (Hermite and Laguerre case) or by dividing the parameters  α 1 \alpha_{1}  and  α 2 \alpha_{2}  by  2 2  (Laguerre and Jacobi cases). This gives the first terms in ( 8.33 )-( 8.35 ). 
 
 
 The correction  ψ n ​ ( k ) \psi_{n}(k)  is a weighted Mellin transform of the corresponding orthogonal polynomial. In the JOE and LOE this takes the form 
 

 
 (8.40) 
 
 ψ n ​ ( k ) = ∫ I x k ​ w 1 ​ ( x ) ​ p 2 ​ n ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x ∫ I w 1 ​ ( x ) ​ p 2 ​ n ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x . \psi_{n}(k)=\frac{\int_{I}x^{k}w_{1}(x)p_{2n}(x)\,dx}{\int_{I}w_{1}(x)p_{2n}(x)\,dx}. 
 
 
 while for the GOE  x k x^{k}  is replaced with  | x | 2 ​ k |x|^{2k} . The integral ( 8.40 ) can be computed explicitly by expanding  p 2 ​ n ​ ( x ) p_{2n}(x)  as a sum and integrating term by term. This expansion turns out to be a terminating hypergeometric series which can be identified as one of the hypergeometric polynomials appearing in the claimed result. In fact, for the Gaussian and Laguerre cases, precisely this calculation is carried out in a different context in  [ 23 ] , so let us just explain the Jacobi case. Then the monic polynomials  p 2 ​ n ​ ( x ) p_{2n}(x)  are proportional to the usual Jacobi polynomials which can be written down explicitly (see  e.g.   [ 73 , Eq. 4.32] ). Integrating term by term in ( 8.40 ) gives 
 

 
 
 ψ n ​ ( k ) = n ! ​ Γ ​ ( α 1 + α 2 2 + 1 + n ) Γ ⁡ ( α 1 + 1 2 + n ) ​ Γ ​ ( α 2 + 1 2 + n ) ​ Γ ​ ( α 1 + α 2 2 + 2 ​ n + k + 1 ) × ∑ i = 0 2 ​ n ( − 1 ) i ​ ( 2 ​ n + α 2 2 ​ n − i ) ​ ( 2 ​ n + α 1 i ) ​ Γ ​ ( α 2 + 1 2 + i + k ) ​ Γ ​ ( α 1 + 1 2 + 2 ​ n − i ) . \begin{split}&\psi_{n}(k)=\frac{n!\Gamma(\frac{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}{2}+1+n)}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha_{1}+1}{2}+n)\Gamma(\frac{\alpha_{2}+1}{2}+n)\Gamma(\frac{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}{2}+2n+k+1)}\\
&\times\sum_{i=0}^{2n}(-1)^{i}\binom{2n+\alpha_{2}}{2n-i}\binom{2n+\alpha_{1}}{i}\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}+1}{2}+i+k\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha_{1}+1}{2}+2n-i\right).\end{split} 
 
 
 The sum can be matched with a hypergeometric function and we obtain 
 

 
 
 ψ n ​ ( k ) = η n , α 1 , α 2 ​ Γ ​ ( α 2 + 1 2 + k ) Γ ⁡ ( 1 + α 1 + α 2 2 + k + 2 ​ n ) ​ F 2 3 ​ ( k + ( α 2 + 1 ) / 2 , − 2 ​ n , − α 1 − 2 ​ n α 2 + 1 , − ( α 1 − 1 ) / 2 − 2 n , 1 ) \psi_{n}(k)=\frac{\eta_{n,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}+1}{2}+k\right)}{\Gamma\left(1+\frac{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}{2}+k+2n\right)}\,{}_{3}F_{2}\left(\begin{matrix}k+(\alpha_{2}+1)/2,-2n,-\alpha_{1}-2n\\
\alpha_{2}+1,-(\alpha_{1}-1)/2-2n\end{matrix};1\right) 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 
 η n , α 1 , α 2 = n ! ​ Γ ​ ( 1 + α 1 + α 2 2 + n ) ​ Γ ​ ( 1 + α 2 + 2 ​ n ) ​ Γ ​ ( 1 + α 1 2 + 2 ​ n ) ( 2 ​ n ) ! ​ Γ ​ ( 1 + α 2 ) ​ Γ ​ ( 1 + α 1 2 + n ) ​ Γ ​ ( 1 + α 2 2 + n ) . \eta_{n,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}=\frac{n!\Gamma(1+\frac{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}{2}+n)\Gamma(1+\alpha_{2}+2n)\Gamma(\frac{1+\alpha_{1}}{2}+2n)}{(2n)!\Gamma(1+\alpha_{2})\Gamma(\frac{1+\alpha_{1}}{2}+n)\Gamma(\frac{1+\alpha_{2}}{2}+n)}. 
 
 
 Finally, comparing ( 8.3 ) with the definition of the continuous Hahn polynomial in ( 3.14 ) gives the result.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark . 
 
 In the Gaussian and Laguerre cases, the evaluation of the integral ( 8.40 ) already appeared in the literature on special functions, see the work of Bump et al.  [ 21 ] , Coffey et al. [ 23 ,  24 ] , though no connection to random matrix theory is made. These works show that the quantity ( 8.40 ) satisfies a functional equation and Riemann hypothesis with critical line  Re ( k ) = − 1 / 2 \mathrm{Re}(k)=-1/2  (Hermite polynomials) and  Re ⁡ ( k ) = 0 \mathrm{Re}(k)=0  (Laguerre polynomials) in our notation. We believe it is new that precisely these Mellin transforms should appear in the context of random matrices. The last and most complicated case of Jacobi appears to be absent from the literature. This turns out to be a continuous Hahn polynomial  p 2 ​ n ​ ( − i ​ k , a , b , c , d ) p_{2n}(-ik;a,b,c,d)  with  a = c > 0 a=c>0  and  b = d < 0 b=d<0 . The analogous properties in this case are most easily proved by noticing that the continuous Hahn polynomial can be represented in terms of the Wilson polynomial with a negative fourth parameter. Explicitly, one has 
 

 
 
 p 2 ​ n ​ ( − i ​ k , α 1 + 1 / 2 , − α 1 − α 2 − 2 ​ n , α 1 + 1 / 2 , − α 1 − α 2 − 2 ​ n ) = ( 2 α 2 + 1 ) ( 2 α 2 + 3 ) ⋯ ( 2 α 2 + 2 n − 1 ) ( 2 ​ n − 1 ) !! ​ n ! ​ W n ​ ( ( i ​ k ) 2 , 1 2 , 0 , α 1 + 1 2 , − α 1 − α 2 − 2 ​ n ) p_{2n}(-ik;\alpha_{1}+1/2,-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-2n,\alpha_{1}+1/2,-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-2n)=\\
\frac{(2\alpha_{2}+1)(2\alpha_{2}+3)\cdots(2\alpha_{2}+2n-1)}{(2n-1)!!\;n!}W_{n}\left((ik)^{2};\;\frac{1}{2},0,\alpha_{1}+\frac{1}{2},-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}-2n\right) 
 
 
 This identity demonstrates that the Mellin transforms satisfy a symmetry on the line  Re ⁡ ( k ) = 0 \mathrm{Re}(k)=0  (the polynomials are invariant under  k → − k k\to-k ). Furthermore, by the orthogonality property ( C.2 ), we can deduce that the zeros all lie on the imaginary axis (this does not seem to be obvious from the Hahn polynomial representation). 
 
 
 
 We now study the orthogonal ensemble with  n n  even. In this case the analytic structure of the Mellin transform seems to be more complicated and remains somewhat mysterious to us. For this reason we restrict ourselves to the Gaussian case, though analogous results for Laguerre and Jacobi could easily be derived. We are able to prove an analytic continuation of  Q k ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ n ) Q^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}(2n)  to an entire function of  k k  as in the previous sections, but with a more complicated structure. We first consider the simplest case  n = 2 n=2  where this structure already appears. Directly integrating  | x | 2 ​ k |x|^{2k}  against the density ( 3.1 ) with  β = 1 \beta=1  and a Gaussian weight gives 
 

 
 (8.41) 
 
 Q k ℝ ( 2 ) = 2 k ​ Γ ​ ( k + 1 / 2 ) π + 1 2 ∫ 0 ∞ x 2 ​ k x erf ( x / 2 ) e − x 2 / 4 d x . Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(2)=\frac{2^{k}\Gamma(k+1/2)}{\sqrt{\pi}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{2k}x\,\mathrm{erf}(x/2)e^{-x^{2}/4}\,dx. 
 
 
 Clearly, the first term above has a similar structure to that already observed in the GSE and GUE. But the second term, which is a weighted Mellin transform of the error function, is different. It is analytic in the half-plane  Re ⁡ ( 2 ​ k ) > − 1 \mathrm{Re}(2k)>-1  and standard properties of Mellin transforms show that it extends as to an analytic function in the entire complex plane except for simple poles when  2 ​ k + 1 ∈ { − 2 , − 4 , − 6 , … } 2k+1\in\{-2,-4,-6,\ldots\} . Since these simple poles are eliminated on dividing by  Γ ⁡ ( k + 1 / 2 ) \Gamma(k+1/2) , this gives an entire function of  k k . In fact this analytic continuation can be given in terms of a hypergeometric function: 
 

 
 
 Q k ℝ ​ ( 2 ) \displaystyle Q_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}(2) 
 = 2 2 ​ k + 3 / 2 ​ F 1 2 ​ ( 1 / 2 , k + 3 / 2 3 / 2 , − 1 ) ​ Γ ​ ( k + 3 / 2 ) / π + 2 k ​ Γ ​ ( k + 1 / 2 ) π \displaystyle=2^{2k+3/2}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}1/2,k+3/2\\
3/2\end{matrix};-1\right)\Gamma(k+3/2)/\sqrt{\pi}+\frac{2^{k}\Gamma(k+1/2)}{\sqrt{\pi}} 
 
 
 
 
 = ( 2 ​ k − 1 ) !! ​ ( ( 2 ​ k + 1 ) ​ M k ​ ( − 1 ,  3 / 2 , 1 / 2 ) + 1 ) . \displaystyle=(2k-1)!!\;\left((2k+1)M_{k}(-1;\;3/2,1/2)+1\right). 
 
 
 This hypergeometric function reduces to a polynomial whenever  k k  is a positive integer. But its analytic continuation to  k ∈ ℂ k\in\mathbb{C}  appears more complicated than in the previously considered cases. Indeed one has the asymptotics (see  e.g.   [ 72 ] ): 
 

 
 
 2 F 1 ( 1 / 2 , k + 3 / 2 3 / 2 ; − 1 ) ∼ 1 2 π k , k → + ∞ , 2 F 1 ( 1 / 2 , k + 3 / 2 3 / 2 ; − 1 ) ∼ − k 2 − 3 / 2 − k , k → − ∞ . \begin{split}&{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}1/2,k+3/2\\
3/2\end{matrix};-1\right)\sim\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{k}},\qquad k\to+\infty,\\
&{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}1/2,k+3/2\\
3/2\end{matrix};-1\right)\sim-k2^{-3/2-k},\qquad k\to-\infty.\end{split} 
 
 
 
 
 For say  n = 4 , 6 , 8 , … n=4,6,8,\ldots  and so on, this structure persists and follows a similar pattern. As for the GSE, the results of  [ 62 ]  are again useful here, providing a general formula for the GOE moments: 
 

 
 (8.42) 
 
 Q k ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ n ) = Q k ℂ ​ ( 2 ​ n − 1 ) − ∑ j = 0 n − 1 ∑ i = 0 n − j − 1 ( k i ) ​ ( k i + j ) ​ ( n − i − j ) ( k + 1 / 2 ) ( n − j ) ( 1 / 2 ) + A k ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ n ) . Q^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}(2n)=Q^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(2n-1)-\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\sum_{i=0}^{n-j-1}{k\choose i}{k\choose i+j}\frac{(n-i-j)_{(k+1/2)}}{(n-j)_{(1/2)}}+A^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}(2n). 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 
 A k ℝ ( 2 n ) := c n ∫ 0 ∞ x 2 ​ k e − x 2 / 2 H 2 ​ n − 1 ( x ) erf ( x / 2 ) d x . A^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}(2n):=c_{n}\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{2k}e^{-x^{2}/2}H_{2n-1}(x)\mathrm{erf}(x/\sqrt{2})\,dx. 
 
 
 The first two terms in ( 8.42 ) have a simple analytic structure, similar to that found in the symplectic case. The term  A k ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ n ) A^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}(2n)  is the generalization to larger  n n  of the second term in ( 8.41 ). 
 
 
 Proposition 8.11 . 
 
 For any positive integer  n n , the ratio  Q k ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ n ) / Γ ⁡ ( k + 1 / 2 ) Q^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}(2n)/\Gamma(k+1/2)  has an analytic continuation to an entire function of  k k . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 It is clear that the first two terms in ( 8.42 ) yield a polynomial in  k k  after dividing by  Γ ⁡ ( k + 1 / 2 ) \Gamma(k+1/2) . The third term ( 8.3 ) is the Mellin transform of the function  ϕ n ( x ) = e − x 2 / 2 H 2 ​ n − 1 ( x ) erf ( x / 2 ) \phi_{n}(x)=e^{-x^{2}/2}H_{2n-1}(x)\mathrm{erf}(x/\sqrt{2})  which is analytic in the right-half plane  Re ⁡ ( 2 ​ k ) > − 3 \mathrm{Re}(2k)>-3 . To extend to the left-half plane  Re ⁡ ( 2 ​ k ) ≤ − 3 \mathrm{Re}(2k)\leq-3  it suffices to notice that  ϕ n ​ ( x ) \phi_{n}(x)  has an asymptotic expansion near  x = 0 x=0  with respect to the sequence  { x 2 ​ k } k ≥ 1 \{x^{2k}\}_{k\geq 1} .
Therefore  A 2 ​ k ℝ ​ ( 2 ​ n ) A^{\mathbb{R}}_{2k}(2n)  has an meromorphic continuation into the left-half plane except for simple poles when  2 ​ k + 1 = − 2 , − 4 , − 6 , … 2k+1=-2,-4,-6,\ldots . Precisely these poles are eliminated after dividing through by the factor  Γ ⁡ ( k + 1 / 2 ) \Gamma(k+1/2) .
∎

 Appendix A  Orthogonal and symplectic ensembles: duality 

 
 The purpose of this section is to prove Proposition  8.8  in the three classical ensembles. The proof is based on explicit results for the eigenvalue density of orthogonal and symplectic ensembles obtained by Adler et al.  [ 3 ] . To begin with, we introduce the notation and relevant results obtained in  [ 3 ] . There, the notation  e − 2 ​ V ​ ( x ) e^{-2V(x)}  is equivalent to our  w 2 ​ ( x ) w_{2}(x)  as in ( 3.2 ). 
 
 
 We begin by denoting by  p n ​ ( x ) p_{n}(x)  the unique degree  n n  monic polynomial orthogonal with respect to  w 2 ​ ( x ) w_{2}(x)  and we set 
 

 
 (A.1)

 Note that the expression in the second line of ( A.5 ) corrects a typo in  [ 3 , Eq. 4.18] . The formula for  ρ 2 ​ n ( 1 ) ​ ( x ) \rho^{(1)}_{2n}(x)  in ( A.5 ) is given in  [ 3 , Eq. 4.12]  as

 But by definition

 Now we must demonstrate the relation ( 8.31 ). 
 
 
 Proof of Proposition  8.8 . 
 
 The point of the proof is that ( A.5 ) can be simplified considerably. There are two calculations required in the proof, which in the GOE case were carried out in  [ 3 , Eqs 4.19 and 4.13]  respectively. The first claim is that the following identity holds in all three cases: 
 

 
 (A.16) 
 
 γ 2 ​ n − 2 ​ s ~ 2 ​ n − 2 s ~ 2 ​ n = γ 2 ​ n − 1 \gamma_{2n-2}\frac{\tilde{s}_{2n-2}}{\tilde{s}_{2n}}=\gamma_{2n-1} 
 
 
 This can be verified by direct computation using the above explicit formulae for  s ~ 2 ​ n \tilde{s}_{2n}  and  γ n \gamma_{n} . Then formula ( A.5 ) becomes 
 

 
 (A.17) 
 
 ρ 2 ​ n + 1 ( 1 ) ​ ( x ) = ρ 2 ​ n − 1 ( 2 ) ​ ( x ) + γ 2 ​ n − 2 ​ e − V 1 ​ ( x ) ​ p 2 ​ n − 1 ​ ( x ) ​ ϕ ~ 2 ​ n − 2 ​ ( x ) − γ 2 ​ n − 1 ​ e − V 1 ​ ( x ) ​ ( ϕ ~ 2 ​ n ​ ( x ) ​ p 2 ​ n − 1 ​ ( x ) − p 2 ​ n ​ ( x ) ​ ϕ ~ 2 ​ n − 1 ​ ( x ) ) + e − V 1 ​ ( x ) ​ p 2 ​ n ​ ( x ) 2 ​ s ~ 2 ​ n , \begin{split}\rho^{(1)}_{2n+1}(x)&=\rho^{(2)}_{2n-1}(x)+\gamma_{2n-2}e^{-V_{1}(x)}p_{2n-1}(x)\tilde{\phi}_{2n-2}(x)\\
&-\gamma_{2n-1}e^{-V_{1}(x)}\left(\tilde{\phi}_{2n}(x)p_{2n-1}(x)-p_{2n}(x)\tilde{\phi}_{2n-1}(x)\right)+\frac{e^{-V_{1}(x)}p_{2n}(x)}{2\tilde{s}_{2n}},\end{split} 
 
 
 The second claim is the following identity 
 

 
 (A.18) 
 
 e − V 1 ​ ( y ) ​ p 2 ​ n − 1 ​ ( y ) ​ ( γ 2 ​ n − 2 ​ ϕ ~ 2 ​ n − 2 ​ ( x ) − γ 2 ​ n − 1 ​ ϕ ~ 2 ​ n ​ ( x ) ) = e − V 1 ​ ( y ) − V 1 ​ ( x ) ​ f ​ ( x ) ​ p 2 ​ n − 1 ​ ( x ) ​ p 2 ​ n − 1 ​ ( y ) h 2 ​ n − 1 e^{-V_{1}(y)}p_{2n-1}(y)\left(\gamma_{2n-2}\tilde{\phi}_{2n-2}(x)-\gamma_{2n-1}\tilde{\phi}_{2n}(x)\right)=e^{-V_{1}(y)-V_{1}(x)}f(x)\frac{p_{2n-1}(x)p_{2n-1}(y)}{h_{2n-1}} 
 
 
 where  f ⁡ ( x ) f(x)  is given by ( A.3 ). Setting  x = y x=y  and inserting it into ( A.17 ) gives the simplification 
 

 
 (A.19) 
 
 ρ 2 ​ n + 1 ( 1 ) ​ ( x ) = ρ 2 ​ n ( 2 ) ​ ( x ) + γ 2 ​ n − 1 ​ e − V 1 ​ ( x ) ​ p 2 ​ n ​ ( x ) ​ ϕ ~ 2 ​ n − 1 ​ ( x ) + e − V 1 ​ ( x ) ​ p 2 ​ n ​ ( x ) 2 ​ s ~ 2 ​ n , \rho^{(1)}_{2n+1}(x)=\rho^{(2)}_{2n}(x)+\gamma_{2n-1}e^{-V_{1}(x)}p_{2n}(x)\tilde{\phi}_{2n-1}(x)+\frac{e^{-V_{1}(x)}p_{2n}(x)}{2\tilde{s}_{2n}}, 
 
 
 where we used the explicit form of the  β = 2 \beta=2  density ( A.10 ) and that 
 

 
 (A.20) 
 
 e − 2 ​ V 1 ​ ( x ) + log ⁡ f ⁡ ( x ) = e − 2 ​ V ​ ( x ) = w 2 ​ ( x ) . e^{-2V_{1}(x)+\log f(x)}=e^{-2V(x)}=w_{2}(x). 
 
 
 This now looks very similar to ( A.13 ). Indeed, the proof is complete if we can check that  ϕ ~ 2 ​ n − 1 ( x ) = − ∫ I w 1 ( y ) p 2 ​ n − 1 ( y ) 𝟙 y > x d y \tilde{\phi}_{2n-1}(x)=-\int_{I}w_{1}(y)p_{2n-1}(y)\mathbbm{1}_{y>x}\,dy . Comparing with the definition ( A.7 ) we see that 
 

 
 (A.21) 
 
 ϕ ~ 2 ​ n − 1 ( x ) = − ∫ I w 1 ( y ) p 2 ​ n − 1 ( y ) 𝟙 y > x d y + 1 2 ∫ I w 1 ( y ) p 2 ​ n − 1 ( y ) d y , \tilde{\phi}_{2n-1}(x)=-\int_{I}w_{1}(y)p_{2n-1}(y)\mathbbm{1}_{y>x}\,dy+\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}w_{1}(y)p_{2n-1}(y)\,dy, 
 
 
 but the second integral is  s ~ 2 ​ n − 1 \tilde{s}_{2n-1}  which is zero by Lemma  A.11 . This immediately implies 
 

 
 (A.22) 
 
 ρ 2 ​ n + 1 ( 1 ) ​ ( x ) = 2 ​ ρ ~ n ( 4 ) ​ ( x ) + e − V 1 ​ ( x ) ​ p 2 ​ n ​ ( x ) 2 ​ s ~ 2 ​ n \rho^{(1)}_{2n+1}(x)=2\tilde{\rho}^{(4)}_{n}(x)+\frac{e^{-V_{1}(x)}p_{2n}(x)}{2\tilde{s}_{2n}} 
 
 
 as given in the statement of Proposition  8.8 . It remains to check identity ( A.18 ). Cancelling  e − V 1 ​ ( y ) ​ p 2 ​ n − 1 ​ ( y ) e^{-V_{1}(y)}p_{2n-1}(y) , it is equivalent to checking 
 

 
 (A.23) 
 
 γ 2 ​ n − 2 ​ ϕ ~ 2 ​ n − 2 ​ ( x ) − γ 2 ​ n − 1 ​ ϕ ~ 2 ​ n ​ ( x ) = f ⁡ ( x ) ​ e − V 1 ​ ( x ) ​ p 2 ​ n − 1 ​ ( x ) h 2 ​ n − 1 . \gamma_{2n-2}\tilde{\phi}_{2n-2}(x)-\gamma_{2n-1}\tilde{\phi}_{2n}(x)=f(x)e^{-V_{1}(x)}\frac{p_{2n-1}(x)}{h_{2n-1}}. 
 
 
 Differentiating both sides of ( A.23 ) with respect to  x x  reduces the claim to 
 

 
 (A.24) 
 
 h 2 ​ n − 1 ​ γ 2 ​ n − 2 ​ p 2 ​ n − 2 ​ ( x ) − h 2 ​ n − 1 ​ γ 2 ​ n − 1 ​ p 2 ​ n ​ ( x ) = 1 w 1 ​ ( x ) ​ d d ​ x ​ ( f ⁡ ( x ) ​ w 1 ​ ( x ) ​ p 2 ​ n − 1 ​ ( x ) ) h_{2n-1}\gamma_{2n-2}p_{2n-2}(x)-h_{2n-1}\gamma_{2n-1}p_{2n}(x)=\frac{1}{w_{1}(x)}\frac{d}{dx}\left(f(x)w_{1}(x)p_{2n-1}(x)\right) 
 
 
 Now using standard differential identities for the classical orthogonal polynomials and some routine calculation shows that ( A.24 ) is a consequence of the three term recurrence relation.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix B  Mellin transform 

 
 We summarise here some properties of the Mellin transform (and its extension).
The Mellin transform of  f ⁡ ( x ) f(x)  is defined by the integral 
 

 
 (B.1) 
 
 ℳ ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( x ) ; s ] = ∫ 0 ∞ f ⁡ ( x ) ​ x s − 1 ​ 𝑑 x , \mathcal{M}\left[f(x);s\right]=\int_{0}^{\infty}f(x)x^{s-1}dx, 
 
 
 when it exists. We set  f ∗ ​ ( s ) = ℳ ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( x ) ; s ] f^{*}(s)=\mathcal{M}\left[f(x);s\right] . 
 
 
 In general, the integral ( B.1 ) converges and defines a holomorphic function  f ∗ ​ ( s ) f^{*}(s)  only in a vertical strip  D D  of the complex plane. It turns out that, in the frequently occurring case where  f ⁡ ( x ) f(x)  is of rapid decay at infinity and has an asymptotic expansion  f ⁡ ( x ) ∼ ∑ j = 0 ∞ a j ​ x b j f(x)\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a_{j}x^{b_{j}}  as  x → 0 + x\to 0^{+}  (as in all instances in this paper), the Mellin transform  f ∗ ​ ( s ) f^{*}(s)  has a meromorphic continuation to the whole complex plane with simple poles of residue  a j a_{j}  at  s = − b j s=-b_{j} . For more details on meromorphic extensions of Mellin transforms see  [ 76 ] . 
 
 
 If the integral ( B.1 ) converges in the strip  D D , then the following relations hold: 
 

 
 (B.2) 
 
 ℳ ​ [ f ( m ) ​ ( x ) ; s ] \displaystyle\mathcal{M}\left[f^{(m)}(x);s\right] 
 = ( − 1 ) m ​ ( s − m ) m ​ f ∗ ​ ( s − m ) \displaystyle=(-1)^{m}(s-m)_{m}f^{*}(s-m) 
 s − m \displaystyle s-m 
 ∈ D \displaystyle\in D 
 
 
 (B.3) 
 
 ℳ ⁡ [ x m ​ f ​ ( x ) ; s ] \displaystyle\mathcal{M}\left[x^{m}f(x);s\right] 
 = f ∗ ​ ( s + m ) \displaystyle=f^{*}(s+m) 
 s + m \displaystyle s+m 
 ∈ D \displaystyle\in D 
 
 
 (B.4) 
 
 ℳ ⁡ [ x m ​ f ( m ) ​ ( x ) ; s ] \displaystyle\mathcal{M}\left[x^{m}f^{(m)}(x);s\right] 
 = ( − 1 ) m ​ ( s ) m ​ f ∗ ​ ( s ) \displaystyle=(-1)^{m}(s)_{m}f^{*}(s) 
 s \displaystyle s 
 ∈ D . \displaystyle\in D. 
 
 
 
 
 Suppose that  f ⁡ ( s ) f(s)  and  g ⁡ ( s ) g(s)  have Mellin transforms  f ∗ ​ ( s ) f^{*}(s)  and  g ∗ ​ ( s ) g^{*}(s) , respectively, analytic in a vertical strip  D D  in the complex plane. Take any  c ∈ D c\in D . Then 
 

 
 (B.5) 
 
 ℳ ⁡ [ f ⁡ ( x ) ​ g ​ ( x ) ; s ] = 1 2 ​ π ​ i ​ ∫ c − i ​ ∞ c + i ​ ∞ f ∗ ​ ( s − u ) ​ g ∗ ​ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u . \mathcal{M}\left[f(x)g(x);s\right]=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}f^{*}(s-u)g^{*}(u)du. 
 
 
 whenever the Mellin transfom of  ( f ​ g ) ​ ( x ) (fg)(x)  exists. 
 
 
 
 Appendix C  Hypergeometric orthogonal polynomials 

 
 We report a few basic properties of some families of hypergeometric OP’s. 
 
 
 C.1.  Wilson 

 
 The Wilson polynomials
are solutions of the discrete Sturm-Liouville problem  [ 48 , Section 9.1] 
 

 
 (C.1) 
 
 B ⁡ ( x ) ​ y ​ ( x + i ) − [ B ⁡ ( x ) + D ⁡ ( x ) ] ​ y ​ ( x ) + D ⁡ ( x ) ​ y ​ ( x − i ) = n ⁡ ( n + a + b + c + d − 1 ) ​ y ​ ( x ) , B(x)y(x+i)-\left[B(x)+D(x)\right]y(x)+D(x)y(x-i)=n(n+a+b+c+d-1)y(x), 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 
 y ⁡ ( x ) = W n ​ ( x 2 , a , b , c , d ) y(x)=W_{n}(x^{2};a,b,c,d) 
 
 
 and 
 

 
 
 { B ⁡ ( x ) = ( a − i ​ x ) ​ ( b − i ​ x ) ​ ( c − i ​ x ) ​ ( d − i ​ x ) 2 ​ i ​ x ​ ( 2 ​ i ​ x − 1 ) D ⁡ ( x ) = ( a + i ​ x ) ​ ( b + i ​ x ) ​ ( c + i ​ x ) ​ ( d + i ​ x ) 2 ​ i ​ x ​ ( 2 ​ i ​ x + 1 ) . \left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad}cr}\displaystyle B(x)=\frac{(a-ix)(b-ix)(c-ix)(d-ix)}{2ix(2ix-1)}\\
\\
\displaystyle D(x)=\frac{(a+ix)(b+ix)(c+ix)(d+ix)}{2ix(2ix+1)}.\end{array}\right. 
 
 
 In this paper we have considered the less conventional situation when  a , b , c , 1 − d > 0 a,b,c,1-d>0 . For this range of the parameters, Neretin  [ 67 , Section 3.3]  found the orthogonality relation 
 

 
 
 
 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ ℝ + | Γ ⁡ ( a + i ​ x ) ​ Γ ​ ( b + i ​ x ) ​ Γ ​ ( c + i ​ x ) Γ ⁡ ( 1 − d + i ​ x ) ​ Γ ​ ( 2 ​ i ​ x ) | 2 ​ W m ​ ( x 2 , a , b , c , d ) ​ W n ​ ( x 2 , a , b , c , d ) ​ d ⁡ x \displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\left|\frac{\Gamma(a+ix)\Gamma(b+ix)\Gamma(c+ix)}{\Gamma(1-d+ix)\Gamma(2ix)}\right|^{2}W_{m}(x^{2};\;a,b,c,d)W_{n}(x^{2};\;a,b,c,d)\operatorname{d}x 
 
 
 
 
 = a + b + c + d − 1 a + b + c + d + 2 ​ n − 1 ​ ( a + b ) n ​ ( a + c ) n ​ ( a + d ) n ​ ( b + c ) n ​ ( b + d ) n ​ ( c + d ) n ( a + b + c + d − 1 ) n \displaystyle=\frac{a+b+c+d-1}{a+b+c+d+2n-1}\frac{(a+b)_{n}(a+c)_{n}(a+d)_{n}(b+c)_{n}(b+d)_{n}(c+d)_{n}}{(a+b+c+d-1)_{n}} 
 
 
 (C.2) 
 
 
 × Γ ⁡ ( a + b ) ​ Γ ​ ( a + c ) ​ Γ ​ ( b + c ) ​ Γ ​ ( 1 − a − b − c − d ) Γ ⁡ ( 1 − a − d ) ​ Γ ​ ( 1 − b − d ) ​ Γ ​ ( 1 − c − d ) ​ n ! ​ δ m ​ n , \displaystyle\small\times\frac{\Gamma(a+b)\Gamma(a+c)\Gamma(b+c)\Gamma(1-a-b-c-d)}{\Gamma(1-a-d)\Gamma(1-b-d)\Gamma(1-c-d)}n!\;\delta_{mn}, 
 
 
 for  n , m < 1 − a − b − c − d n,m<1-a-b-c-d . 
 
 
 
 C.2.  Continuous dual Hahn 

 
 The continuous dual Hahn polynomials  S n ​ ( x 2 , a , b , c ) S_{n}(x^{2};a,b,c)  can be found from the Wilson polynomials by dividing by  ( a + d ) n (a+d)_{n}  and letting  d → ∞ d\to\infty .
If  a a ,  b b  and  c c  are positive, then  [ 48 , Section 9.3] 
 

 
 
 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ ℝ + | Γ ⁡ ( a + i ​ x ) ​ Γ ​ ( b + i ​ x ) ​ Γ ​ ( c + i ​ x ) Γ ⁡ ( 2 ​ i ​ x ) | 2 ​ S m ​ ( x 2 , a , b , c ) ​ S n ​ ( x 2 , a , b , c ) ​ d ⁡ x \displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\left|\frac{\Gamma(a+ix)\Gamma(b+ix)\Gamma(c+ix)}{\Gamma(2ix)}\right|^{2}S_{m}(x^{2};\;a,b,c)S_{n}(x^{2};\;a,b,c)\operatorname{d}x 
 
 
 (C.3) 
 
 = Γ ⁡ ( n + a + b ) ​ Γ ​ ( n + a + c ) ​ Γ ​ ( n + b + c ) ​ n ! ​ δ m ​ n . \displaystyle=\Gamma(n+a+b)\Gamma(n+a+c)\Gamma(n+b+c)n!\;\delta_{mn}. 
 
 
 The continuous dual Hahn polynomials are solution of the discrete Sturm-Liouville problem 
 

 
 (C.4) 
 
 B ⁡ ( x ) ​ y ​ ( x + i ) − [ B ⁡ ( x ) + D ⁡ ( x ) ] ​ y ​ ( x ) + D ⁡ ( x ) ​ y ​ ( x − i ) = n ​ y ​ ( x ) , B(x)y(x+i)-\left[B(x)+D(x)\right]y(x)+D(x)y(x-i)=ny(x),

\displaystyle D(x)=\frac{(a+ix)(b+ix)(c+ix)}{2ix(2ix+1)}.\end{array}\right. 
 
 
 
 
 
 C.3.  Meixner-Pollaczek

satisfy the orthogonality relation  [ 48 , Section 9.7] 
 

 
 
 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ ℝ e ( 2 ​ ϕ − π ) ​ x ​ | Γ ⁡ ( λ + i ​ x ) | 2 ​ P m ( λ ) ​ ( x , ϕ ) ​ P n ( λ ) ​ ( x , ϕ ) ​ d ⁡ x \displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{(2\phi-\pi)x}|\Gamma(\lambda+ix)|^{2}P_{m}^{(\lambda)}(x;\phi)P_{n}^{(\lambda)}(x;\phi)\operatorname{d}x 
 
 
 (C.5) 
 
 = Γ ⁡ ( n + 2 ​ λ ) ( 2 ​ sin ⁡ ϕ ) 2 ​ λ ​ n ! δ m ​ n , λ > 0 and 0 < ϕ < π , \displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(n+2\lambda)}{(2\sin\phi)^{2\lambda}n!}\;\delta_{mn},\quad\lambda>0\quad\text{and}\quad 0<\phi<\pi, 
 
 
 and the Sturm-Liouville equation (set  y ⁡ ( x ) = P n ( λ ) ​ ( x , ϕ ) y(x)=P_{n}^{(\lambda)}(x;\phi) ): 
 

 
 (C.6)

     Instructions for reporting errors 
     We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
      support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
      methods listed below: 
     
       Click the "Report Issue"  ( 
             
           )  button, located in the page header. 
     
     Tip:  You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report. 
     Our team has already identified  the following issues . We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
      may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
      should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
      all. 
     Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a  list of packages that need conversion , and welcome  developer contributions .
